# Content review checklist (for a community rabbi)

> English edition of docs/RABBI-REVIEW.md. Every item keeps the same ids (lesson, card, question, interview, calendar, prayer and map ids) as the Russian checklist, so a note on an item applies to both languages: the English texts are in content/en/, the Russian ones in content/ru/.

This document is compiled from the reports of the authors of the eight sections of the Shorashim app. Each author attached a “RABBI REVIEW” list — items about which he himself is unsure and which require a rabbi’s review: exact source references, disputed formulations, and communal customs.

**How to use it.** Go through the items in order. Mark what you have checked with `[x]`. If a formulation needs to be changed, write the correction in the item itself or as a separate list; corrections may be sent as ordinary text, and you do not need to edit the JSON yourself. The texts of the sections are in the files `content/en/sections/01-osnovy.json`, `02-ivrit.json`, `03-shabbat.json`, `04-kashrut.json`, `05-prazdniki.json`, `06-semya.json`, `07-etika.json`, `08-beit-din.json`. The levels “Orthodox / Conservative (Masorti) / Reform” mean that material presenting a stricter position is shown only to students of the corresponding level.

## 1. Foundations

- [ ] Recognition of giyurim: that the Rabbinate recognizes only a giyur conducted under its supervision; that the Law of Return recognizes non-Orthodox giyurim performed abroad; the 2021 decision of the Supreme Court of Israel on non-Orthodox giyurim within the country; the dependence of Conservative communities’ acceptance of a Reform giyur on brit milah and tevilah.
- [ ] Requirements of Reform giyur: the CCAR resolution of 1892 (it does not require milah and the mikveh) and present-day practice in Israel and the CIS — practice differs by community.
- [ ] Women and the reading of the Shema: a summary of the majority of opinions is given in the Orthodox block; customs differ.
- [ ] Talmudic references: Bechorot 30b (acceptance of all the mitzvot); Bava Metzia 59b, “36 times” (there is a variant count — 46); the tradition that Shemaya and Avtalyon were gerim, and that Rabbi Akiva was descended from gerim.
- [ ] “Ani Ma'amin”: the author of the prose text is unknown; “Yigdal” is attributed to Daniel ben Yehudah; the singing of “Ani Ma'amin” in the camps is given with the cautious formulation “according to testimonies.”
- [ ] Dates: ancient dates are given according to the scholarly chronology (586 BCE and others), with a general footnote that the traditional Jewish reckoning is different; also check the dates: JTS 1886/87, the 1950 ruling on driving to synagogue on Shabbat, Regina Jonas 1935, the founding of the World Union for Progressive Judaism (WUPJ) 1926.
- [ ] The name of the Russian progressive union: the text uses only the abbreviation “ROOPI” — confirm the full official name.

## 2. Hebrew and prayer

- [ ] Siman numbers of the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch: 1:2 (Modeh Ani), 9 (tzitzit), 10 (tefillin), 11 (mezuzah), 17 (Shema), 18 (Amidah) — written from memory; check against Kutukov’s translation.
- [ ] Pirkei Avot 2:13 (Rabbi Shimon on prayer): the numbering of the mishnah differs among editions (in some it is 2:18).
- [ ] “Baruch Shem”: the story of Yaakov (Pesachim 56a) and the rule that it is said aloud only on Yom Kippur.
- [ ] That a woman should say at least the first verse of the Shema is a custom; the exact formulation and the degree of obligation require checking.
- [ ] Some say “shehecheyanu” on the day of giyur, for example after the mikveh; communities differ.
- [ ] Minhagim that vary by community: the age at which one begins to wear a tallit gadol (after marriage, or from bar mitzvah); the direction of winding the tefillin strap (away from oneself or toward oneself); one or two blessings on the tefillin; the count of tzitzit windings 7-8-11-13; the slant of the mezuzah.
- [ ] Attribution of the four blessings of Birkat HaMazon (Moshe, Yehoshua, David and Shlomo, Yavneh; Berachot 48b) and the “bedi'avad” rule for “Shehakol.”
- [ ] The 30-day rule for a mezuzah in rented housing outside Israel (Menachot 44a) and checking a household mezuzah twice in seven years (Yoma 11a).
- [ ] Masorti: women in a minyan since 1973; women rabbis at JTS since 1985; the statement that the CJLS recognizes an equal obligation of women in tallit and tefillin (the 2014 position).
- [ ] Reform: how Mishkan T'filah treats the second paragraph of the Shema; the formulation “mechayeh ha-kol / mechayeh metim”; the reworked blessing against informers; whether Kaddish is said without a minyan.
- [ ] The dating of the Tiberian Masoretes (7th–10th centuries) and the statement that “Ashrei” is recited three times a day.
- [ ] Examples of Ashkenazi pronunciation (“omein,” “Toyro,” “Shabbos”) and the Lithuanian “ey” for cholam.

## 3. Shabbat

- [ ] References in the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch: 72:1, 75, 77, 96; the paraphrase of 72:1 (a public violation of Shabbat is equated with renunciation of the entire Torah).
- [ ] Paragraph numbers of the Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim: 261:2, 263:5 (the Rema), 271:13, 272, 298:2, 299:1, 300, 366 and 328:2.
- [ ] Candle-lighting time: “18 minutes, Jerusalem 40, some communities 20–30”; the end of Shabbat — “about 40–50 minutes after sunset,” or 72 minutes (Rabbeinu Tam). Both depend on the community.
- [ ] The order of the blessing over the candles: that most Sephardim bless before lighting, and the Chabad/Sephardi formulation “ner shel Shabbat kodesh.”
- [ ] Washing the hands: “two pours among Ashkenazim, three among Chabad and many Sephardim”; the custom of German Jews of washing before kiddush.
- [ ] A revi'it: about 86 ml (Rav Chaim Naeh) and about 150 ml (the Chazon Ish); according to Ashkenazi custom, the morning kiddush may be said over another significant beverage.
- [ ] Electricity: a summary of the grounds for the prohibition, including the Chazon Ish’s “building” argument; the Shabbat elevator is still under discussion.
- [ ] Women and havdalah: “according to the majority of halachic authorities” they are obligated — this is disputed (the Rema expresses a doubt); the Ashkenazi custom that women do not drink the havdalah wine.
- [ ] Sephardi blessings over the spices (over trees: “atzei vesamim”; over herbs: “isvei vesamim”) and folk customs with the havdalah wine.
- [ ] Masorti in Israel: it is written that the movement did not adopt the 1950 permission to drive; confirm that this is its current official position; also confirm the authors of the 1950 responsum (Adler, Agus, Friedman).
- [ ] Reform sources: the citation of the Pittsburgh Platform of 1885, the CCAR guide “Gates of Shabbat,” the 1999 Statement of Principles; and also what the Reform conversion schools of Russia (St. Petersburg, Moscow) in fact require of candidates.
- [ ] A candidate’s observance of Shabbat before giyur (Sanhedrin 58b and the advice to deliberately perform one small forbidden act): practice differs greatly among rabbis; the formulation is left general.
- [ ] Other statements: a quotation from Ahad Ha'am, dated 1898; Rav Yisrael Najara as the author of “Ya Ribon,” 16th century; “Ana BeKoach” is described as a prayer “in many communities”; melaveh malkah is linked with King David; making up a missed havdalah until the end of Tuesday.

## 4. Kashrut

- [ ] Siman numbers of the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch (36 — salting meat, 37 — immersion of utensils) were written from memory; for the most part the Shulchan Aruch, Yoreh De'ah, is cited — check that as well: 19:1, 39, 66, 82:2, 113:7, 120:3 and 123:3.
- [ ] Sturgeon and black caviar are marked as non-kosher “according to the Orthodox position”; some Conservative authorities may permit sturgeon — this is omitted.
- [ ] Fish with dairy: it is written that some Sephardi communities avoid this; how widespread this is.
- [ ] Hard cheese: wait as after meat; the boundary “aged 6 months or more”; the exact boundary depends on the authority.
- [ ] Turkey, pheasant, guinea fowl: pheasant and guinea fowl are mentioned only as birds that have a tradition in some communities.
- [ ] Tevilat kelim: the blessing over glass; the treatment of glazed ceramic, disposable aluminum, and electrical appliances — this differs by community and is set out in general terms.
- [ ] Bishul akum: the Ashkenazi rule (it is enough to light the fire) and the Sephardi rule (a Jew must place the food on the fire); the mention of Rav Moshe Feinstein on milk under state supervision.
- [ ] Wine: the temperature of mevushal is disputed; the linking of yayin nesech with Devarim 32:38 requires checking.
- [ ] The Conservative movement: the CJLS decisions on the wine of non-Jews (Rav Dorff’s responsum) and on certain cheeses are given in general terms; also not checked: the “Magen Tzedek” initiative “in the 2000s,” the statement that many Conservative communities practice tevilat kelim, and that their positions on bishul akum and pat akum are more lenient.
- [ ] The Reform movement: the formulations concerning the Pittsburgh Platform of 1885 and the principles of 1999 are a paraphrase, not a quotation; check “The Sacred Table” (CCAR, 2011) and the statement that a Reform rabbi does not require a kosher kitchen of a candidate.
- [ ] Customs of waiting (6, 3, or 1 hour): which custom a ger adopts is decided with his rabbi.
- [ ] Talmudic references: Chullin 63b (24 non-kosher birds) and Makkot 16b (several prohibitions for eating a single insect).
- [ ] Pirkei Avot 3:3: the numbering differs among editions (3:3 or 3:4); there is a footnote in the lesson.
- [ ] Russia: the line that kashrut certification comes from “the rabbinates of large Jewish organizations” is deliberately general, without names.

## 5. Festivals

- [ ] Chapter numbers of the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch: 97 (Rosh Chodesh, including the rule about a forgotten “Ya'aleh v'yavo” in the evening), 108, 120, 121, 124, and 129–138. Where the author was not sure, ranges of chapters are given — replace them with exact references after checking.
- [ ] The Conservative movement and the second day of a festival: it is written that the Committee on Jewish Law permits a community to keep one day of yom tov in the Diaspora; the author is not sure — the exact decision and the date are needed.
- [ ] Reform practice: many communities keep one day of Rosh Hashanah and sound the shofar on Shabbat; Tisha B'Av is chiefly a day of remembrance, and the fast is a personal choice; a description of confirmation on Shavuot.
- [ ] Conservative Pesach: the committee’s decision of 2015 permitting kitniyot; the altered wording of “Nachem” in some Conservative siddurim.
- [ ] Kapparot: the Rashba and Rabbi Yosef Karo are named as opponents, the Rema as a supporter; check these attributions.
- [ ] Chanukah: the formulation of the blessing “ner Chanukah” or “ner shel Chanukah”; the order of lighting according to the Rema 676:5; Sephardi details are given in general terms; women do not work while the candles are burning; lighting after havdalah on motza'ei Shabbat.
- [ ] The Megillah: a woman who reads for the community (the Orthodox and Masorti positions are summarized together).
- [ ] Fasts: pregnant and nursing women are exempt from the minor fasts; in the present calendar, 10 Tevet never falls on Shabbat; Ashkenazi and Sephardi customs of the Nine Days.
- [ ] The Omer: on which days the mourning applies depends on the community; Lag BaOmer as the end of the plague among the students of Rabbi Akiva, and as the yahrzeit of Rabbi Shimon bar Yochai, is a late tradition.
- [ ] Sukkot: the list of “ushpizot” (women of the Tanakh as guests of the sukkah) differs by community; the size of a kezayit (27–30 g) is one opinion; sleeping in the sukkah depends on the custom of the community.
- [ ] Midrashim and traditions: the women’s custom of Rosh Chodesh as a reward for the golden calf; David was born and died on Shavuot (Jerusalem Talmud, Chagigah 2:3); “at Sinai all Israel underwent giyur” — the source is not given.
- [ ] Israeli days of remembrance and the positions of the communities: the Haredi and Religious Zionist positions on Yom HaAtzmaut; Hallel with a blessing or without; the general Kaddish day of 10 Tevet. All of this is set out in general terms.
- [ ] Attribution of quotations: the line “teshuvah, tefillah, tzedakah” is assigned to the siddur (Unetaneh Tokef), and not to the Talmud; the quotation “Awake, you sleepers,” given without a source in the lesson, is the Rambam, Laws of Teshuvah 3:4.

## 6. Life cycle and family

The section author’s report has not yet arrived. Review items will be added here after it is received; on the first pass the section should be read in full, with attention to the same kinds of questions as in the other sections: source references, communal customs, and formulations about the movements.

## 7. Ethics

- [ ] The formulation about Reform tikkun olam (the block in lesson 6): the centrality of tikkun olam is presented in general terms (“platforms of the late 20th century”) — confirm the exact formulation and the reference to the 1999 Statement of Principles against the movement’s documents.
- [ ] Maaser in Reform (the note in lesson 3): the practice of progressive communities differs; the formulation should be confirmed by the rabbi of the receiving community.
- [ ] The Masorti position (the card for lesson 6, the ethics question about tikkun olam, the block in lesson 6): it relies on the Derakhim overview — confirmation by a Masorti rabbi is desirable.
- [ ] The number of the Torah’s warnings about oppressing a ger (36 or 46 — the count varies among the commentators): the text deliberately says “many times, dozens of times,” without a number; if precision is needed, check (Chizkuni and others count differently).
- [ ] The Rambam’s ladder (Matnot Aniyim 10:7–14): it is given according to the exact text (rungs 2–4 — mutual/one-sided anonymity of the giving); popular literature has a different scheme (“the secret fund” as a separate rung) — confirm the presentation accepted in the community.
- [ ] Interest (ribbit) from a non-Jew: it is noted that later halacha restricted this; actual practice differs greatly (heter iska and the like) — Orthodox students should follow their rabbi.
- [ ] The parable of Dama ben Netina (Kiddushin 31a): the sum of the transaction varies in the manuscripts (600/800 thousand); the main variant is taken — 600 thousand.
- [ ] The numbering of Avot 2:16: in the Vilna edition the same saying is 2:21; there is a footnote in the lesson — make sure it does not confuse the student.
- [ ] Aggadic formulations: “the first question at the Heavenly court” (KSA 62:1, following Shabbat 31a) and “a person who desires life” as the basis of the name Chafetz Chaim — widely accepted, but aggadic; at the rabbi’s discretion.
- [ ] The homiletical development of “a true hin” in KSA 62:16 is a preacher’s gloss; it is marked as such.

## 8. Beit din

- [ ] Yevamot 47a–b: the procedure and the Hebrew quotation (“דְּווּיִים, דְּחוּפִים, סְחוּפִים וּמְטֹרָפִין”).
- [ ] The Rambam, Laws of Forbidden Relations: 13:6–7 (three judges; tevilah at night and on Shabbat is not permitted), 14:6, 18:3 (a giyoret may not marry a kohen); also Mamrim 5:11 and De'ot 6:4.
- [ ] Shulchan Aruch, Yoreh De'ah 268:1/2/3/5/7: which se'if says what; in particular, the mohel’s blessing “לָמוֹל אֶת הַגֵּרִים וּלְהַטִּיף מֵהֶם דַּם בְּרִית” is in 268:5, and whether hatafat dam brit is performed without a blessing.
- [ ] Yoreh De'ah 198/201/264, Kitzur 46 and 72; Avot 2:4 (“do not separate yourself from the community”) — in some editions 2:5.
- [ ] The measure of 40 se'ah is deliberately given vaguely (“several hundred liters”); the lesson also says that the sea and natural bodies of water can serve as a mikveh — with qualifications.
- [ ] How tevilah is performed: how many immersions, whether “shehecheyanu” is said, where the judges stand during a woman’s immersion (behind the door, or screened by the water or a garment), the position of the body — all of this depends on the community.
- [ ] Whether milah is to be performed in the presence of the three judges; how long to wait between hatafat dam brit and tevilah; the rule that a ger’s milah is not performed on Shabbat.
- [ ] The name “bat Avraham ve-Sarah” as against “bat Avraham Avinu”: which communities use which.
- [ ] The “threefold refusal” is described as a custom, not as a law.
- [ ] Orthodox requirements: no longer to live with a non-Jewish partner, and to live not far from a synagogue; whether the Chief Rabbinate recognizes foreign Orthodox batei din; the “Nativ” program.
- [ ] Masorti: whether women sit on a beit din; “about a year” of study; whether driving to synagogue on Shabbat is permitted.
- [ ] Reform (ROOPI): tevilah is included in the ROOPI path, although in the section plan only brit milah had been confirmed — check on reformspb.ru; also the North American Reform position (milah and tevilah are not obligatory “since the end of the 19th century”) and patrilineal descent in the corresponding question.
- [ ] The Law of Return: the amendment of 1970, Supreme Court decisions on the recognition of giyurim, the difference between civil and religious recognition — deliberately set out in general terms; check against the latest decisions.
- [ ] The statement that Orthodox and Conservative halacha require a separate giyur for children born before the mother’s giyur (Ketubot 11a).
- [ ] An Orthodox detail: whether a ger is a relative of his non-Jewish family, and the restrictions added by the Sages.
- [ ] The question about the candidate’s former faith (beit-din-q27 was rewritten on the branch feat/mashiach: “You grew up in another faith. How do you relate to it now?”).
- [ ] The reference to the Electronic Jewish Encyclopedia (EEE) article “Proselytes” (in question 16).

## New lessons: “One and indivisible” and “Mashiach” (branch feat/mashiach)

At the request of the app’s owner, two lessons were added to the “Foundations” section, and short explanations in existing lessons. The principle: the teachings of other faiths are nowhere named; the ideas themselves (G-d in several persons, a G-d who became a human being, a Mashiach who “has already come and will return,” replacement of the Torah by a “new” covenant, prayer through an intermediary) are described and designated as false teaching for Judaism — only as explanations of the source under discussion, with a reference. The aim is to give the student knowledge of the sources, so that he will not be led astray. Please check both the formulations and this approach itself.

### New lessons (01-osnovy.json)
- [ ] **osnovy-8 “One and indivisible: Jewish monotheism”** (after lesson osnovy-4): the Shema and the Rambam, Foundations of the Torah 1:7–1:11; Devarim 4:35, 4:39; Yeshayahu 40:25, 44:6; Malachi 3:6; Bamidbar 23:19; Hoshea 11:9; principles 1–5 and “Ani Ma'amin” (the 3rd principle); ruach hakodesh as a level of prophetic inspiration; avodah zarah (Shemot 20:3–5; Sanhedrin 74a); the boundary with false teaching according to the Laws of Teshuvah 3:7; the beit din and the Laws of Forbidden Relations 14:2. The section “Verses that are often reinterpreted”: “Elohim” as a form of majesty with the verb in the singular (Bereshit 1:1), “Let us make man” (Bereshit 1:26, Rashi), Avraham’s three guests (Bereshit 18, Rashi on 18:2; 19:1), “echad” and “basar echad” (Bereshit 2:24), Devarim 13:2–4 and Foundations of the Torah 8:1 (a miracle is not proof). 16+3 cards, 18+3 questions.
- [ ] **osnovy-9 “Mashiach: whom Judaism awaits and how”**: the meaning of the word; Mashiach is a human being and is mortal (Commentary on the Mishnah, Sanhedrin ch. 10); Yeshayahu 2:4, 11:1–2, 11:6, 11:9, 11:12; Yirmiyahu 23:5–6; Yechezkel 37:24–28; Laws of Kings 11:1, 11:3 (Bar Kochba; miracles are not required), 11:4 (the criteria, “bechezkat Mashiach”), 12:1–2, 12:4–5; Berachot 34b; the aggadah of Sanhedrin 98a (clouds / a donkey; “Today, if you listen”; “in its time I will hasten it”); techiyat hametim in brief; “Ani Ma'amin” (the 12th principle); the Amidah, “Et Tzemach David”; Shabbat 31a, “Did you hope for redemption?”; Sanhedrin 97b on calculating the time; Shabtai Tzvi; the movements (Emet Ve-Emunah 1988, the Pittsburgh Platform of 1885). The conclusion from 11:4: Mashiach has not yet come, and his coming will be the first and only one; belief in a “second coming” of one who has already come contradicts the definition and the 12th principle. The section on reinterpretations: Yeshayahu 7:14 (“almah,” a sign to Achaz, 7:16), Yeshayahu 52:13–53:12 (the servant = Israel, 49:3, 41:8, Rashi), Yechezkel 18:20, Devarim 24:16, Hoshea 14:3 (there is no atonement through another’s death); a note on preachers who use Jewish rites. 18+4 cards, 20+3 questions.

### Explanatory notes in the existing lessons
- [ ] osnovy-2: a note on the name “Tanakh” and the eternal covenant (Bereshit 17:7; Shemot 31:16; Devarim 29:13–14; the 9th principle; Foundations of the Torah 9:1). To explain why we do not speak of an “old” covenant, the text says “an ‘old’, that is, outdated, covenant” — please check whether this wording is acceptable. A note on Devarim 13:1, Foundations of the Torah 9:1, Laws of Teshuvah 3:8, and Yirmiyahu 31:30–32 (“a new covenant” = the same Torah on the heart). Cards osnovy-2-u1…u4, questions osnovy-u6, u9–u11.
- [ ] osnovy-3: a note on which views contradict principles 2, 3, 5, and 12 (Commentary on the Mishnah, Sanhedrin ch. 10; Laws of Teshuvah 3:6–7; Laws of Kings 11:4); a link to the lesson on Mashiach after “Ani Ma'amin”; a direct mention of another faith’s teaching has been removed from the term “Mashiach”. Card osnovy-3-u2, questions osnovy-u1, u2.
- [ ] osnovy-4: “echad” according to Foundations of the Torah 1:7, Bamidbar 23:19, “My son, My firstborn” (Shemot 4:22) and “You are My son” (Tehillim 2:7; II Shmuel 7:14) as an image; a link to the lesson on unity. Cards osnovy-4-u1…u3, question osnovy-u4.
- [ ] ivrit-3 (Shema): a note on “echad”; card ivrit-3-u1; question ivrit-u1.
- [ ] ivrit-4 (Amidah): blessings 10, 14, and 15 as the expectation of Mashiach; prayer without an intermediary (the 5th principle; Laws of Teshuvah 3:7). Cards ivrit-4-u1, u2; questions ivrit-u3, u5.
- [ ] shabbat-5 (Havdalah): Eliyahu will come before Mashiach (Malachi 3:23; Laws of Kings 12:2). Card shabbat-5-u1; question shabbat-u1.
- [ ] prazdniki-2 (Yom Kippur): atonement through teshuvah, without an intermediary and without someone else’s sacrifice (Hoshea 14:3; Yechezkel 18:20). Card prazdniki-2-u1; question prazdniki-u1.
- [ ] prazdniki-6 (Tisha B'Av): hope for the rebuilding of the Temple; the fasts will become festivals (Laws of Fasts 5:19; Zechariah 8:19). Card prazdniki-6-u2; questions prazdniki-u2, u3.
- [ ] beit-din-3: a paragraph on the questions about the unity of G-d and about Mashiach; question beit-din-q27 and card beit-din-3-c9 have been rewritten without the names of other faiths’ teachings (“You grew up in another faith. How do you now relate to it?”); new questions beit-din-m1 (“Has Mashiach already come?”), beit-din-m2 (“Can G-d be in three persons, or appear in the form of a human being?”), cards beit-din-3-m1, m2.
- [ ] osnovy-q10: the explanation of the division into 39 books has been replaced with “with a different division into books”.

### Questions for the rabbi
- [ ] Is the approach itself acceptable: to describe other teachings without naming them, and to call them a “false teaching” (*minut*)? Is the tone calm enough? Where are the explanations too many, or too few?
- [ ] Shituf (the Orthodox block of osnovy-8): forbidden for a Jew and for a ger; the dispute about non-Jews — Tosafot, Sanhedrin 63b; the Rema, Orach Chaim 156:1; many understand the Rambam more strictly. Is it set out correctly, and is it needed in the course at all?
- [ ] Rambam, Laws of Kings 11:4: the criteria and the conclusion “one who was killed is not Mashiach”. Is the meaning conveyed correctly? Should the tradition about Mashiach ben Yosef (Sukkah 52a) be mentioned, so that the learner does not become confused, or is it better not to?
- [ ] “Mashiach will die, and after him his son and grandson will reign” (Commentary on the Mishnah, introduction to the chapter “Chelek”) — is the reference correct, and is the wording correct?
- [ ] Interpretations of verses: “Elohim” as a form of majesty; Rashi on Bereshit 1:26 and 18:2; Yeshayahu 7:14 (*almah*); Yeshayahu 53 = the people of Israel (Rashi); Tehillim 2:7 about David; Yirmiyahu 31:30–32. Is this enough, and are they presented correctly?
- [ ] “Ruach hakodesh is a level of prophetic inspiration, and not a separate person” — is this how it should be formulated?
- [ ] Wording about atonement (prazdniki-2): “the teaching that forgiveness is possible only through faith in someone’s atoning death is not Judaism”.
- [ ] The note on preachers who use a tallit, a shofar, and Pesach, but teach that Mashiach has already come (osnovy-9) — is it appropriate?
- [ ] Differences among the communities: Masorti and Reform on a personal Mashiach; are clarifications needed for your community?
- [ ] Rabbi Mikhail Finkel speaks a great deal on these subjects (broadcasts on ITON.TV). We did not find verifiable texts with quotations, and so we do not quote him. Should his lectures be recommended in “Go deeper”, and which ones specifically?
- [ ] The sections “Verses that are often reinterpreted” (osnovy-8, osnovy-9) are in effect a separate block of defense against missionaries. Keep them, shorten them, or make them optional?
- [ ] “Belief in a Mashiach who has already come, died, and returned is a false teaching” and “one who was killed is not Mashiach”: how does this relate to views current in some Hasidic circles? Is a caveat needed?
- [ ] Atonement: the wording has been replaced with “forgiveness does not depend on faith in another’s atoning death” (osnovy-9-u3, q23), so as not to conflict with “the death of the righteous atones” (Moed Katan 28a; Rashi on Bamidbar 20:1). Is this correct?
- [ ] “I do not take part in other religious services”: what about being present as a guest at relatives’ weddings and funerals?
- [ ] The note on the “Tanakh” (osnovy-2) names the term “old” covenant only in order to reject it. Is that acceptable?
- [ ] Reform: alongside the Pittsburgh Platform of 1885, should the Pittsburgh Principles of 1999 be mentioned?

<!-- interview:start -->
## Beit Din Interview Trainer (content/en/interview.json)

New section: 63 typical beit din questions in 9 topics. For each question it states what the judges want to check, the key points of an honest answer, common mistakes, and links to the lessons. Please check the wording, the accuracy of the halachic points, and the notes on how the movements differ. Source of the edit: `tools/interview/build_content.py`.

For the “Practice” and “Rehearsal” modes, 3–4 answer options have been added to each question (a correct one, and plausible but clearly incorrect distractors). This is new text; it needs to be checked as well. Where the movements differ, the options are written separately for the Orthodox, Conservative (Masorti), and Reform paths, with equal completeness. In “Topics” one still reads the question and a model answer, without keyboard input.

Introductory text of the section:

> The beit din does not examine you from a textbook: the judges want to understand who you are, why you have come, and how you already live. Collected here are questions that rabbis often ask at the interview, and explanations of what lies behind them. These are not ready-made answers to memorize. The judges hear a memorized text at once; answer in your own words, and about your real life. If you do not know something, or do not yet observe it, say so honestly.

### Motivation

- [ ] **`mot-why` Why do you want to become a Jew?**
  - What they check: The main question of the interview (“What did you see?”, Yevamot 47a). The judges check that the decision is your own, well thought out, and connected with the Torah and the Jewish people, and not with an external benefit.
  - Key points: A personal story: what brought you, and when — specific moments, people, books, Shabbats. / What you found specifically in Judaism: a connection with G-d through the mitzvot, the Torah, the people. / Why it is not enough for you to respect Judaism from the outside (the seven laws of the descendants of Noach). / How this decision has already changed your life.
  - Common mistakes: General words (“I like the culture”, “beautiful traditions”) without personal experience. / External reasons as the main ones: marriage, aliyah, documents. / A long memorized speech that sounds like a text from a book.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-3`, `beit-din-1`, `osnovy-1`, `osnovy-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “Through Shabbats, study, and meetings I found a connection with G-d, the Torah, and the Jewish people, and this is already changing my life.” / incorrect: “I like Jewish culture and beautiful traditions.” / “This is needed for marriage and for the documents for aliyah.” / “I learned a ready-made answer from a study guide and will repeat it word for word.”
- [ ] **`mot-when` When and how did you first begin to think about giyur?**
  - What they check: The judges want to see the path: the decision ripened gradually and stood the test of time, and did not appear yesterday on the impulse of a moment.
  - Key points: An honest chronology: first acquaintance, study, the first Shabbat, a meeting with a rabbi. / The doubts that there were, and how you worked through them. / How long you have already been living Jewishly and studying.
  - Common mistakes: Embellishing the story or inventing a “wonderful” moment. / Hiding important circumstances (a Jewish partner, Jewish ancestors): the judges will find out anyway.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-2`, `beit-din-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “I will tell the honest chronology: acquaintance, study, the first Shabbat, a meeting with a rabbi, doubts, and how long I have already been living Jewishly.” / incorrect: “I will invent a vivid “wonderful” moment so that the story sounds stronger.” / “I will hide that I have a Jewish partner or Jewish ancestors: that is unnecessary.” / “I will say that the decision came yesterday; the details do not matter.”
- [ ] **`mot-partner` Is your decision connected with marriage or with a Jewish partner?**
  - What they check: Giyur for the sake of marriage is not in itself considered a sufficient reason. The judges check that you would have come to Judaism even without this, and that you will keep the mitzvot whatever the outcome of the relationship.
  - Key points: Do not hide a partner or plans for marriage: honesty matters most here. / Explain how the interest became your own choice. / What you do on your own, without a partner: study, prayer, Shabbat, community. / Does your partner also observe, or is ready to observe? (in an Orthodox beit din this matters).
  - Common mistakes: Saying “for the sake of the family” as the only reason. / Saying that the partner does not matter, if that is not true.
  - Movements: Orthodox batei din expect that the Jewish partner will observe as well. Conservative (Masorti) and Reform batei din often perform giyur in the context of marriage, but they likewise expect the decision to have become your own: study, prayer, Shabbat, and community — not only “for the sake of a husband or a wife”. In the progressive communities of ROOPI this is explicitly part of a year of living alongside the community.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-3`, `semya-3`, `semya-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Orthodox path: correct: “I will speak honestly about my partner and show that the interest has become my own; in an Orthodox beit din it matters that the partner observes as well.” / incorrect: “I will say that I am coming only for the sake of family and marriage — that is enough.” / “I will deny that the partner is connected with the decision, even if that is not so.” / “I will hide the partner and will not say whether he observes the mitzvot.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Conservative (Masorti) path: correct: “I will speak honestly about my partner. Even if the giyur takes place within a marriage, the judges expect that the interest has become my own choice, and that I observe on my own.” / incorrect: “I will say that I am coming only for the sake of family and marriage — that is enough.” / “I will deny that the partner is connected with the decision, even if that is not so.” / “I will hide the partner: a Conservative (Masorti) beit din does not ask about this.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Reform path: correct: “I will speak honestly about my partner. A Reform beit din often accompanies giyur within a marriage, but it too expects that the interest has become my own choice.” / incorrect: “I will say that I am coming only for the sake of family and marriage — that is enough.” / “I will deny that the partner is connected with the decision, even if that is not so.” / “I will hide the partner: in a Reform beit din one’s personal choice is not checked.”
- [ ] **`mot-suffering` Do you know that Jews were persecuted and are still hated to this day? Why do you want this?**
  - What they check: A classic question from the Talmud (Yevamot 47a). The judges check that you understand the cost of belonging to the people and do not idealize it.
  - Key points: Yes, I know and I understand: antisemitism, the history of persecutions, the Shoah. / I accept the fate of the people together with them (the words of Ruth: “Your people is my people”). / This does not scare me off, because the decision is based on the truth of the Torah, and not on benefit.
  - Common mistakes: Brushing it aside (“it does not matter now”). / Speaking beautifully about “suffering” without understanding the real consequences for yourself and your children.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-3`, `osnovy-5`, `beit-din-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “I know about antisemitism, persecutions, and the Shoah, and I accept the fate of the people together with them; the decision is based on the truth of the Torah, and not on benefit.” / incorrect: “It no longer matters now; one need not think about hatred of Jews.” / “I will speak beautifully about “suffering” without looking into what it means for me and my children.” / “I hope that it will never touch me personally, so the question is unnecessary.”
- [ ] **`mot-noahide` A righteous non-Jew also has a share in the World to Come. Why do you want to take upon yourself 613 mitzvot?**
  - What they check: The judges check whether you understand that giyur is not needed for “salvation”, and that you consciously take upon yourself the burden of the mitzvot (kabbalat mitzvot).
  - Key points: I know about the seven laws of the descendants of Noach, and that the righteous nations of the world have a share in the World to Come. / I want not merely to be righteous, but to be part of the covenant of Israel and to keep the mitzvot. / I understand that after giyur, transgressing the mitzvot will be a sin for me.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that without giyur a person is “lost”. / Not knowing about the seven laws of the descendants of Noach.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-6`, `osnovy-1`, `beit-din-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “I know the seven laws of the descendants of Noach, and that the righteous nations have a share in the World to Come; I want to be in the covenant of Israel and to accept the mitzvot consciously.” / incorrect: “Without giyur a person is lost and has no share in the World to Come.” / “I have not heard of the seven laws of the descendants of Noach; giyur is needed for “salvation”.” / “After giyur the mitzvot may be kept selectively; it changes nothing.”
- [ ] **`mot-pressure` What will you do if the beit din says “not yet, wait”?**
  - What they check: A test of steadfastness: tradition pushes the candidate away with the left hand while drawing the candidate near with the right. The judges watch whether you will abandon the path at the first refusal.
  - Key points: I will accept it, and I will go on studying and living Jewishly. / I will ask what to work on, and I will come again. / My choice does not depend on timelines.
  - Common mistakes: Taking offense, arguing, pressuring the judges. / Saying that you will then “try somewhere else” without a reason.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-1`, `beit-din-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “I will accept it, ask what to work on, go on studying and living Jewishly, and come again: my choice does not depend on timelines.” / incorrect: “If they do not accept me at once, this path is not for me, and I will step away.” / “I will argue with the judges and demand a decision immediately.” / “I will move to another beit din the next day, changing nothing in my life.”
- [ ] **`mot-name` What Jewish name have you chosen, and why?**
  - What they check: A name shows who is an example for you. The judges want to hear that the choice is a thoughtful one.
  - Key points: The name and its meaning. / A connection with a hero of the Tanakh or a sage whose story is close to you (often Avraham, Sarah, Ruth). / A ger is called “ben/bat Avraham Avinu” (and “Sarah Imeinu”).
  - Common mistakes: Choosing a name only “because it sounds beautiful” and not knowing who bore it.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-5`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking): correct: “I will name the name I have chosen and explain the connection with the Patriarchs, the Matriarchs, or the meaning of the name; if I have not chosen yet, I will say so honestly, and how I think I will choose.” / incorrect: “I will take the first name that comes along; the meaning does not matter.” / “I will keep only my civil name: a Jewish name is not needed after giyur.” / “I will choose a celebrity’s name because it sounds beautiful, with no connection to tradition.”
- [ ] **`mot-community-choice` Why did you choose this particular movement and this community, and not another beit din?**
  - What they check: The judges want to hear not criticism of others’ paths, but a positive choice: in which community you want to pray, study, mourn, and raise children, and whether you understand the consequences of how the giyur is recognized.
  - Key points: Positive reasons: the language of the covenant, prayer, equality, a teacher, the people of the community. / What has already become your life in this community: services, study, festivals, activities. / An understanding of how other movements and the State of Israel recognize this giyur. / Honesty: you are choosing to live here, and you are not looking for the “easiest” court.
  - Common mistakes: Only against others: “I did not go to the Orthodox because it is too strict there”. / “It is easier in Reform, so that is why I am here”. / Confusion about the rules of one’s own giyur school.
  - Movements: The question is asked in every beit din. In a Reform beit din it is especially important to be able to explain an informed choice, equality in the community, and the rules of one’s own school (ROOPI, IMPJ, or the North American program), without belittling other paths.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-7`, `beit-din-2`, `beit-din-7`
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Orthodox path: correct: “I will explain the positive choice of this Orthodox community and beit din: prayer, study, a teacher, the life of the people — and I understand the consequences of recognition, without belittling other paths.” / incorrect: “I am here only because the other paths are “wrong”.” / “I am choosing the court that will accept me sooner, with no connection to a community.” / “I do not know the rules of my own beit din.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Conservative (Masorti) path: correct: “I will explain why I want to live, pray, and raise children in this particular Conservative (Masorti) community; I understand the recognition of giyur, and I am not looking for the easiest court.” / incorrect: “I am here because “it is easier this way than with the Orthodox”.” / “The other paths must be condemned in order to justify the choice.” / “I do not know the rules of my own beit din.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs checking), Reform path: correct: “I will explain the informed choice of a Reform community: the language of the covenant, equality, a teacher, the people, the rules of my school (ROOPI, IMPJ, or the North American program), and how others recognize this giyur — without belittling other paths.” / incorrect: ““It is easier in Reform, so that is why I am here”.” / “The only reason I did not go to the Orthodox is that it is too strict there — and that is enough.” / “I mix up the rules of my own giyur school, or I do not know them; it does not matter.”

### Faith and the Oneness of G-d

- [ ] **`faith-god` What do you believe? Tell us about G-d as you understand Him.**
  - What they check: A check of monotheism: you believe in the One, incorporeal, eternal G-d who gave the Torah, and you have no other beliefs alongside this.
  - Key points: G-d is One; He is not a body and has no image (Rambam's principles 1–5). / One prays to Him alone, without intermediaries. / He gave the Torah to Moshe at Sinai and knows the deeds of people. / You can say the Shema (“Shema Yisrael”) and what these words mean to you.
  - Common mistakes: A vague “I believe in a higher power.” / Any notions of G-d in human form or of a “composite” G-d.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-4`, `osnovy-8`, `osnovy-3`, `ivrit-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I believe in the one G-d, the Creator of the world, without image or partners; I'll tell how this understanding lives in my prayer and my deeds.” / incorrect: “G-d is more of a beautiful idea or a force of nature, not the Creator to whom one turns.” / “You can combine belief in the one G-d with other deities; it doesn't interfere.” / “Better not to talk about G-d at the interview: it's personal and has nothing to do with giyur.”
- [ ] **`faith-prev` What religion did you practice before? How did you part with it?**
  - What they check: The judges check that you have fully left your former beliefs and rituals behind and are not combining them with Judaism.
  - Key points: Name your past honestly: family, upbringing, baptism or rituals, if any. / Say clearly: you no longer take part in the rituals of your former religion and don't believe in its teaching. / Explain what specifically in Jewish monotheism became true for you.
  - Common mistakes: Hiding the past. / Saying that “all religions are about the same thing” or that you still go “just to keep someone company.” / Speaking about your former religion with hatred: clarity matters, not malice.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-8`, `beit-din-3`, `osnovy-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I'll honestly tell about my former path and how I came to the pure monotheism of the Torah, without mixing former notions with Judaism.” / incorrect: “I'll hide my former beliefs: the judges don't need to know.” / “I'll keep my former views alongside Judaism in order to “take the best of different paths.”” / “I'll say there was no past, even if that's not true.”
- [ ] **`faith-mashiach` Whom does Judaism await as Mashiach, and how?**
  - What they check: A check that you have accepted the Jewish understanding of Mashiach and rejected other teachings about him.
  - Key points: Mashiach is a human being, a descendant of King David, not a deity. / The signs: he will gather the exiles, restore the Temple, and bring peace and the knowledge of G-d to the world (Rambam, Laws of Kings 11). / Until this has happened, Mashiach has not yet come.
  - Common mistakes: Speaking of Mashiach as G-d or an intermediary. / Saying that Mashiach has already come.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-9`, `osnovy-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Judaism awaits Mashiach — a human being of David's line who will gather the exiles, restore justice and peace; he is not a deity.” / incorrect: “Mashiach has already come; there is no one left to wait for.” / “Mashiach is only an inner state of a person, not the Torah's promise about the end of days.” / “You can consider any influential leader of our time to be Mashiach.”
- [ ] **`faith-torah` What is the Torah? What is the Oral Torah, and why is it needed?**
  - What they check: A check that you recognize the Written Torah and the Oral Torah, not just “the text of a book.”
  - Key points: The Written Torah is the Five Books of Moshe; the Tanakh is the Torah, the Prophets, and the Writings. / The Oral Torah explains the Written Torah: the Mishnah, the Talmud, halacha. / The Oral Torah explains how the generations understood the mitzvot (tefillin, Shabbat, kashrut); in the Reform path it is studied as wisdom, not necessarily as a legal code.
  - Common mistakes: Reducing Judaism to reading the Tanakh without the tradition of interpretation. / Calling the Tanakh by foreign terms instead of Jewish ones.
  - Movements: In Orthodox and Conservative Judaism the Oral Torah is binding as law (for Masorti, a developing one). Reform Judaism sees the Written Torah and the Oral Torah as sacred, historically unfolding revelation: they must be known and loved, but halacha is not accepted as an unchanging legal code. At a Reform beit din, explain this view in your own words and show how the Torah is already changing your life. Don't just say “for us it's not obligatory.”
  - Lessons: `osnovy-2`, `osnovy-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “The Written Torah and the Oral Torah were given together; the Oral Torah is binding and explains how to fulfill the mitzvot.” / incorrect: “Only the Written Torah is needed; one need not accept the oral tradition.” / “The Torah is ancient literature; there's no need to consider it binding.” / “The Oral Torah appeared recently and has nothing to do with Sinai.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “The Torah is the covenant of Israel; the Oral Torah and halacha are binding, but the Conservative movement sees in them a living, evolving tradition, and I answer within my own beit din.” / incorrect: “Only the Written Torah is needed; the oral tradition can be set aside.” / “In the Conservative movement halacha isn't discussed at all; personal feeling is enough.” / “The Oral Torah has nothing to do with the Conservative path; only the Orthodox study it.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “The Torah is the covenant and the foundation of Jewish life; the Reform movement understands the binding nature of the Oral Torah differently, and I answer honestly within my own beit din, knowing the traditional view as well.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement you can skip studying the Torah: good deeds are enough.” / “The Oral Torah isn't needed by Reform Jews, and there's no point knowing it.” / “I'll say I accept the Oral Torah as in an Orthodox beit din, even though that's not my movement's position.”
- [ ] **`faith-doubt` Do you ever have doubts about faith? What do you do with them?**
  - What they check: The judges don't expect perfect faith without questions. What matters to them is honesty, and that your doubts lead you to learning, not away from Judaism.
  - Key points: Yes, questions come up; that's normal. / I look for answers: I study, ask my rabbi, and read. / Questions don't cancel my decision to observe the mitzvot.
  - Common mistakes: Saying “I never had doubts” if that's not true. / Listing doubts about the fundamentals of faith without a readiness to resolve them.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-3`, `beit-din-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Doubts do come, and I don't hide them: I ask my teacher and community questions and keep studying and living by the mitzvot.” / incorrect: “I've never had doubts and never will.” / “If doubts appear, it's better to step away from the path right away.” / “Doubts should be hidden from the rabbi and from yourself.”
- [ ] **`faith-principles` Name several of Rambam's Thirteen Principles of Faith.**
  - What they check: A brief check of basic knowledge about faith and that you accept it.
  - Key points: G-d exists, He is One, incorporeal, eternal; He alone may be prayed to. / Prophecy; Moshe is the greatest of the prophets; the Torah is from Heaven and unchanging. / G-d knows people's deeds and gives reward and punishment; Mashiach will come; resurrection of the dead.
  - Common mistakes: Reciting them mechanically without understanding. / Mixing up the order or inventing principles — better to honestly say you don't remember them all.
  - Movements: Rambam's Thirteen Principles are a common language of faith worth knowing in any movement. Reform Judaism does not require accepting them as mandatory dogma in every formulation (for example, about the literal giving of the Torah or about resurrection); be able to name several principles and calmly say how your path understands them, without argument and without denying tradition.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I'll name several of Rambam's Thirteen Principles and I accept them: the unity of G-d, the Torah from Heaven, reward and punishment, the coming of Mashiach, resurrection of the dead.” / incorrect: “Knowing Rambam's principles isn't required; general faith is enough.” / “You can make up the missing principles as long as the list is complete.” / “Rambam composed the Ten Commandments; I'll name those.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I'll name several of Rambam's principles — a common language of faith; I'll tell how the Conservative path understands them, without denying tradition.” / incorrect: “In the Conservative movement there's no need to learn Rambam's principles.” / “I'll recite them mechanically and say the meaning doesn't matter.” / “Rambam composed the Ten Commandments; I'll name those.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I'll name several of Rambam's principles as a common language of tradition and calmly say how the Reform path understands particular formulations, without argument and without denying tradition.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to know Rambam's principles; they're “not for us.”” / “I'll reject all of Rambam's tradition to emphasize progressiveness.” / “I'll say I accept every formulation as a binding dogma, even though that's not my movement's position.”
- [ ] **`faith-covenant` What does the covenant (brit) between G-d and Israel mean to you, and how do you enter it?**
  - What they check: The covenant is the common core of giyur in every movement. On the Reform path it is especially important to be able to formulate it: not as signing on to a legal code, but as a living bond with G-d, the Torah, and the people.
  - Key points: The covenant is the bond between G-d and the people, which a ger enters freely and fully. / For you it is concrete: the Torah, the community, the mitzvot you carry, and responsibility for the people. / Giyur is not a diploma, but the beginning of life in the covenant.
  - Common mistakes: Only abstract philosophy, without the people and without practice. / Reducing the covenant to “this is convenient for me.” / Confusing citizenship of Israel with entering the covenant.
  - Movements: The Orthodox emphasis is acceptance of the mitzvot as the law of the covenant. The Reform emphasis is a conscious entry into the same covenant through study, community, and an honest measure of mitzvot. The meaning of belonging to the people is shared.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-4`, `osnovy-1`, `beit-din-1`, `beit-din-7`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I enter the covenant by accepting the mitzvot as the law of the covenant: it is a lifelong obligation, not only a feeling of belonging.” / incorrect: “The covenant is a beautiful word with no obligations.” / “After giyur one need not accept the mitzvot; a feeling is enough.” / “The covenant can be accepted “on trial.””
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I enter the same covenant through halacha and life in a Conservative community: it is a serious obligation, not only a feeling.” / incorrect: “A covenant with no obligations.” / “After giyur nothing changes.” / “The covenant can be cancelled if it becomes hard.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I consciously enter the same covenant through study, community, and an honest measure of mitzvot: belonging is not only a feeling, and this is serious.” / incorrect: “On the Reform path the covenant is only a feeling, without study and without mitzvot.” / “The covenant does not apply to a ger.” / “The covenant can be accepted “on trial” and cancelled.”

### Shabbat

- [ ] **`shab-how` How do you observe Shabbat? Tell us about last Shabbat.**
  - What they check: One of the main questions about practice. The judges want to hear a concrete, vivid account of a real Shabbat, not a list of rules.
  - Key points: Friday preparation: food, candles before sunset, clothes. / Evening: prayer, kiddush, challot, the meal, songs. / Saturday: synagogue, the meal, study, rest, havdalah. / What is still hard for you and what you're working on.
  - Common mistakes: Reciting laws from a book instead of your own experience. / Claiming you observe everything perfectly if that's not so.
  - Movements: An Orthodox account includes candles before sunset, kiddush, meals, walking to synagogue, and refraining from melachot. A Reform account is a concrete rhythm: candles, kiddush, a service (often Friday evening), family, abstaining from work and shopping if you have taken that on; driving and electricity are usually permitted. Talk about your own day, not someone else's standard.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-2`, `shabbat-3`, `shabbat-5`, `shabbat-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I'll tell about a real Shabbat: candles before sunset, kiddush, meals, walking to synagogue, and refraining from melachot — and what's still hard for me.” / incorrect: “I'll recite laws from a book instead of my own experience.” / “I'll say I observe everything perfectly, even if that's not so.” / “In an Orthodox account synagogue and refraining from melachot don't matter.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I'll tell about my real Shabbat in a Conservative community: candles, kiddush, a service, family, and how I keep to my synagogue's practice.” / incorrect: “I'll recite a perfect Shabbat from a book, not my own day.” / “In the Conservative path Shabbat is just a dinner; you don't need to know about services.” / “I'll say in general words “I love Shabbat” without a single example.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I'll tell about my own rhythm: candles, kiddush, often a Friday-evening service, family, abstaining from work and shopping if I have taken that on; driving and electricity are usually permitted in Reform practice. I talk about my own day, not someone else's standard.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement you can skip observing Shabbat; it's not required.” / “I'll describe the full Orthodox prohibition of melachot, even though that's not my practice.” / “I'll say in general words “I love Shabbat” without a single example.”
- [ ] **`shab-melacha` What is forbidden on Shabbat, and why?**
  - What they check: A check of understanding the 39 types of work (melachot) and the meaning of the prohibition, not just the list.
  - Key points: 39 types of work connected with building the Mishkan: for example, kindling a fire, writing, cooking, carrying in a public domain. / From this follow electricity, the car, the phone, money. / The meaning: to stop creating and to acknowledge the Creator; Shabbat is a sign of the covenant.
  - Common mistakes: Reducing the prohibition to “you can't do your job.” / Not knowing about the prohibition of carrying and about the eruv.
  - Movements: The Orthodox path accepts the 39 melachot as binding law, including fire, writing, carrying, electricity, and driving. The Conservative movement considers Shabbat binding, but a number of responsa permit electric light and driving to synagogue. Reform Judaism asks you to know the melachot as the language of tradition and to build the day's holiness through rest, prayer, family, and abstaining from weekday work and shopping; driving to services and electricity are not usually considered violations in themselves. Tell us what your Shabbat actually looks like.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-4`, `shabbat-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “The 39 melachot are not done (fire, writing, cooking, carrying); from this follow electricity, the car, the phone, and money. The meaning is to stop creating and to acknowledge the Creator; I also know about the eruv.” / incorrect: “The only thing forbidden is going to work; at home everything is permitted.” / “The main thing is not to get tired; if the activity is enjoyable, it's not work.” / “Knowing about the eruv and the prohibition of carrying isn't required.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I know the 39 melachot and the meaning of Shabbat as a sign of the covenant; the Conservative movement views driving to synagogue and electricity differently, and I keep to my beit din's position.” / incorrect: “The only thing forbidden is going to work; at home everything is permitted.” / “In the Conservative movement there are no Shabbat prohibitions; it's just a family dinner.” / “A Conservative candidate has no need to know about the 39 types of work.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know the traditional 39 melachot and the meaning of Shabbat; the Reform movement views driving and electricity differently, and I honestly keep to my community's practice and my beit din's position.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement you can skip observing Shabbat; it's not required.” / “A Reform Jew has no need to know about the 39 types of work.” / “I'll say I fully keep the Orthodox prohibitions, even if that's not my practice.”
- [ ] **`shab-pikuach` What should you do if someone feels unwell on Shabbat?**
  - What they check: A check that you know: saving a life (pikuach nefesh) overrides almost all prohibitions.
  - Key points: Calling an ambulance and driving to the hospital are a duty, not a violation. / Pikuach nefesh overrides the prohibitions of Shabbat. / You must not delay or ask permission.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking you have to wait until Shabbat ends. / Not knowing the very concept of pikuach nefesh.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Saving a life (pikuach nefesh) overrides almost all Shabbat prohibitions: calling an ambulance and driving to the hospital are a duty; you must not delay.” / incorrect: “You have to wait until Shabbat is over, even if the person feels unwell.” / “First ask the rabbi for permission and only then call for help.” / “Pikuach nefesh applies only to Jews; nobody worries about others on Shabbat.”
- [ ] **`shab-candles` When and how are the Shabbat candles lit?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge of the practice: timing, the blessing, who lights.
  - Key points: No later than about 18 minutes before sunset (according to local custom). / At least two candles are lit and the blessing is said (by Ashkenazi custom — first light the candles, cover the eyes, and recite the blessing; many Sephardim say the blessing before lighting). / Usually the woman of the house lights them; if she isn't there, any adult. / You check the time in the calendar for your city.
  - Common mistakes: Lighting after sunset. / Not knowing the text or meaning of the blessing.
  - Movements: In Orthodox practice the candles are lit before sunset (usually 18 minutes before). In Reform homes and synagogues the candles are often lit at the start of dinner or at the start of the Friday-evening service, even if the sun has already set: the communal welcoming of the day itself becomes the ritual. Name your custom and your community's custom.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “The candles are lit before sunset, usually 18 minutes before, at least two of them, with a blessing; I check the time in my city's calendar.” / incorrect: “You can light the candles after sunset, when you sit down to dinner.” / “Knowing the blessing isn't required; the main thing is fire on the table.” / “Only the rabbi in the synagogue may light the candles.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I'll name the custom of my Conservative community and home: when the candles are lit, who lights them, and which blessing is said.” / incorrect: “In the Conservative path candles aren't lit; that's only an Orthodox custom.” / “Timing and the blessing don't matter; any candles at dinner are enough.” / “Only the rabbi may light the candles.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I'll name my custom and my community's custom: in Reform homes and synagogues the candles are often lit at the start of dinner or the Friday-evening service, even if the sun has already set; the communal welcoming of the day itself becomes the ritual.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to light candles; that is someone else's custom.” / “I will say that I always light them strictly 18 minutes before sunset, even though that is not my custom.” / “There is no need to know the berachah or the meaning of the candles.”
- [ ] **`shab-work` What will you do if your employer requires you to work on Shabbat?**
  - What they check: The judges check that keeping Shabbat is not negotiable for you and that you have thought through real-life situations.
  - Key points: I will explain to my employer and arrange another day. / If that is impossible, I will look for another job. / If this has already come up at your workplace, tell us how you resolved it.
  - Common mistakes: “It depends on the circumstances.” / Saying that Shabbat can be set aside for money.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-6`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I do not break Shabbat: I will arrange things with work in advance, look for a shift swap or a different position; if there is no choice, I will look for another job rather than break Shabbat.” / incorrect: “If my boss orders it, I will come in on Saturday: work comes first.” / “I will decide each time according to my mood.” / “I will hide from the beit din that I work on Saturdays.”
- [ ] **`shab-community` Where do you spend Shabbat? Do you walk to synagogue?**
  - What they check: In an Orthodox giyur, a candidate is usually expected to live within walking distance of a synagogue. The judges look at whether your Shabbat is woven into the community.
  - Key points: Where you live relative to the synagogue. / Whom you share meals with: community families, the rabbi. / If you live far away, what you plan to do (moving, Shabbat at the homes of people you know).
  - Common mistakes: Saying that you drive to synagogue on Shabbat (in an Orthodox beit din). / Spending Shabbat always alone and not knowing the community.
  - Movements: An Orthodox beit din usually expects walking distance to a synagogue and Shabbatot in observant families. In the Conservative movement, driving to synagogue has historically been permitted so that a person would not be left without a service. In Reform communities, the Friday service is often the main one of the week, and people often drive to it; the judges will ask whether you attend regularly, with whom you welcome Shabbat, and what has already become a rhythm rather than a one-time visit.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-6`, `beit-din-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “An Orthodox beit din expects the synagogue to be within walking distance; I will tell them where I live, where I go, and how things actually work.” / incorrect: “I will say that I walk, even if I get there another way.” / “It is fine to drive to synagogue on Shabbat; that is usual in an Orthodox community.” / “Where I spend Shabbat is none of the judges' business.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell honestly where I spend Shabbat and how I get there; in the Conservative movement, driving to synagogue has historically been permitted, and I follow my own community's practice.” / incorrect: “I will say that I walk even if it is not true.” / “A synagogue is not needed on the Conservative path; a home dinner is enough.” / “Where I spend Shabbat is none of the judges' business.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will tell honestly where I welcome Shabbat and how I get to the community; in the Reform movement, driving is viewed differently, and I follow my own synagogue's practice.” / incorrect: “I will say that I walk even if it is not true.” / “In the Reform movement, a synagogue on Shabbat is optional, and you do not have to know the community.” / “Where I spend Shabbat is none of the judges' business.”
- [ ] **`shab-spirit` What makes Shabbat holy for you if you do not observe all 39 melachot? What does your day actually look like?**
  - What they check: For candidates on the progressive path and on some of the Conservative paths: to check that declining the full code of melachot does not mean an empty Saturday, but rests on a living rhythm of rest and holiness.
  - Key points: The meaning of the day: creation, freedom, the covenant, and stopping weekday work. / A concrete rhythm: candles, kiddush, a service, family, and abstaining from work and shopping if you have taken that on. / You can explain the 39 melachot even if you do not accept them as a binding code. / Honestly: what you already keep every week, and what you are still learning.
  - Common mistakes: “Shabbat is holy, but on that day I live as I do on an ordinary Saturday.” / Being unable to name a single melachah or a single habit of one's own. / Passing off an Orthodox observance that you do not have.
  - Movements: At an Orthodox beit din this question is not put this way: there they expect observance of the melachot. If you are standing before an Orthodox court, speak about the full rest of the day, not about a Reform model.
  - Lessons: `shabbat-1`, `shabbat-4`, `shabbat-6`, `shabbat-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “At an Orthodox beit din I speak about the full rest of the day and observance of the melachot, not about a Reform formulation of “the spirit of Shabbat” in place of the prohibitions.” / incorrect: “A beautiful thought about rest is enough; the melachot need not be observed.” / “Shabbat is an ordinary day off.” / “I will bring a Reform answer into an Orthodox court.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell how, in Conservative practice, I set Shabbat apart from weekdays: rest, a service, meals, and the measure of prohibitions accepted in the community.” / incorrect: “A thought about rest is enough, without changing anything in the day.” / “Shabbat is an ordinary day off.” / “I will bring someone else's answer into my own court.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will tell how I already set Shabbat apart: rest, candles, kiddush, community, and abstaining from work and shopping if I have taken that on; it is a holy day of the covenant, not only a day off.” / incorrect: “On the Reform path a beautiful thought about rest is enough, without changing anything in the day.” / “Shabbat is an ordinary day off; there is no holiness in it.” / “I will promise a complete renunciation of all the melachot, even though that is not my practice and not the norm of my court.”

### Kashrut

- [ ] **`kosh-home` Is your kitchen kosher? Tell us how it is set up.**
  - What they check: A check of practice: the judges want to see that kashrut is already part of your home.
  - Key points: Separate dishes for meat and dairy (and, if you wish, for pareve). / Products with a reliable hechsher. / How you kashered the kitchen and who helped.
  - Common mistakes: Speaking vaguely — “I try” — without knowing what exactly you do. / Not knowing what a hechsher is.
  - Movements: An Orthodox kitchen: kosher products, separation of meat and milk, often two sets of dishes. A Reform one: two sets are usually not required, but knowledge of the laws and an honest standard are expected — for example, avoiding animals forbidden by the Torah, separating meat and dairy, paying attention to the ethics of food. Describe your actual arrangement.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-5`, `kashrut-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I will describe an actual Orthodox kitchen: kosher products with a hechsher, separation of meat and milk, often two sets of dishes, and how we kashered the home.” / incorrect: “I will speak vaguely — “I try” — without knowing what exactly I do.” / “There is no need to know what a hechsher is.” / “Kashrut applies only to restaurants.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will describe my actual routine and my Conservative community's custom: which products I choose, how I treat meat and milk, and what has already become a habit at home.” / incorrect: “I will say vaguely “I try,” with no single rule at home.” / “On the Conservative path, kashrut at home is not discussed.” / “I will promise two sets of dishes that I have no intention of keeping.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will describe my actual routine: a Reform kitchen usually does not require two sets of dishes, but it expects knowledge of the laws and an honest standard — for example, avoiding animals forbidden by the Torah, attention to meat and milk, and to the ethics of food.” / incorrect: ““In Reform, kashrut is not needed, so the kitchen is like everyone else's, and there is no need to know the laws.”” / “I will promise a full Orthodox kitchen with two sinks, which I have no intention of keeping.” / “I will say vaguely “I try,” without knowing what a hechsher is or which animals are forbidden.”
- [ ] **`kosh-meatmilk` Why may meat and dairy not be mixed? How long do you wait after meat?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge of one of the main laws of kashrut and its source.
  - Key points: Source: three times in the Torah, “Do not cook a kid in its mother's milk.” / One may not cook, eat, or benefit from the mixture. / After meat, one waits by custom: 6 hours, in some communities 3 or 1 hour. / After dairy, one waits less (by custom) and rinses one's mouth.
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing which waiting custom you follow. / Considering chicken “not meat.”
  - Movements: Orthodox halacha sets waiting hours after meat (usually six, in some customs three or one) and separate dishes and sinks. A Reform home may keep separation at the table or in time, without two sets of dishes; what matters is to explain that you know the classical rule and what standard you have adopted yourself.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “The source is three times in the Torah, “Do not cook a kid in its mother's milk”; one may not cook, eat, or benefit from the mixture. After meat, one waits by community custom (often six hours).” / incorrect: “Chicken can be counted as “not meat” and eaten with dairy.” / “Not knowing which waiting custom I follow and thinking there is no need to wait.” / “The prohibition applies only to goat meat.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I know the source of the prohibition in the Torah and my Conservative community's custom: how meat and milk are separated and how long one waits.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path, this law is not studied.” / “Chicken can always be eaten with cheese; it is not meat.” / “I do not know the source of the prohibition, and there is no need to know it.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know the source in the Torah and the meaning of the separation; I will tell honestly what standard I have already adopted in Reform practice, without promising someone else's waiting custom.” / incorrect: ““In Reform, meat and milk can always be mixed; there is no need to know the law.”” / “I will promise to wait six hours, even though that is neither my practice nor my community's.” / “In any case, chicken may be eaten with dairy; it is not meat, and there is no source in the Torah.”
- [ ] **`kosh-animals` Which animals and fish may be eaten?**
  - What they check: A brief check of knowledge about permitted food.
  - Key points: Animals: split hooves and chewing the cud (cow, sheep, goat). / Fish: fins and scales (no shellfish, shrimp, or catfish). / Birds: according to tradition (chicken, turkey, duck); birds of prey are forbidden. / Even a permitted animal must undergo shechitah and inspection.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that it is enough to “not eat pork.”
  - Lessons: `kashrut-2`, `kashrut-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Of land animals — those that chew the cud and have split hooves, slaughtered by shechitah; of fish — only those with fins and scales; birds — of the permitted species, not birds of prey.” / incorrect: “Any meat may be eaten if it is fresh and of good quality.” / “Seafood, including shrimp and squid, is kosher, because it is fish.” / “Whether an animal is kosher depends only on who prepared it.”
- [ ] **`kosh-out` How will you eat at relatives' homes or at work?**
  - What they check: The judges check that you have thought through difficult everyday situations and that you maintain your relationships with loved ones.
  - Key points: I will explain to my loved ones, bring my own food, or bring kosher products in sealed packaging. / I do not eat hot food from non-kosher dishes. / I keep respect for my parents and do not turn the table into a conflict.
  - Common mistakes: “At someone else's home you can skip observance.” / Cutting off relations with the family over kashrut.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-6`, `etika-2`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will plan ahead: my own food, a kosher café, vegetables and fruit, a polite refusal; I will explain to my relatives in advance, without breaking kashrut “out of politeness.”” / incorrect: “At relatives' homes, kashrut may be set aside so as not to offend anyone.” / “At work I will eat whatever is served, and at home — kosher food.” / “It is better not to visit people at all and not to explain your rules to loved ones.”
- [ ] **`kosh-why` Why is kashrut needed if modern food is safe?**
  - What they check: A check of understanding its meaning: kashrut is not hygiene, but a mitzvah and holiness.
  - Key points: It is a chok — a law whose meaning is not always explained; we keep it as the will of G-d. / Kashrut sanctifies the everyday: every meal becomes a choice. / It distinguishes and unites the people.
  - Common mistakes: Explaining kashrut solely in terms of health or hygiene.
  - Movements: Alongside talk of holiness and self-discipline, Reform Judaism often speaks of ethical kashrut: the treatment of animals, fair labor, ecology. This adds to, rather than cancels, the question of why the Torah singles out permitted food at all.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “Kashrut is a mitzvah of the Torah and a path of holiness, not just hygiene; modern food safety does not cancel it.” / incorrect: “Kashrut was needed only in antiquity; today it is superfluous.” / “Kashrut is a health diet.” / “The meaning of kashrut lies only in being different from those around.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “Kashrut is a mitzvah and a Jewish path of holiness; I will tell how the Conservative movement understands it and how I already keep it.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path, kashrut is considered outdated hygiene.” / “Kashrut is just a diet.” / “Kashrut has no meaning; it is a habit of the ancestors.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “For me, kashrut is holiness, belonging to the covenant, and often also the ethics of food; it is not just “because it's healthier this way,” and modern safety does not cancel the mitzvah.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement, kashrut need not be understood: it was abolished long ago.” / “Kashrut is nothing but the hygiene of antiquity.” / “Its only meaning is to set oneself apart from those around.”
- [ ] **`kosh-wine` What do you know about kosher wine and products with a hechsher?**
  - What they check: Practical knowledge: what to look for in the store.
  - Key points: Wine and grape juice must be kosher (special laws). / A hechsher is the mark of supervision by an authoritative rabbinate. / Bread, cheese, meat — only with a reliable hechsher.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that any “natural” product is kosher.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Wine and grape juice for the Jewish table require special kosher status; on products one looks for the hechsher — the mark of supervision.” / incorrect: “Any wine from a store is automatically kosher.” / “A hechsher is needed only on meat; other products do not have one.” / “Kosher wine differs from ordinary wine only in price.”
- [ ] **`kosh-measure` Which standard of kashrut have you adopted, and why that one? Which of the laws do you know, even if you do not keep all of them?**
  - What they check: They check knowledge of the classical categories and honest personal practice, especially in Reform homes and in some Conservative homes, where there are not two sinks, but there is a conscious order of eating.
  - Key points: Knowledge: permitted animals, blood, meat and dairy, the meaning of a hechsher. / Your standard: what you already do not eat, how the kitchen is set up, what you keep at home and outside the home. / Why this is holy for you, and not only “because it is more convenient.” / The ethical layer, if it matters to you: animals, labor, ecology — as an addition, not as a replacement.
  - Common mistakes: “Kashrut is not obligatory, so there is no question.” / Not distinguishing pork from the question of dishes. / Promising a full Orthodox kitchen that you have no intention of keeping.
  - Movements: An Orthodox beit din expects a kosher kitchen according to halacha. A Reform one expects knowledge and an honest standard. Do not carry someone else's answer into someone else's beit din.
  - Lessons: `kashrut-1`, `kashrut-5`, `kashrut-6`, `kashrut-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “An Orthodox beit din expects a kosher kitchen according to halacha: I will tell how it is already set up and what I am preparing for.” / incorrect: “Kashrut is not obligatory; there is no question.” / “I do not distinguish pork from the question of dishes.” / “I will say “I try,” without having any order at home.”
