  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell what I know of the laws and my honest standard in Conservative practice: what I already do not eat, how the kitchen is set up, what I keep at home and outside the home.” / incorrect: “Kashrut is not obligatory; there is no question.” / “I do not distinguish pork from the question of dishes.” / “I will promise a kitchen that I have no intention of keeping.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know the permitted animals, blood, meat and milk, and the meaning of a hechsher, and I will honestly name my standard: what I already do not eat, how the kitchen is set up; the ethics of food, if it matters to me, is an addition, not a replacement.” / incorrect: ““Kashrut is not obligatory, so there is no question.”” / “I do not distinguish pork from the question of dishes.” / “I will promise a full Orthodox kitchen that I have no intention of keeping.”

### Holidays

- [ ] **`hol-favorite` Which holiday is closest to you, and why? How did you spend it?**
  - What they check: A personal question: the judges want to hear experience, not an encyclopedia.
  - Key points: A specific holiday and what you did (seder, sukkah, shofar, Chanukah candles). / What it means to you personally. / With whom you celebrated it.
  - Common mistakes: Retelling from a textbook without one's own experience. / Choosing a holiday only for its “atmosphere.”
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-2`, `prazdniki-3`, `prazdniki-4`, `prazdniki-5`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will name the holiday that is close to me and tell how I actually spent it: what I did, with whom, which mitzvot I fulfilled.” / incorrect: “I will list all the holidays in a row, without highlighting personal experience.” / “I have not yet celebrated the holidays, but I will say that I have, in order to look prepared.” / “Jewish holidays mean nothing to me so far; public holidays are enough.”
- [ ] **`hol-yomkippur` What is Yom Kippur, and how is it observed?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge of the holiest day of the year.
  - Key points: The Day of Atonement: a fast of about 25 hours, prayers, repentance (teshuvah). / Eating, drinking, leather shoes, anointing, and marital relations are forbidden. / Sins against other people are forgiven only after one has asked the person for forgiveness.
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing that for the sick fasting is not obligatory (pikuach nefesh).
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-2`, `prazdniki-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Yom Kippur is the Day of Atonement: fasting, prayer, a ban on work, making peace with people in advance; it is not a “sad day,” but a return to G-d.” / incorrect: “Yom Kippur is a merry holiday with an abundant meal, like Pesach.” / “It is enough not to go to work; prayer and fasting are optional.” / “Yom Kippur atones even for wrongs between people, even if you have not made peace with those close to you.”
- [ ] **`hol-pesach` Tell us about Pesach: what do you do at home?**
  - What they check: A check of practice: chametz, seder, matzah.
  - Key points: Chametz is removed and sold, the home is searched. / Seder: the Haggadah, the four cups, matzah, maror. / Seven days (eight in the Diaspora) without chametz.
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing what chametz is.
  - Movements: Diaspora Orthodox communities keep eight days and a strict prohibition of chametz; Ashkenazim usually do not eat kitniyot. Many Reform communities celebrate seven days and one seder, and each person adopts the measure of chametz removal consciously. Tell us how you prepare your home and conduct the seder.
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I remove chametz, eat matzah, hold a seder with the Haggadah, the four cups, and the story of the Exodus — as it was at my home.” / incorrect: “On Pesach it is enough to eat matzah once; chametz need not be removed.” / “A seder is an ordinary dinner without any order.” / “Pesach is celebrated only in synagogue.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell how chametz is removed, matzah eaten, and the seder conducted in my Conservative family and community — concretely, from my own experience.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path, a seder is optional.” / “Chametz need not be removed; matzah on the table is enough.” / “Pesach is celebrated only in synagogue.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will tell about my seder and preparation: matzah, the Haggadah, the story of the Exodus, and which arrangement of chametz my Reform community follows — honestly, from my own experience.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement, Pesach need not be celebrated at home.” / “A seder is just a secular dinner without the Haggadah and without the meaning of the Exodus.” / “I will promise a full Orthodox sale of chametz, which I have no intention of doing.”
- [ ] **`hol-sukkot` What is a sukkah, and what are the four species?**
  - What they check: A brief check of knowledge about Sukkot.
  - Key points: A sukkah is a temporary booth: one eats in it (and many sleep in it too) for seven days. / The four species: etrog, lulav, myrtle (hadas), willow (aravah). / A reminder of life in the desert and of G-d's protection.
  - Common mistakes: Confusing Sukkot with other harvest festivals.
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “A sukkah is a booth in which one eats (and, where possible, lives) for seven days; the four species are the lulav, etrog, hadas, and aravah — they are taken together and waved (na'anuim).” / incorrect: “A sukkah is a yard decoration; eating in it is not necessary.” / “The four species are any four beautiful plants you can find.” / “Sukkot lasts one day and coincides with Simchat Torah.”
- [ ] **`hol-calendar` Why do Jewish holidays fall on different dates every year?**
  - What they check: A check of understanding of the Jewish calendar.
  - Key points: The calendar is lunisolar: months follow the moon, and the year is aligned with the sun. / In a leap year a second Adar is added. / Days begin in the evening.
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing that the day begins at sunset.
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “The Jewish calendar is lunisolar: months follow the moon, and an extra month is added to adjust the year, so the dates shift relative to the civil calendar.” / incorrect: “The holidays are deliberately moved each year for convenience.” / “The Jewish year is reckoned only by the sun, like the civil one.” / “The dates change at random; there is no system to it.”
- [ ] **`hol-fasts` Which fasts are there in the Jewish year? What is Tisha B'Av?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge of the memorial days and of mourning over the destruction of the Temple.
  - Key points: Yom Kippur and Tisha B'Av are full fasts. / Tisha B'Av commemorates the destruction of both Temples and other calamities of the people. / There are also minor fasts: the Fast of Gedaliah, 10 Tevet, 17 Tammuz, the Fast of Esther.
  - Common mistakes: Considering fasts a “diet.”
  - Movements: Orthodox communities keep the full and minor fasts according to the calendar. The Conservative movement recognizes the fasts, though the minor ones depend on the community. In Reform communities many observe Yom Kippur as a full fast, often do not keep the minor fasts, and today frequently mark Tisha B'Av with the reading of Eichah and reflection; fasting remains a personal choice. Tell us your community's practice and your own.
  - Lessons: `prazdniki-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “The main fasts are Yom Kippur and Tisha B'Av; there are also shorter ones. I will tell which ones I already keep.” / incorrect: “Apart from Yom Kippur there are no fasts.” / “Tisha B'Av is a joyful day of the giving of the Torah.” / “Fasts concern only rabbis.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will name the main fasts and my Conservative community's custom: which days I already observe and how.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path there are no fasts, except perhaps Yom Kippur, and there is no need to know them.” / “Tisha B'Av is a holiday.” / “Fasts concern only rabbis.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will name Yom Kippur and Tisha B'Av and tell which fasts I already keep in my Reform community; knowing the calendar is needed even where not all days are observed in the same way.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to know the fasts: in this movement there are none.” / “Tisha B'Av is a holiday of joy.” / “I will promise to observe all the minor fasts, even though that is not my practice.”

### Family and community

- [ ] **`fam-parents` How do your parents and loved ones feel about your decision?**
  - What they check: The judges check that you have thought through your relationships with your family and that you maintain respect for your parents.
  - Key points: Honestly: who supports you and who is against it. / How you explain your decision to them. / The mitzvah of honoring parents remains, and you keep it.
  - Common mistakes: Saying that after giyur you will break with your family. / Hiding the conflict, if there is one.
  - Lessons: `etika-2`, `beit-din-6`, `semya-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will tell honestly how my loved ones reacted: support, arguments, or pain — and how I preserve respect for my parents without abandoning the path.” / incorrect: “I will hide the conflict with my parents so that the story looks smooth.” / “After giyur, my parents stop being my family; I can break with them.” / “If loved ones are against it, it is better not to pursue giyur, even if the choice is mine.”
- [ ] **`fam-children` How will you raise your children?**
  - What they check: The judges want to hear that your children will grow up Jewish: school, home, mitzvot.
  - Key points: A Jewish home: Shabbat, kashrut, holidays. / Jewish education, Torah study. / Brit milah for sons, bar and bat mitzvah.
  - Common mistakes: “Let them decide when they grow up.”
  - Movements: In every movement you will be asked how you will raise your children as Jews. In the Reform movement it is also important to understand the 1983 CCAR decision about a child of one Jewish parent who is raised as a Jew, and the fact that Orthodox and Conservative communities do not accept this rule.
  - Lessons: `semya-1`, `semya-2`, `semya-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I want to raise my children in Torah, mitzvot, and community: Shabbat, holidays, study, a Jewish name, and belonging to the people.” / incorrect: “No Jewish upbringing is needed until adulthood.” / “It is enough to tell stories now and then.” / “Giyur concerns only me; there is no need to talk about children.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I want to raise my children in a Conservative community: Shabbat, holidays, study, and belonging to the people — and I discuss their status and path honestly.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path, raising children is not discussed.” / “The children will choose for themselves later; until then, nothing is needed.” / “Giyur concerns only me.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I want to raise my children in a Reform community: Shabbat, holidays, study, a Jewish name, and the life of the people; I discuss their status and the path of giyur honestly, if it is needed.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement, children need not be raised as Jews.” / “Stories are enough, without a community and holidays.” / “Giyur concerns only me; the judges have no need to know about the children.”
- [ ] **`fam-kids-existing` Do you already have children? Will they undergo giyur?**
  - What they check: An important practical question: the giyur of minor children, and how the family will live.
  - Key points: Children before bar/bat mitzvah may undergo giyur together with a parent, by decision of the beit din. / How the children already take part in Jewish life now. / If the other parent is not Jewish — how you have agreed.
  - Common mistakes: Not discussing this question with the rabbi in advance.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-2`, `beit-din-6`, `semya-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will say honestly whether I have children, and how we discuss their path: the giyur of children is a separate decision; it is not hidden and not put off ‘for later, without a conversation.’” / incorrect: “I will hide that I have children: the beit din has no need to know.” / “The children automatically become Jews together with me; no separate steps are needed.” / “This conversation need not be held with the children.”
- [ ] **`fam-community` Which community do you attend? Who is your rabbi?**
  - What they check: The judges check that you are not alone: there is a community and a mentor to whom you turn.
  - Key points: The name of the community, how often you are there. / Who your rabbi is and how you study together. / How you help the community (volunteering, the minyan, meals).
  - Common mistakes: Knowing the community only from the internet.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-2`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will name the community and the rabbi I go to, how often, and how I take part; if I do not yet have a community, I will say so honestly, and how I am looking for one.” / incorrect: “I will invent a community and a rabbi's name, so as to look rooted.” / “A community is not needed; it is enough to study from books at home.” / “I go to different places without a lasting connection, and it is better not to specify that.”
- [ ] **`fam-purity` What do you know about the laws of family purity?**
  - What they check: For those who are married and those who intend to marry: a check of readiness for the mikveh and the laws of niddah.
  - Key points: The time of separation during the niddah period and after it. / Immersion in the mikveh before intimacy is resumed. / You have studied these laws with a mentor or a rabbi.
  - Common mistakes: Considering these laws “optional.”
  - Movements: An Orthodox beit din asks about the laws of family purity as a requirement and expects study with a mentor. The Conservative movement keeps the subject as a tradition that a family may accept, but rarely makes it a condition of giyur. Reform Judaism does not consider the laws of niddah obligatory; the mikveh, at the same time, is often alive as a spiritual source of transition — giyur, a wedding, personal milestones. Be able to explain the classical law and say what place you want to give the mikveh in your family.
  - Lessons: `semya-5`, `beit-din-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “An Orthodox beit din will ask about this without fail: niddah, marital separation on those days, and immersion in the mikveh; I will tell what I already know and how I am prepared to observe it.” / incorrect: “These are outdated rules; they are not obligatory for an Orthodox candidate.” / “Family purity concerns only kohanim.” / “It is better to keep silent about this, even if the judge asks.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I know the laws of niddah and the mikveh, and my Conservative community's attitude toward them; I will tell honestly what I am learning and what I am ready to accept within my beit din.” / incorrect: “In the Conservative movement, family purity does not exist; there is no need to know it.” / “Family purity concerns only kohanim.” / “It is better to keep silent about this, even if the judge asks.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know what niddah and the mikveh are, and how my Reform community approaches family purity; I will speak about this just as seriously as about the other sides of Jewish family life.” / incorrect: “A Reform candidate has no need to know the laws of family purity; this is ‘not about us.’” / “Family purity concerns only kohanim.” / “It is better to keep silent about this, even if the judge asks.”
- [ ] **`fam-israel` What do Israel and the Jewish people mean to you?**
  - What they check: A check of the sense of belonging: giyur is an entry both into the people and into the faith.
  - Key points: The bond with Eretz Israel in prayer and in history. / Responsibility for other Jews (all Jews are responsible for one another). / What you are doing for the people now.
  - Common mistakes: Reducing the answer to politics. / Saying that Israel is the main or the only reason for giyur.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-5`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Israel is the land of the covenant and the home of the Jewish people; I am bound to it, I follow its life, and I see myself as part of the people, not as an outside observer.” / incorrect: “For me Israel is an ordinary country; it has nothing to do with the Jewish path.” / “The people and the Land do not matter; personal faith without a connection to Israel is enough.” / “It is better not to discuss one's attitude to Israel at the interview.”

### Mitzvot and halacha

- [ ] **`mitz-accept` Are you ready to take upon yourself all the mitzvot of the Torah?**
  - What they check: This is the essence of giyur — kabbalat mitzvot. Without full agreement, giyur is not valid (in the Orthodox understanding).
  - Key points: Orthodox answer: I accept all the mitzvot, including those I do not yet fully observe. Reform: I enter the covenant, I will study, and I will honestly bear the measure of mitzvot I have accepted. / I understand that I will continue to study and grow. / Which of the mitzvot you already observe.
  - Common mistakes: Reservations of “except this one” or “I will see later.” / Promising perfect observance of what you do not yet know.
  - Movements: An Orthodox beit din asks about readiness to accept all the mitzvot, including those you do not yet fully keep. The Conservative movement also speaks the language of obligation, although halacha is understood as developing. At a Reform beit din you will be asked about entering the covenant: whether you are ready to live as a Jew of your community, to study all your life, and honestly to bear the measure of mitzvot that you consciously accept. The answer “I accept everything, although I observe nothing” is just as unconvincing here as the answer “I need nothing, because in Reform one may.”
  - Lessons: `beit-din-1`, `osnovy-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “Yes, I am ready to accept all the mitzvot of the Torah (kabbalat mitzvot) as a lifelong obligation and to grow in observance.” / incorrect: “I will accept only the convenient mitzvot; the rest can be postponed forever.” / “The mitzvot are advice, not obligations.” / “I will say ‘yes’ for the record, without intending to change my life.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “Yes, I am ready to live by the mitzvot within the Conservative understanding of halacha: this is a serious obligation of my beit din, not a set of optional advice.” / incorrect: “In the Conservative movement the mitzvot need not be accepted; good intentions are enough.” / “The mitzvot are advice, not obligations; after giyur nothing changes.” / “I will say ‘yes’ for the record, without intending to change my life.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “Yes, I am ready to accept the Jewish way of life and the mitzvot as my Reform movement understands them: this is a serious obligation, not a formality, and I am answerable within the framework of my beit din.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement the mitzvot need not be accepted; it is enough to call oneself a Jew.” / “The mitzvot are day-to-day advice; after giyur nothing changes.” / “I will say ‘yes’ as at an Orthodox beit din, although that is not the position of my movement and not my practice.”
- [ ] **`mitz-hard` Which mitzvah comes hardest for you?**
  - What they check: A check of self-knowledge and honesty: there are no perfect candidates.
  - Key points: Name a real difficulty. / Explain what you are doing to overcome it. / Show that this is not a reason to give up the mitzvah.
  - Common mistakes: “Everything is easy for me.” / Naming a difficulty and saying that you do not intend to observe it.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-3`, `osnovy-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will honestly name what comes harder (Shabbat, kashrut, prayer, or something else), and how I am working on it: the judges value the truth more than the picture of ‘everything is easy for me.’” / incorrect: “I will say that all the mitzvot come equally easily, even if that is not so.” / “A difficult mitzvah can simply be left unobserved; the beit din has no need to know.” / “It is better to name an easy mitzvah, so as not to seem weak.”
- [ ] **`mitz-ethics` Which mitzvot between one person and another do you consider important?**
  - What they check: Judaism is not only rites. The judges look at whether you understand the ethics of the Torah.
  - Key points: “Love your fellow as yourself” and love of the ger. / Tzedakah, chesed, honesty in one's dealings. / The ethics of speech: the prohibition of evil speech (lashon hara).
  - Common mistakes: Speaking only about rites.
  - Lessons: `etika-2`, `etika-3`, `etika-4`, `etika-5`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “The mitzvot between a person and their fellow are important: honesty, tzedakah, the prohibition of evil speech, respect for parents, justice; I will tell how this shows in my life.” / incorrect: “Between one person and another there are no special mitzvot; rites are enough.” / “Ethics is a general ‘be kind’; it is not tied to the Torah.” / “With the judges it is better to speak only about the kitchen and Shabbat; about people, it is not worth it.”
- [ ] **`mitz-tzedaka` What is tzedakah? Do you give it?**
  - What they check: A check of practice: tzedakah is an obligation, not simply charity according to one's mood.
  - Key points: Tzedakah is justice: sharing is obligatory. / It is customary to give a tenth of one's income (maaser). / The highest level is to help a person become independent (the Rambam).
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing that this is an obligation.
  - Lessons: `etika-3`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Tzedakah is the mitzvah of justice: setting aside a portion of one's means for those in need and for the community; I will tell how I already give, even if only a little.” / incorrect: “Tzedakah is a voluntary gesture for the wealthy; an ordinary person does not need it.” / “Tzedakah replaces all the other mitzvot; nothing else need be observed.” / “After giyur I need not give tzedakah: now I myself am the ‘receiving side’ in the community.”
- [ ] **`mitz-ger` What will change in your life after giyur?**
  - What they check: A check of understanding of the new status of a ger and of the obligations.
  - Key points: You are a Jew with full rights, and all the mitzvot become an obligation. / The prohibition of reminding a ger of their past (ona'at ha-ger). / Continuing to study, to live in the community, to keep a Jewish home.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that after giyur one can “relax.”
  - Lessons: `beit-din-6`, `etika-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “After giyur I become a Jew in every respect: the mitzvot are obligatory, I am part of the people forever, and both joy and responsibility are bound up with this.” / incorrect: “After giyur life will stay the same; only an entry will appear in the documents.” / “Giyur can be ‘tried,’ and if one wishes one can go back without consequences.” / “After giyur it is enough to appear at the synagogue on the holidays; weekdays do not change.”
- [ ] **`mitz-informed` What does an informed choice of the mitzvot mean to you, and how do you already apply it in your life?**
  - What they check: A question characteristic of a Progressive beit din: they check that the principle has not turned into a justification for inaction, and that the candidate has a living, honest measure of practice.
  - Key points: First study and the voice of the tradition, then a personal decision. / Concrete examples: Shabbat, kashrut, prayer, the holidays, ethics. / How the choice differs from arbitrariness and from accepting all of halacha as a code. / Where you are headed after giyur, and not only what you already do.
  - Common mistakes: “In Reform one need not observe anything.” / General words without a single personal habit. / Passing ignorance off as a principle.
  - Movements: In an Orthodox and a Conservative beit din the same conversation sounds like accepting an obligation according to the halacha of one's movement. Answer in the language of the court before which you stand.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-7`, `osnovy-7`, `osnovy-6`, `etika-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “At an Orthodox beit din this conversation is about accepting an obligation according to halacha: I will tell how I already live by the mitzvot and what I am preparing for, in the language of this court.” / incorrect: “One may observe only what is convenient; that is what ‘informed choice’ is.” / “General words without a single habit.” / “Ignorance can be passed off as a principle.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “In a Conservative beit din I speak of the obligation according to the halacha of my movement: study, concrete practice, and growth after giyur.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path the mitzvot need not be practiced.” / “General words without habits.” / “Ignorance can be passed off as a principle.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “Informed choice is first study and the voice of the tradition, then a personal decision, not a justification for inaction; I will give living examples and say where I am heading after giyur.” / incorrect: “‘In Reform one need not observe anything.’” / “General words without a single personal habit.” / “Passing ignorance off as a principle.”
- [ ] **`mitz-tikkun` What is tikkun olam in your life? How is the service of justice connected, for you, with prayer, Shabbat, and belonging to the people?**
  - What they check: In Progressive Judaism, repair of the world is a religious obligation, not a replacement for rites. The judges want to see concrete deeds and an understanding that tikkun olam stands alongside the Torah and the community, not in their place.
  - Key points: Concrete deeds: chesed, tzedakah, justice, care for people and for the world. / The connection with the Jewish rhythm: this is part of the covenant, not only civic kindness. / Tikkun olam does not cancel prayer, Shabbat, and study.
  - Common mistakes: General slogans without a single deed. / “It is enough for me to be a good person; the rest is not needed.” / Political speech without a Jewish root.
  - Movements: The subject is close to all the movements (tzedakah, chesed, din). In a Reform beit din it is often asked directly, as part of the candidate's religious life.
  - Lessons: `etika-6`, `etika-3`, `etika-2`, `beit-din-7`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “Tzedakah, chesed, and justice are mitzvot; I will tell of concrete deeds and of the fact that they stand alongside the Torah, prayer, and Shabbat, not in their place.” / incorrect: “It is enough to be a good person; the mitzvot are not needed.” / “General slogans without deeds.” / “Political speech without a Jewish root.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell of concrete deeds of justice and mercy, and how they are connected with the life of the Conservative community, with prayer, and with Shabbat.” / incorrect: “It is enough to be a good person; the rest is not needed.” / “General slogans without deeds.” / “Political speech without a Jewish root.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “Tikkun olam in my life is concrete deeds of chesed, tzedakah, and justice, connected with the Torah, prayer, Shabbat, and the people; it is a religious obligation, not a replacement for rites.” / incorrect: “‘It is enough for me to be a good person; prayer and Shabbat are not needed.’” / “General slogans without a single deed.” / “Political speech without a Jewish root.”

### Prayer and Hebrew

- [ ] **`pray-daily` Do you pray every day? Which prayers do you know?**
  - What they check: The judges check that prayer is part of your day.
  - Key points: Three prayers: Shacharit, Minchah, Maariv. / “Modeh Ani” in the morning, “Shema” in the morning and in the evening, the Amidah. / What you read yourself, and in which language.
  - Common mistakes: Saying that you pray “in your own words” and do not know a single prayer.
  - Movements: The Orthodox norm is three daily prayers and berachot at their proper times. Reform practice often places at the center an understanding of the words, the Friday service, and those daily prayers that a person actually carries: the Shema, Modeh Ani, the blessings over food. Say what you already say every day, and in which language.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-4`, `ivrit-3`, `ivrit-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I will tell honestly which of the three daily prayers I already say, whether I know the Shema and the Amidah, and what I am still learning.” / incorrect: “I will say that I pray three times a day, even if that is not so.” / “An Orthodox candidate does not need daily prayer.” / “The rabbi recites the prayer for me.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell which prayers I already say and how my Conservative community prays — honestly, without embellishment.” / incorrect: “I will say that I pray three times a day, even if that is not so.” / “On the Conservative path, daily prayer is not needed.” / “The rabbi recites the prayer for me.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will tell honestly which prayers I already say myself and how my Reform community prays: my own words, the community siddur, the Shema — and what I am still learning, without promising someone else’s schedule of three prayers.” / incorrect: “In the Reform movement there is no need to pray oneself; coming once a year is enough.” / “I will say that I already read three full Orthodox tefillot a day, even though that is not so.” / “The rabbi recites the prayer for me; there is no need to learn the siddur.”
- [ ] **`pray-shma` Read “Shema Yisrael” and explain its meaning.**
  - What they check: A nearly obligatory question: “Shema” is the foundation of Jewish faith.
  - Key points: “Shema Yisrael, Adonai Eloheinu, Adonai echad”: Hear, Israel, the L-rd is our G-d, the L-rd is One. / This is a proclamation of the unity of G-d and acceptance of the yoke of Heaven. / It is read in the morning and in the evening, and before sleep.
  - Common mistakes: Being unable to read the first line. / Not understanding the translation.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-3`, `osnovy-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “‘Shema Yisrael’ is acceptance of the one G-d and of the burden of the mitzvot; I will read what I already know and explain the meaning in my own words, without hiding it if I do not yet know it by heart.” / incorrect: “The Shema is a table song; there is no special meaning in it.” / “If I do not know it by heart, it is better to stay silent or to invent a text.” / “The Shema is read only on Yom Kippur; on weekdays it is not needed.”
- [ ] **`pray-brachot` Which berachot do you say before eating?**
  - What they check: A check of the daily practice of blessings.
  - Key points: HaMotzi — for bread; Mezonot — for baked goods; HaGafen — for wine. / HaEtz — for fruit of the trees; HaAdamah — for fruit of the ground; Shehakol — for everything else. / Birkat HaMazon after bread.
  - Common mistakes: Knowing the berachot only in theory.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-5`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Before eating one says a berachah according to the kind of food: ‘HaMotzi’ for bread, ‘Mezonot’ for baked goods, ‘HaGafen’ for wine, ‘HaEtz’, ‘HaAdamah’, and ‘Shehakol’; after a meal with bread — Birkat HaMazon.” / incorrect: “Before eating it is enough to say ‘thank you’ once in one’s own words; there is no need to learn the berachot.” / “There is one berachah for all food; the kind of food does not matter.” / “Berachot are recited only in the synagogue; they are not said at home.”
- [ ] **`pray-hebrew` Do you read in Hebrew?**
  - What they check: The judges check a basic ability to read a siddur (the requirements differ).
  - Key points: Honestly: I read slowly / fluently / I am learning. / You know the alphabet and the vowel signs. / One may also pray in a language one understands, but learning Hebrew is important.
  - Common mistakes: Claiming that you read fluently if that is not so: they will ask you to read.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-1`, `ivrit-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “I will say honestly at what level I read: the letters, prayers with vowel signs, or already without them; and how I am learning further. The judges do not require a scholar, but they expect progress.” / incorrect: “I will say that I read Hebrew fluently, even if for now I can only make out the letters.” / “Hebrew is not needed for giyur; one can pray one’s whole life only from an interlinear translation and never study.” / “I will learn Hebrew sometime later; there is no need to begin before giyur.”
- [ ] **`pray-objects` What are tefillin, a tallit, and a mezuzah?**
  - What they check: Knowledge of the objects of the mitzvot.
  - Key points: Tefillin are boxes with passages from the Torah; they are put on the arm and the head on weekday mornings. / A tallit is a prayer shawl with tzitzit. / A mezuzah is a scroll with the “Shema” on the doorpost.
  - Common mistakes: Confusing the purpose of the objects.
  - Movements: A tallit, tefillin, and a mezuzah are obligatory in Orthodox practice, each according to its own rules (tefillin — on weekdays). In Reform communities a mezuzah is very widespread; a tallit is often worn by everyone who comes up to the Torah; tefillin are a personal and increasingly common custom, including among women. Describe what you yourself use.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “Tefillin are laid on weekdays, a tallit with tzitzit is for prayer, a mezuzah is a scroll on the doorpost; I know that the text of the Torah is inside them, and how this is already entering my life.” / incorrect: “These are ornaments; there is nothing inside them.” / “A mezuzah is a good-luck amulet; the text does not matter.” / “These objects are needed only by rabbis.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I know what tefillin, a tallit, and a mezuzah are, and how my Conservative community uses them; I will tell what I already do myself.” / incorrect: “On the Conservative path these objects are not used; there is no need to know them.” / “A mezuzah is an amulet; the text does not matter.” / “They are needed only by rabbis.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know what tefillin, a tallit, and a mezuzah are and what text is in them; I will tell how my Reform community relates to them and what has already entered my practice, without promising someone else’s custom.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to know these objects; this is ‘not about us.’” / “A mezuzah is a good-luck amulet; the text inside does not matter.” / “I will promise to lay tefillin every day, although that is not my community’s practice and not my own.”
- [ ] **`pray-community` How does prayer go in your community? Which siddur do you use, and which prayers do you already say yourself?**
  - What they check: They check not knowledge of rare formulations, but living participation: the service, the community siddur, the Shema and the berachot with understanding, Hebrew alongside a language one understands.
  - Key points: What the service looks like: the day of the week, Hebrew and the community’s language, the participation of men and women. / Which siddur you pray from (in North American communities, often Mishkan T'filah; in ROOPI — the siddur of one’s own synagogue). / What you already say yourself: the Shema, Modeh Ani, the berachot over food, parts of the Amidah. / Understanding the words matters more than a text memorized without meaning.
  - Common mistakes: “We pray like everyone else,” without a single detail. / Not knowing which book is in your synagogue. / Claiming that Hebrew is not needed in Reform prayer.
  - Movements: Orthodox communities pray according to the traditional nusach, without instrumental music on Shabbat and with separate seating. Reform communities often use their own siddurim, mixed seating, a language one understands and, in many communities, music. Name your own custom exactly.
  - Lessons: `ivrit-6`, `ivrit-3`, `ivrit-4`, `ivrit-5`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I will tell about the traditional nusach of my Orthodox community: without instrumental music on Shabbat, with separate seating, and which prayers I already say myself.” / incorrect: “I will invent customs I do not know.” / “It is enough for me to be present in silence; there is no need to learn the nusach.” / “Instrumental music on an Orthodox Shabbat is ordinary practice.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will tell which siddur and which order my Conservative community prays with, and what I already say myself.” / incorrect: “I will invent a siddur I have not seen.” / “There is no need to know the community’s custom.” / “It is enough for me to sit in silence in the hall my whole life.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will tell how my Reform community prays: its own siddur, often mixed seating, sometimes music, and which prayers I already say myself.” / incorrect: “I will invent a siddur and customs I do not know.” / “In a Reform synagogue it is enough to be present in silence; there is no need to learn the siddur.” / “I will say that we have separate seating and a ban on music, as in an Orthodox community, even though that is not so.”

### Knowledge check

- [ ] **`know-avot` Who are the patriarchs and matriarchs of the Jewish people?**
  - What they check: A basic check of knowledge.
  - Key points: Avraham, Yitzchak, Yaakov. / Sarah, Rivka, Rachel, Leah. / Avraham was the first to come to the One G-d; that is why a ger is called a son of Avraham.
  - Common mistakes: Mixing up the generations.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-5`, `beit-din-5`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “The patriarchs are Avraham, Yitzchak and Yaakov; the matriarchs are Sarah, Rivka, Rachel and Leah.” / incorrect: “The patriarchs are Moshe, Aharon and Yehoshua.” / “The matriarchs are Miriam, Devorah and Esther, and there are no patriarchs in the tradition.” / “Only the kohanim of Aharon’s line are called the patriarchs.”
- [ ] **`know-ruth` Who is Ruth, and why is her story important for a ger?**
  - What they check: Ruth is the model of giyur. The judges often ask about her.
  - Key points: A Moabite woman who followed her mother-in-law Naomi. / “Your people are my people, and your G-d is my G-d” (Ruth 1:16). / The great-grandmother of King David; the Book of Ruth is read on Shavuot.
  - Common mistakes: Not knowing who Ruth is.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-3`, `prazdniki-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Ruth the Moabite came to the people of Israel (‘your people are my people, your G-d is my G-d’) and became the ancestress of David’s line; her path is a model of sincere giyur.” / incorrect: “Ruth is a queen who was born Jewish and was never a giyoret.” / “Ruth’s story has nothing to do with a ger; it is simply a family tale.” / “Ruth accepted Judaism for the sake of gain and a quick marriage, and that is the example.”
- [ ] **`know-613` How many mitzvot are there in the Torah? How are they divided?**
  - What they check: A check of the foundations.
  - Key points: 613 mitzvot: 248 positive commandments and 365 prohibitions. / Between a person and G-d and between a person and another person. / Some of the mitzvot are connected with the Temple or with Eretz Israel and are not fulfilled now.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that there are ten commandments.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “The Torah has 613 mitzvot: 248 positive and 365 prohibitions; not all of them apply to each person, but the obligation is accepted as a whole.” / incorrect: “The Torah has ten commandments; there are no others.” / “There are 613 mitzvot, and every person fulfills all of them equally at every moment.” / “The number 613 is a beautiful symbol; there is no need to count the mitzvot and divide them.”
- [ ] **`know-mikveh` What is a mikveh, and why does a ger immerse in it?**
  - What they check: A check of understanding of the rite of giyur itself.
  - Key points: A mikveh is a pool of “living” water. / The ger’s immersion (tevilah) is the completion of giyur in the presence of the witnesses of the beit din. / Before the immersion one accepts the mitzvot and says a berachah.
  - Common mistakes: Comparing the immersion with the rites of other religions.
  - Movements: For Orthodox and, as a rule, Conservative giyur, tevilah is obligatory. In ROOPI and in Israeli Progressive giyur it is also part of the path; in some North American communities the rabbi may require it, strongly recommend it, or leave it as a strong custom. Explain the meaning of the immersion, and not only “needed / not needed.”
  - Lessons: `beit-din-4`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “A mikveh is a pool of ‘living’ water; tevilah is obligatory and completes giyur in the presence of the witnesses of the beit din.” / incorrect: “A bathtub at home is enough; a beit din is not needed.” / “A man does not need a mikveh.” / “A mikveh is the washing of the hands before prayer.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “For Conservative giyur, tevilah is, as a rule, obligatory: a mikveh of ‘living’ water, immersion before witnesses.” / incorrect: “In Conservative giyur a mikveh is not needed.” / “A bathtub at home is enough.” / “A mikveh is the washing of the hands.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know the meaning of the immersion, and not only ‘needed / not needed’: in ROOPI and in Israeli Progressive giyur, tevilah is part of the path; in some North American communities the rabbi may require it, strongly recommend it, or leave it as a strong custom.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to know the mikveh; there is no immersion on this path.” / “A bathtub at home is enough; the meaning of ‘living’ water does not matter.” / “Immersion is only a women’s rite; a man does not need it in any school.”
- [ ] **`know-milah` What does a man need for giyur besides the mikveh?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge for male candidates.
  - Key points: Brit milah is circumcision; if he is already circumcised — hatafat dam brit (a drop of the blood of the covenant). / Then immersion in the mikveh. / Kabbalat mitzvot before the beit din.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that a medical circumcision replaces everything.
  - Movements: For Orthodox and Conservative giyur a man needs brit milah or hatafat dam brit. In ROOPI brit milah is obligatory; in Israeli Progressive giyur it usually is as well; in North America it is often expected, although the CCAR resolution of 1892 formally left this to the rabbi’s discretion.
  - Lessons: `beit-din-5`, `semya-1`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “For Orthodox giyur a man needs brit milah or hatafat dam brit, then the mikveh and kabbalat mitzvot.” / incorrect: “A medical circumcision is enough; no other steps are needed.” / “Circumcision is not required for giyur.” / “Brit milah can be postponed for years after giyur.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “For Conservative giyur a man needs brit milah or hatafat dam brit, then immersion.” / incorrect: “In Conservative giyur circumcision is not needed.” / “A medical circumcision is always enough, without hatafat dam brit.” / “This can be postponed for years.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I know how the schools differ: in ROOPI brit milah is obligatory; in Israeli Progressive giyur it usually is as well; in North America it is often expected, although the CCAR resolution of 1892 formally left this to the rabbi’s discretion. I answer according to the rules of my own school.” / incorrect: “A Reform Jew has no need to know about brit milah; on this path it does not occur.” / “A medical circumcision is always enough, and here the schools are the same.” / “I will say that brit milah is nowhere needed in Progressive giyur.”
- [ ] **`know-movements` What movements are there in Judaism, and why did you choose this beit din?**
  - What they check: The judges check whether you understand which community you are joining, and that recognition of giyur differs.
  - Key points: The Orthodox, Conservative (Masorti), and Reform movements. / How they differ in their relation to halacha. / Why you chose this particular movement and beit din; whether you know about the recognition of giyur in Israel.
  - Common mistakes: Criticizing the other movements. / Not knowing which movement your beit din belongs to.
  - Movements: Name the three main movements and explain why you chose this particular beit din — in the language of that movement, and not against the others. For the Reform path: covenant and informed choice, equality in the community, tikkun olam, the clear rules of your school of giyur, and an understanding of how others recognize this giyur.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-7`, `beit-din-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “I will name the three main movements and explain why I chose this particular Orthodox beit din — in the language of this path, and not against the others; I understand how such a giyur is recognized.” / incorrect: “All the movements are the same; one can choose at random.” / “One must condemn the other movements in order to emphasize one’s own choice.” / “I do not know which movement my beit din belongs to.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “I will name the three movements and explain why I chose a Conservative beit din: the relation to halacha, the community, recognition — without criticizing others’ paths.” / incorrect: “All the movements are the same.” / “One must condemn the other paths.” / “I do not know which movement my beit din belongs to.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will name the three movements and explain my Reform choice in the language of this path: covenant and informed choice, equality in the community, tikkun olam, the rules of my school of giyur, and an understanding of how others recognize this giyur — not against the other paths.” / incorrect: “All the movements are the same; the school of giyur does not matter.” / “I will choose Reform because ‘it is easier here,’ and I will condemn the Orthodox.” / “I do not know the rules of my school of giyur, and I do not know whether others recognize this giyur.”
- [ ] **`know-tanakh` What parts make up the Tanakh?**
  - What they check: A short check that you use Jewish concepts.
  - Key points: Torah (the Five Books), Nevi'im (the Prophets), Ketuvim (the Writings). / Tanakh is an abbreviation of these three words. / The five books of the Torah: Bereshit, Shemot, Vayikra, Bamidbar, Devarim.
  - Common mistakes: Using non-Jewish names for the parts of the Tanakh.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-2`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “The Tanakh is Torah (the Five Books), Nevi'im (the Prophets) and Ketuvim (the Writings); the word itself is an abbreviation of these three parts. The five books of the Torah: Bereshit, Shemot, Vayikra, Bamidbar, Devarim.” / incorrect: “The Tanakh is only the Five Books; there are no other parts.” / “The Tanakh includes the Five Books and a prayer book; the Prophets are not included.” / “It is better to call the parts of the Tanakh by the names customary outside the Jewish tradition, and not Torah, Nevi'im and Ketuvim.”
- [ ] **`know-mourning` What do you know about Jewish customs of mourning?**
  - What they check: A check of knowledge about the life cycle.
  - Key points: Burial as soon as possible, without cremation. / Shivah — seven days of mourning, Kaddish. / A ger continues to respect and mourn his non-Jewish parents.
  - Common mistakes: Thinking that a ger no longer grieves for his parents.
  - Lessons: `semya-7`, `beit-din-6`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review): correct: “Burial as soon as possible, without cremation; shivah — seven days of mourning, Kaddish. A ger still respects and mourns his non-Jewish parents.” / incorrect: “After giyur a ger no longer grieves for non-Jewish parents.” / “Cremation is the accepted practice in Judaism; there is no shivah.” / “Mourning lasts one day; Kaddish is not recited.”
- [ ] **`know-patrilineal` How do the different movements define a child’s Jewishness? What did the Reform movement decide in 1983, and why does a ger need to know this?**
  - What they check: This is a check of maturity, not an invitation to a dispute. The candidate should calmly distinguish the maternal line from the CCAR resolution of 1983 and understand how this will affect children in different communities.
  - Key points: Orthodox and Conservative (Masorti) Judaism proceed from the mother’s Jewishness (or from giyur). / The CCAR resolution of 1983: a child of one Jewish parent — mother or father — is a Jew if raised as a Jew. / A ger needs to know this for clarity about the status of children and about recognition in other communities, not for polemic.
  - Common mistakes: Confusing 1983 with the 1892 ruling on the mikveh. / Claiming that “all Jews think so.” / Speaking of this dismissively toward the other movements or toward one’s own.
  - Movements: The subject is especially important if you are joining a Progressive community, or if the family already has children. In an Orthodox and in a Conservative beit din the answer about a child’s status will be different — speak according to the norm of your own court.
  - Lessons: `osnovy-1`, `osnovy-7`, `semya-2`, `beit-din-7`
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Orthodox path: correct: “In an Orthodox beit din Jewish status follows the mother; if a child is not recognized as a Jew, that child’s path is a separate giyur. I speak about this honestly.” / incorrect: “In all the movements a Jew’s child is always a Jew, equally through the father or through the mother.” / “I will hide that there are children in the family.” / “The judges have no need to know the children’s status.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Conservative path: correct: “In a Conservative beit din a child’s status is determined differently than in some Progressive communities; I will speak honestly about the children and ask which path they need.” / incorrect: “In all the movements the rules are the same.” / “I will hide that there are children in the family.” / “This can be decided later, and not told to the judges.”
  - Answer options (new text, needs review), Reform path: correct: “I will explain the rule of my own school: in some Progressive communities, when the conditions are met, the paternal line is also recognized, and I will say honestly what this means for my children; in an Orthodox and in a Conservative beit din the answer would be different.” / incorrect: “In all the movements, patrilineal descent is accepted in the same way.” / “I will hide that there are children in the family.” / “I do not know the rules of my own school, and there is no need to clarify them.”
<!-- interview:end -->

## General questions

Questions that apply to all the sections at once, and not to a single file.

- [ ] Positions of the movements: whether each movement is described fairly — according to its own self-description. In particular, Masorti in Israel does not follow the American ruling on driving on Shabbat (sections 1 and 3); the Conservative and Reform positions are in many places given in general terms and rest on specific documents (CJLS, CCAR, “The Sacred Table”, platforms), which are worth cross-checking.
- [ ] Customs of Ashkenazim and Sephardim: blocks about differing minhagim are gathered across the various sections — the tallit gadol, the wrapping of the tefillin, the number of blessings over the tefillin, the count of the tzitzit, the slant of the mezuzah (section 2); the order of the blessing over the candles, the number of pourings at the washing, the blessings over the spices of havdalah (section 3); bishul akum, fish with dairy, the customs of the Nine Days (sections 4–5). Check not only the accuracy of each, but also the uniformity of the presentation: whether it is clear to the reader everywhere that the subject is customs that depend on the community.
- [ ] Transliteration: the transliteration is done purely in Cyrillic (“х” instead of “h”: “Ве-ахавта”, “манхиг”); in section 2 the Hebrew texts are given according to the Ashkenazi siddur (הַגָּפֶן, נִהְיָה), with the Sephardi forms mentioned in the text — make sure that this choice is consistent and clear to a Sephardi student.
- [ ] Writing the Name of the Almighty: everywhere the designation ה׳ is used, with the transliteration “Adonai” (section 2; the principle is stated as a general one). Confirm that this way of writing is permissible, or indicate another accepted variant.
- [ ] Recognition of giyurim: the topic is raised in sections 1 and 8 (the Rabbinate and its requirements, the Law of Return of 1970 with the amendment, the decisions of the Supreme Court of Israel, including the 2021 decision; the acceptance of Reform giyur by Conservative communities where there is milah and tevilah; the CCAR resolution of 1892 and the current practice of Reform communities). Check that the formulations are up to date as of the app’s release date.

## Three-dimensional models (3D objects)

The texts of the cards with the rotating models (file `src/ui/model3d/mod.rs`, function `info`). The “introductory text” is always visible; the “caption” appears when the model is clicked. The models were generated (Tripo AI) and simplified; the lettering on the scroll is schematic.

- [ ] **Kiddush cup** (the “Lessons” screen as “Item of the Day”, section “Shabbat”). Introductory text: “Kiddush is the sanctification of Shabbat and the festivals: the blessing is recited over a cup of wine at the beginning of the meal.” Caption: “A silver cup with an embossed pattern. For kiddush it is filled with wine or grape juice; after the blessing, the one who recited the kiddush drinks, and shares with those seated at the table.”
- [ ] **Shabbat candles** (section “Shabbat”). Introductory text: “Before the onset of Shabbat, before sunset, candles are lit in the home — no fewer than two.” Caption: “The two candles recall the two words of the commandment about Shabbat: ‘Remember’ (zachor) and ‘Keep’ (shamor). Usually the woman of the house lights the candles; she has lit them — and for her Shabbat has already begun.” Check: the formulation about accepting Shabbat with the lighting (customs differ).
- [ ] **Temple menorah** (section “Festivals”, Chanukah context). Introductory text: “A seven-branched candelabrum of pure gold, which was lit in the Temple. The miracle of Chanukah is connected with it.” Caption: “This is the menorah of the Temple — seven lamps, and not a chanukiah (a chanukiah has eight lamps and a shamash). When the Hasmoneans purified the Temple, one jug of pure oil for a day was found, and the menorah burned for eight days. That is why Chanukah lasts eight days. By custom, an exact copy of the seven-branched menorah is not made for the home.” Check: whether a 3D model of the seven-branched candelabrum is appropriate in the app, given the prohibition on making an exact copy (Rosh Hashanah 24a; Shulchan Aruch, Yoreh De'ah 141:8), and the formulation “by custom”.
- [ ] **Torah scroll** (section “Foundations”). Introductory text: “A sefer Torah is the Five Books of the Torah, written by hand by a scribe (sofer) on parchment.” Caption: “The scroll is written with a goose quill on parchment made from the skin of a kosher animal, without a single error. It is read in the synagogue on Shabbat, on Mondays and Thursdays, on the festivals and on Rosh Chodesh. The letters on this model are schematic — this is not a real text.” Check: whether it is permissible to show a model of a scroll with schematic “letters” (the approximation is limited; the letters cannot be read); “with a goose quill” — it is also written with a reed kalam.
- [ ] **Shofar** (section “Festivals”, Rosh Hashanah; also in “Item of the Day”) — a Tripo model: a curved ram’s horn. Introductory: “A horn that is blown on Rosh Hashanah and at the end of Yom Kippur.” Caption: “A shofar is made from the horn of a kosher animal, best of all a ram, in memory of the ram that was offered in place of Yitzchak. The sounds of the shofar — tekiah, shevarim and teruah — awaken the heart to teshuvah.” Check: “of a kosher animal” (except a cow).
- [ ] **Second Temple** (section “Foundations”, also in “Festivals” beside Tisha B'Av — with the label “Festivals · Tisha B'Av” — and in “Item of the Day”) — a Tripo model: the whole Temple complex, an artistic reconstruction (based on the model in the Israel Museum). Introductory: “The Temple in Jerusalem on the Temple Mount. King Herod rebuilt it, and the Romans destroyed it in the year 70 (the year 3830 by the Jewish reckoning), on 9 Av — on Tisha B'Av.” Caption: “At the center is the Sanctuary (Heichal), where the Holy of Holies was located. Before it is the Court of the Priests with the altar, and beyond it the Court of Israel and the Court of the Women. All of this stood on the Temple Mount, surrounded by walls. This is an artistic reconstruction: the details are approximate.” Check: is the date of the destruction correct — the year 70 by the common reckoning = 3830 by the Jewish reckoning, 9 Av (Tisha B'Av)? is the formulation “King Herod rebuilt it” precise — does it attribute to him the building of the First Temple? is the order of the courts correct — the Court of the Priests with the altar before the Court of Israel and the Court of the Women? is a 3D image of the Temple appropriate, given the custom of not reproducing its exact appearance — is the disclaimer “artistic reconstruction” sufficient? is the formulation “the Holy of Holies” correct, and its location in the Sanctuary (Heichal)? The altar in the Court of the Priests is enlarged for clarity (schematically: a base, four horns at the corners, a ramp on the south side) — is such a schematic depiction appropriate? is it appropriate to show the Temple in “Festivals” beside Tisha B'Av?

- [ ] **Magen David** (section “Foundations”, also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “The Magen David is a six-pointed star, a generally accepted sign of the Jewish people.” Caption: “As a generally accepted Jewish sign, the star became established relatively recently, in the modern era; today its image stands on the flag of the State of Israel. This is not a mitzvah of the Torah and not a ritual object: this sign has no source in the Torah or in the Temple service. ‘Magen David’ is the established name of the sign; there is no reliable information that this was the personal shield of King David.” Check: is the formulation about the modern era and the flag of Israel accurate, and does it overstate the antiquity of the sign? is it appropriate to say outright that this is not a mitzvah of the Torah and that the sign has no source in the Torah or in the Temple service? is the remark correct that a connection with the personal shield of King David cannot be proven?
- [ ] **Chanukiah** (section “Festivals”, Chanukah context; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A chanukiah is the lamp of Chanukah: eight lights and a shamash, with which they are lit.” Caption: “A chanukiah has eight lamps and also a shamash — a service light, with which the others are lit. This is not the seven-branched menorah of the Temple. It is customary to add one light each evening: on the first evening one is lit, on the eighth — all eight.” Check: the name “chanukiah” (not “menorah”), the role of the shamash, the formulation “it is customary to add each evening” (the opinion of Beit Hillel).
- [ ] **Pesach plate** (section “Festivals”, Pesach context; also in “Item of the Day”). The name “Pesach plate” is already used in the lessons of the section. Introductory text: “The ke'arah is a special plate for the seder, the meal of Pesach, on which the symbols of the festival are placed.” Caption: “On the ke'arah are placed the zeroa — a roasted bone, the beitzah — an egg, maror — bitter herbs, charoset — a mixture of fruit, nuts and wine, and karpas — a vegetable that is dipped in salt water; chazeret is often added as well. Three matzot are also taken for the seder. Each item recalls the Exodus from Egypt. The arrangement of the items differs among communities, so on the model it is schematic.” Check: the composition of the set and the Russian renderings of the names (in the lessons — зроа, кеара, харосет); that the three matzot are named as part of the seder, without an exact place; that the arrangement is not presented as the only correct one. On the model the matzot lie in a stack in the form of a half-disk, the central depression is empty, and under the matzot is a napkin (a napkin above or under the matzot is found in custom); the caption does not describe this, and it calls the arrangement schematic.
- [ ] **Lulav and etrog** (section “Festivals”, Sukkot context; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “The four species of plants — arba'at ha-minim — are taken in the hands on the festival of Sukkot: lulav, hadas, aravah and etrog.” Caption: “The four species include the lulav — an unopened palm branch, hadas — myrtle branches, aravah — willow branches, and the etrog — the fruit of the citron. The lulav, hadas and aravah are usually bound into one bundle, and the etrog is held separately. All four species are taken together and shaken on Sukkot.” Check: the names and translations of the four species (including “an unopened palm branch”); the description of the bundle (three plants are bound, the etrog separately); the formulation “taken together and shaken on Sukkot” (on Shabbat of Sukkot, in some communities the lulav is not taken).
- [ ] **Megillat Esther** (section “Festivals”, Purim context; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “Megillat Esther is the scroll with the story of Purim, one of the books of the Tanakh; it is read on the festival in the evening and in the morning.” Caption: “The Book of Esther tells how Esther and Mordechai saved the Jews from Haman. On Purim the scroll is read twice: in the evening and on the following morning. The letters on this model are schematic — this is not a real text.” Check: that the formulations do not confuse the Megillah (a book of the Tanakh, the Ketuvim) with the Five Books of the Torah; the two readings — in the evening and in the morning; whether it is permissible to show a model of a scroll with schematic letters.
- [ ] **Sukkah** (section “Festivals”, Sukkot context; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A sukkah is a temporary structure under the open sky; the principal mitzvah of Sukkot is to live in it.” Caption: “‘To live’ in the sukkah means first of all to eat there, especially bread and foods made with flour, and also to spend time there and — for one who is able, and in whose community this is the custom — to sleep. The sukkah teaches humility and trust: a person leaves a solid house for a thin roof and remembers that he is protected not by stone, but by the Almighty. Before eating, the blessing ‘leishev ba-sukkah’ is recited. The decorations and the construction on this model are schematic.” Check: the formulation “to live” (food, time, sleep according to communal custom); the blessing “leishev ba-sukkah”; that the decorations and the construction are called schematic.
- [ ] **Tallit** (section “Hebrew and Prayer”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A tallit (tallit gadol) is a large shawl with tzitzit at the four corners, which is put on during the morning prayer.” Caption: “Tzitzit are the fringes on the four corners of a garment: ‘and you shall see them, and remember all the commandments of the L-rd, and fulfill them’ (Bamidbar 15:39). The tallit gadol is put on at Shacharit; the tallit katan is worn all day under the clothes. On this model is a large tallit on a stand; the knots and windings of the tzitzit are schematic.” Check: the distinction between tallit gadol and tallit katan; the verse Bamidbar 15:39; that the knots and windings are called schematic.
- [ ] **Mezuzah** (section “Hebrew and Prayer”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A mezuzah is a parchment with the first two passages of the Shema, rolled in a case and affixed to the doorpost.” Caption: “The mitzvah is taken from the Shema: ‘And write them on the doorposts of your house and on your gates’ (Devarim 6:9). A sofer writes on the parchment (klaf) two passages: Devarim 6:4–9 and 11:13–21. The case is affixed to the right doorpost as one enters: among Ashkenazim at a slant, with the upper end toward the inside of the room; among Sephardim, vertically. On this model is a case on a segment of doorpost; the parchment itself is not visible.” Check: the contents of the text on the klaf (the first two passages of the Shema); the slant among Ashkenazim and the vertical position among Sephardim; that the model shows the case, and not the parchment itself.

- [ ] **Siddur** (section “Hebrew and Prayer”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A siddur is a prayer book for weekdays and Shabbat; the word itself means ‘order’.” Caption: “The siddur contains the established order of the daily and Shabbat prayers. For Rosh Hashanah, Yom Kippur and the pilgrimage festivals, a special prayer book is used — the machzor. Different communities have their own nusachim — recensions of the text and of the order. On this model is an open siddur; the letters on the pages are schematic, this is not a real prayer text.” Check: the distinction between the siddur and the machzor; the word “order”; that the nusachim are called recensions, without tying the model to a specific edition; that the letters on the pages are called schematic.
- [ ] **Tefillin** (section “Hebrew and Prayer”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “Tefillin are two black leather boxes with straps; inside are four passages of the Torah.” Caption: “The mitzvah is taken from the Shema: ‘and bind them as a sign on your hand, and they shall be a band between your eyes’ (Devarim 6:8). The tefillin shel yad are placed on the biceps of the weaker arm, opposite the heart; the tefillin shel rosh — on the head, above the hairline. They are put on on weekdays for the morning prayer; on Shabbat and on festivals they are not put on — these days are themselves a ‘sign’. On this model is the head tefillin (shel rosh): a square box (bayit) on a wider base (titura), with a passage for the strap (ma'abarta) at the back. On the right side is a raised letter shin with three heads, on the left a shin with four, as accepted in the law. The straps (retzuot) come out of the ma'abarta, form a loop and hang down. The knot on the loop is schematic; this is not the exact form of the shel rosh knot.” Check: the verse Devarim 6:8; the distinction between shel yad and shel rosh; that on Shabbat and festivals tefillin are not put on; that the model is called shel rosh; the sides of the shin (three heads on the right, four on the left, as for the wearer); that the knot is called schematic.

- [ ] **Kotel** (section “Festivals”, Tisha B'Av context; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “The Kotel HaMa'aravi is the Western Wall of the Temple Mount, a place of prayer at the surviving wall of the Second Temple.” Caption: “This is the retaining wall of the Temple Mount from the time of the Second Temple; after the destruction of the Temple in the year 70 (the year 3830 by the Jewish reckoning) it became the principal place of prayer at the Mount. It is customary to place notes with requests in the cracks of the wall. The destruction of the Temple is remembered on the fast of Tisha B'Av. On this model is a small fragment of the masonry with plants in the joints; this is an artistic approximation, and not an exact view of the wall, and there are no notes on the model.” Check: the naming (Kotel / Western Wall); the connection with the Second Temple and Tisha B'Av; that the model is called a fragment, without notes.
- [ ] **Chuppah** (section “Life Cycle and Family”, the lesson on kiddushin and the chuppah; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A chuppah is the wedding canopy under which the ceremony of a Jewish marriage takes place.” Caption: “Under the chuppah, kiddushin and nisuin are performed: the blessings over the wine, the ring, the ketubah. Often the cloth of the chuppah is a tallit or a white fabric on four poles; under it stand the bridegroom and the bride. On this model is a schematic chuppah (cloth with blue stripes, wooden poles, a small table with a cup); the cloth is thin, the fringes are uneven, and the construction does not lay claim to an exact ritual appearance.” Check: kiddushin and nisuin; that the chuppah may be a tallit; that the model is explicitly called schematic.

- [ ] **Ketubah** (section “Life Cycle and Family”, the lesson on kiddushin and the chuppah; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A ketubah is the marriage contract, which is read and given to the bride under the chuppah.” Caption: “The ketubah sets down the husband’s obligations to his wife: maintenance, clothing and conjugal rights, and also the sum due to the wife in the event of divorce or the husband’s death. It is written before the chuppah, signed by witnesses, read under the chuppah and given to the bride. The text and the decorations on this model are schematic: the letters do not make up a real ketubah; this is not a valid document.” Check: the three obligations and the ketubah sum; that it is read under the chuppah and given to the bride; that the model is explicitly called schematic, not a real document.
- [ ] **Meat and dairy utensils** (section “Kashrut”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “In kashrut, meat (basar) and dairy (chalav) are not mixed, and separate utensils are kept for them.” Caption: “The Torah forbids boiling a kid in its mother’s milk; from this comes the law not to eat meat with dairy and not to cook them together. A kitchen therefore usually has two sets of utensils: one for meat, the other for milk. The colors on this model are a schematic teaching sign: the red set (plate, bowl, fork) denotes meat, the blue (plate, bowl, spoon) denotes dairy. Utensils are sometimes marked this way for clarity, but the color itself is not the law. The sets stand on one board only for display; in a home they are kept separately.” Check: the formulation of the prohibition and of the separate utensils; that red/blue are called a teaching sign, and not the law; that their standing together on the board is stated to be for display.
- [ ] **Wedding cup** (section “Life Cycle and Family”, the lesson on kiddushin and the chuppah; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “Under the chuppah the bridegroom breaks a cup in memory of the destroyed Temple.” Caption: “Even on the most joyful day, the destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem is remembered. The bridegroom steps on the cup — it is usually wrapped in a napkin or placed in a cloth — and breaks it; the guests answer ‘mazal tov’. On this model is a cup on a wooden tray beside a folded napkin. The glass is rendered schematically: a shiny pale-blue tint, without real transparency.” Check: the connection of the custom with the memory of the Temple; that the cup is wrapped and broken; the response “mazal tov”; that the glass on the model is called schematic, without real transparency.
- [ ] **Kosher fish** (section “Kashrut”, the lesson “Permitted animals, birds and fish”; also in “Item of the Day”). Introductory text: “A fish is kosher if it has fins and scales that can be removed without damaging the skin.” Caption: “The sign of a kosher fish is given in the Torah: it must have fins and scales (Vayikra 11:9). In practice the scales are checked: if they come off without damaging the skin, the fish is kosher; the Mishnah (Niddah 6:9) teaches that every fish with scales also has fins. The details are in the Shulchan Aruch, Yoreh De'ah 83. Carp is a common example of such a fish. A fish does not require shechita, and it is pareve: it is eaten both with meat and with dairy. On this model is a carp on a board; scales removed beside it show that the scales come off. Some scales at the lower edge are slightly thick or stuck together — this is a schematic feature of the model, and not the exact look of fresh scales.” Check: the verse Vayikra 11:9; the rule of fins and of scales that come off; the reference to Niddah 6:9 and Yoreh De'ah 83; that carp is called a common example; that a fish does not require shechita and is pareve; that the scales at the edge are called schematic.
