- Thu: “And the L-rd was with Yosef.” In slavery, far from home, Yosef keeps his faith — and even those around him see it.
- Fri: Yosef resists temptation with the words, “How can I do this great evil and sin before G-d?” The strength to stand firm comes from remembering that the Almighty sees everything.
- Shabbat: The cupbearer forgets Yosef. People may forget kindness, but G-d does not forget — and deliverance will come at the right time.
- [ ] checked

#### `miketz` (Bereshit 41:1–44:17)
- **In brief:** Pharaoh dreams of seven fat and seven lean cows and ears of grain, and Yosef is brought out of prison to interpret the dreams. Yosef foretells seven years of plenty and seven years of famine and becomes ruler of Egypt. During the famine his brothers come to buy grain; Yosef recognizes them, but they do not recognize him. He tests them, demands that they bring Binyamin, and in the end has his goblet placed in Binyamin's sack.
- **Theme:** G-d can turn darkness into light in a single day, and trials open a person's path to teshuvah.
- Sun: Yosef goes from prison to Pharaoh in a single day. Deliverance from G-d can come suddenly; this parashah is almost always read on the Shabbat of Chanukah — the festival of light in the darkness.
- Mon: “It is not I — G-d will give Pharaoh an answer for his welfare.” Yosef does not take credit for his abilities: everything we are able to do is a gift from the Almighty.
- Tue: Yosef advises storing grain in the years of plenty. Wisdom means thinking about the future while there is still the chance.
- Wed: Yosef names his sons Menasheh — “G-d has made me forget my suffering” — and Efraim — “G-d has made me fruitful in the land of my affliction.” You can grow even in a foreign land without forgetting who you are.
- Thu: The brothers say to one another, “We are guilty concerning our brother.” Teshuvah begins with admitting your mistake, even after many years.
- Fri: Yaakov lets Binyamin go with the words, “May G-d Almighty grant you mercy.” When we send our loved ones on their way, we bless them.
- Shabbat: The goblet in Binyamin's sack is a test: will the brothers abandon their brother in trouble, as they once did Yosef? True teshuvah is proven when a person finds himself in the same situation and acts differently.
- [ ] checked

#### `vayigash` (Bereshit 44:18–47:27)
- **In brief:** Yehudah approaches Yosef and offers himself as a slave in place of Binyamin. Unable to hold back, Yosef reveals himself to his brothers and forgives them, seeing G-d's plan in everything. Yaakov learns that Yosef is alive, and the whole family — seventy souls — moves to Egypt, to the land of Goshen. Yosef governs Egypt during the famine, and the children of Israel settle in and multiply.
- **Theme:** Responsibility for one's brother, repentance and forgiveness restore a broken family.
- Sun: Yehudah says, “Let me remain a slave in place of the boy.” The one who once suggested selling his brother is now ready to give up his freedom for his brother — this is complete teshuvah.
- Mon: “I am Yosef. Is my father still alive?” Even at the height of power, Yosef thinks first of all about his father.
- Tue: “It was not you who sent me here, but G-d.” Forgiveness comes more easily when you see that the Almighty is guiding events toward good.
- Wed: Yosef instructs his brothers, “Do not quarrel on the way.” Peace between people must be guarded especially when old grievances are still fresh.
- Thu: On hearing that Yosef was alive, “the spirit of Yaakov revived.” Good news can restore a person's strength; it is worth being the one who brings it.
- Fri: Yaakov sends Yehudah ahead to prepare everything. According to the Midrash, he founded a house of study in Goshen: before setting up life in a new place, you set up a place for Torah.
- Shabbat: “And Israel settled in Egypt, in the land of Goshen.” This marks the beginning of the first exile, and with it the story of how the nation was formed.
- [ ] checked

#### `vayechi` (Bereshit 47:28–50:26)
- **In brief:** Before his death, Yaakov asks Yosef to bury him in the land of Canaan, in the Cave of Machpelah. He blesses Yosef's sons, Efraim and Menasheh, and then each of his own twelve sons. After Yaakov's death he is mourned and buried in Hebron. The brothers fear Yosef's revenge, but he reassures them; before his death Yosef asks that his bones be carried out of Egypt when G-d brings the people out. Thus ends the book of Bereshit.
- **Theme:** The forefathers pass on to their children a blessing and the hope of returning to the Land of Israel.
- Sun: Yaakov asks Yosef to do him “kindness and truth” — to bury him. Rashi explains: caring for the dead is true kindness, because it can never be repaid.
- Mon: “By you shall Israel bless, saying: May G-d make you like Efraim and Menasheh.” With these words Jewish parents bless their sons on Shabbat eve.
- Tue: Yaakov blesses each son “according to his blessing” — each with his own. Every Jew has his own path and his own contribution to the service of G-d.
- Wed: Yehudah is promised, “The scepter shall not depart from Yehudah.” From him will come the kings of Israel, beginning with David, and, according to tradition, Mashiach.
- Thu: Yosef mourns his father for seven days. Tradition sees in this one of the sources of the seven days of mourning — shivah.
- Fri: The brothers fear revenge, but Yosef answers, “You intended evil against me; G-d turned it to good.” Forgiveness also frees the one who forgives.
- Shabbat: Before his death Yosef says, “G-d will surely remember you and bring you up out of this land.” The book ends with hope, and in the synagogue everyone proclaims, “Chazak, chazak venitchazek” — “Be strong, be strong, and let us strengthen one another.”
- [ ] checked

#### `shemot` (Shemot 1:1–6:1)
- **In brief:** Yaakov's descendants multiply in Egypt, and a new Pharaoh enslaves them. He orders newborn boys to be killed, but the midwives Shifrah and Puah refuse. Moshe is rescued from the river, grows up in the palace, flees to Midian and there, at the burning bush, receives from G-d the mission of bringing the people out. Moshe and Aharon come to Pharaoh, but he refuses and makes the work even harder.
- **Theme:** Redemption begins when a person sees someone else's pain and does not turn away.
- Sun: The midwives Shifrah and Puah did not obey Pharaoh's order, because they feared G-d. Sometimes loyalty to the Almighty means quietly saying “no” to injustice.
- Mon: Moshe's mother, his sister Miriam and Pharaoh's daughter saved one child. The future of a people often rests on the courage of a few individuals.
- Tue: Moshe grew up in the palace, but went out “to his brothers” and saw their suffering. Belonging to a people begins with caring.
- Wed: The bush burned yet was not consumed, and Moshe turned aside to look. The willingness to stop and reflect is the first step toward an encounter with the Almighty.
- Thu: Moshe doubted that he would be listened to and said that he was “heavy of mouth.” G-d chooses not the most self-assured, but those who are ready to go despite their doubts.
- Fri: Aharon went out to meet his younger brother and rejoiced in him without envy. Rejoicing in another's success is a great and rare strength.
- Shabbat: After the first meeting with Pharaoh things only got worse, and Moshe asked G-d, “Why?” The Torah does not forbid an honest question — it, too, can be part of faith.
- [ ] checked

#### `vaera` (Shemot 6:2–9:35)
- **In brief:** G-d promises redemption in four expressions: I will bring out, I will deliver, I will redeem, I will take. The people do not listen to Moshe because of hard labor and exhaustion. The families of the tribes of Reuven, Shimon and Levi, from whom Moshe and Aharon descend, are listed. The plagues of Egypt begin: blood, frogs, lice, wild beasts, pestilence of livestock, boils and hail, but Pharaoh hardens his heart again and again.
- **Theme:** The Almighty is the Master of the world and of history, and He is faithful to the promise He gave the forefathers.
- Sun: The four expressions of redemption underlie the four cups of wine at the Pesach Seder. Freedom comes step by step.
- Mon: The Torah interrupts the narrative to list the families. Every person enters history through his family and his people, and a ger, too, becomes part of this lineage.
- Tue: The people “did not listen, out of shortness of spirit and hard labor.” When a person is worn out, it is hard for him to hear even good news — let us be patient with others and with ourselves.
- Wed: The first plague struck the Nile, which the Egyptians worshipped. The Torah teaches that nothing in nature is a deity: everything was created by the One G-d.
- Thu: The Egyptian magicians could not reproduce the plague of lice and admitted, “This is the finger of G-d.” Admitting the truth is sometimes harder than seeing it.
- Fri: During the plague of wild beasts, G-d set apart the land of Goshen, where the Jews lived. The Almighty distinguishes between people, even when disaster comes upon a whole country.
- Shabbat: During the hail, some Egyptians who feared the word of G-d sheltered their servants and livestock. Even in a foreign environment, everyone has the choice to do what is right.
- [ ] checked

#### `bo` (Shemot 10:1–13:16)
- **In brief:** The last plagues come: locusts, darkness and the death of the firstborn. The people receive their first mitzvah — to sanctify the new month and keep their own calendar. The laws of the Pesach offering, matzah and the prohibition of chametz are given, as well as the mitzvah to tell one's children about the Exodus. The people of Israel leave Egypt, and the Torah speaks of the sanctity of the firstborn and of tefillin.
- **Theme:** Freedom becomes real when it is filled with mitzvot and passed on to one's children.
- Sun: Moshe told Pharaoh, “We will go with our young and with our old.” In Jewish service of G-d the whole family takes part, all the generations together.
- Mon: During the darkness, the Egyptians could not see one another. The Sages teach: the heaviest darkness is when a person does not notice his fellow.
- Tue: “This month shall be for you the beginning of months.” The first mitzvah given to the people is to take charge of their time; this is where the Jewish calendar begins.
- Wed: The Torah anticipates a child's question: “What is this service to you?” Children's questions are not a hindrance but the main way the tradition is passed on.
- Thu: “There shall be one law for the native-born and for the ger.” One who has joined the people is fully equal in the mitzvot and in the right to celebrate the festival.
- Fri: G-d sanctified the firstborn of Israel, whom He spared in Egypt. Gratitude for being saved becomes a lasting obligation.
- Shabbat: The account of the Exodus is linked to the mitzvah of tefillin — “a sign on your hand and a remembrance between your eyes.” The memory of freedom should be present both in our thoughts and in our deeds.
- [ ] checked

#### `beshalach` (Shemot 13:17–17:16)
- **In brief:** The people leave Egypt, and Moshe carries the bones of Yosef with him. Pharaoh gives chase, the sea splits, the Jews cross on dry land, and the Egyptians drown. Moshe and Miriam lead the people in a song of thanksgiving. In the wilderness the people complain about water and food, receive the manna and the mitzvah of Shabbat, and finally repel the attack of Amalek.
- **Theme:** Faith means moving forward and trusting the Almighty, even when the way is not yet visible.
- Sun: In the haste of the Exodus, Moshe did not forget the oath — he took Yosef's bones. A promise made generations ago must also be kept.
- Mon: The people were frightened by the pursuit, and Moshe said, “Stand firm and see the salvation of G-d.” Fear is natural, but it should not rob us of hope.
- Tue: G-d says to Moshe, “Why do you cry out to Me? Tell the children of Israel to move forward.” Sometimes prayer must be continued by action.
- Wed: After being saved, the people sang “Az Yashir.” This song is part of the daily morning prayer — we learn to give thanks every day.
- Thu: At Elim there were twelve springs and seventy palm trees. Even in the wilderness the Almighty provides places of rest — what matters is learning to notice them.
- Fri: The manna fell every day, and on Friday a double portion for Shabbat. From this comes the custom of placing two challot on the Shabbat table.
- Shabbat: When Moshe raised his hands, Israel prevailed over Amalek. The Sages explain: they prevailed when their hearts turned toward Heaven.
- [ ] checked

#### `yitro` (Shemot 18:1–20:23)
- **In brief:** Yitro, Moshe's father-in-law and a Midianite priest, hears of the miracles of the Exodus, comes to the camp of Israel and acknowledges G-d's greatness. He advises Moshe to appoint judges so as to share the burden with them. The people arrive at Mount Sinai, prepare for three days and hear the Ten Commandments. The parashah ends with laws about the altar.
- **Theme:** The Giving of the Torah at Sinai is a covenant between G-d and the whole people, open to everyone who comes.
- Sun: Yitro heard about the Exodus and came, leaving his former life behind. The Sages see in him the image of a ger: the parashah in which the Torah is given bears the name of a man who came from outside.
- Mon: Yitro saw that Moshe judged the people from morning until evening and said, “What you are doing is not good.” Wise advice can be accepted from anyone.
- Tue: Moshe listened to his father-in-law and appointed judges. A great leader is not afraid to share responsibility.
- Wed: G-d calls Israel “a kingdom of kohanim and a holy nation.” Being chosen is not superiority but a task: to serve and to sanctify life.
- Thu: The people prepared for three days to receive the Torah. Study and mitzvot require preparing the heart, not only acquiring knowledge.
- Fri: The Ten Commandments begin with the words “I am the L-rd your G-d, Who brought you out.” Faith in Judaism rests on relationship and memory, not only on an idea.
- Shabbat: The Torah forbids steps leading up to the altar, so as not to violate modesty. Even in lofty service, respect for human dignity is important.
- [ ] checked

#### `mishpatim` (Shemot 21:1–24:18)
- **In brief:** Right after Sinai, the Torah gives many laws of everyday life: about the Hebrew servant, damages, property, loans and courts. The mitzvot to protect the ger, the widow and the orphan stand out in particular. Shabbat, the sabbatical year and the three pilgrimage festivals are discussed. At the end the people enter into the covenant, saying, “We will do and we will hear,” and Moshe ascends the mountain for forty days.
- **Theme:** Holiness shows itself in honesty, justice and caring for the weak in ordinary life.
- Sun: The first laws after Sinai concern a servant's freedom after six years. A people that came out of slavery must remember the dignity of every human being.
- Mon: The Torah speaks in detail about responsibility for damages. How we treat other people's property and health is part of serving G-d.
- Tue: “Do not oppress the ger, for you were gerim in the land of Egypt.” The Torah repeats this mitzvah many times: one who has joined the people must be surrounded with love.
- Wed: If you see your enemy's ox gone astray, bring it back to him. A good deed matters more than a personal grudge.
- Thu: “Keep far from falsehood” and “do not take a bribe.” Truth in business and in court is the foundation of a society built on the Torah.
- Fri: G-d promises to send an angel to guard the people on the way. We are not alone — but we ourselves must also listen and walk the right path.
- Shabbat: The people said, “Na'aseh venishma — we will do and we will hear.” Sometimes understanding comes through doing, not before it.
- [ ] checked

#### `terumah` (Shemot 25:1–27:19)
- **In brief:** G-d tells Moshe to collect contributions from everyone whose heart moves him, in order to build the Mishkan — a portable sanctuary. The Aron with the Tablets, its cover with the keruvim, the table for the bread, the menorah, the coverings, the walls, the curtain and the altar are described in detail. The parashah ends with a description of the courtyard of the Mishkan.
- **Theme:** “Let them make Me a sanctuary, and I will dwell among them” — the Almighty wishes to dwell in a world that people sanctify.
- Sun: It says “dwell among them,” not “in it.” The Sages teach: the sanctuary is built first of all in the heart of each person.
- Mon: The Aron was overlaid with gold both outside and inside. A person of Torah must be the same inside and out.
- Tue: The menorah was hammered out of a single piece of gold. Light is born of unity, not of separate parts.
- Wed: The walls of the Mishkan were made of boards joined together. A community is strong when each person supports the other.
- Thu: The curtain set apart the Holy of Holies. In life, too, we need boundaries — they are what make the holy holy.
- Fri: The altar stood in the courtyard, accessible to the whole people. Anyone who comes with a sincere heart can draw near to G-d.
- Shabbat: The courtyard of the Mishkan was enclosed, yet open to enter. The synagogue today is a “small sanctuary,” where there is room for a newcomer too.
- [ ] checked

#### `tetzaveh` (Shemot 27:20–30:10)
- **In brief:** The people are commanded to bring pure olive oil for the constant light of the menorah. The garments of the kohen gadol and of the ordinary kohanim are described in detail: the ephod, the choshen with the stones of the tribes, the me'il with bells, and the golden plate inscribed “Holy to G-d.” The seven-day consecration of the kohanim, the daily tamid offering and the golden altar for incense are described. This is the only parashah from the beginning of the book of Shemot in which Moshe's name is not mentioned.
- **Theme:** Constant, daily service turns the ordinary into the holy.
- Sun: The light of the menorah must burn “constantly.” Consistency in small good deeds matters more than rare bursts of enthusiasm.
- Mon: The kohen gadol wore on his shoulders stones bearing the names of the tribes of Israel. A leader carries the people on himself, not above himself.
- Tue: The choshen with the names of the tribes lay “upon the heart” of Aharon. Serving G-d is inseparable from loving people.
- Wed: The bells on the me'il rang when the kohen entered the sanctuary. The Sages teach: even when entering your own home, announce yourself — this is respect for others.
- Thu: The consecration of the kohanim lasted seven days. Important changes in life require time and preparation, just like the path to giyur.
- Fri: The tamid offering was brought every morning and evening. According to the Sages, the daily prayers were instituted in place of these offerings.
- Shabbat: Moshe's name is not mentioned in this parashah. The Sages see in this his readiness to sacrifice himself for the people — greatness can go unnoticed.
- [ ] checked

#### `ki-tisa` (Shemot 30:11–34:35)
- **In brief:** Every man gives half a shekel for the census; the washbasin, the anointing oil and the craftsman Betzalel are described. The Torah again commands Shabbat as an eternal sign of the covenant. While Moshe is on the mountain, the people make the golden calf; Moshe breaks the Tablets, prays for the people and obtains forgiveness. G-d reveals His Thirteen Attributes of Mercy, Moshe receives the second Tablets and comes down with his face radiant.
- **Theme:** Even after a grave mistake, repentance, forgiveness and the renewal of the covenant are possible.
- Sun: Everyone gave exactly half a shekel, the poor and the rich alike. Every person is equally important, and wholeness comes only together with others.
- Mon: “The children of Israel shall keep the Shabbat… it is a sign forever.” Shabbat is the sign of the covenant, and even building the Mishkan does not override it.
- Tue: The people were afraid that Moshe would not return, and they made the calf. Confusion and impatience push us toward mistakes; it is important to know how to wait.
- Wed: Moshe asks: “Show me Your glory.” The longing to know the Almighty more closely is the path of a whole lifetime.
- Thu: G-d revealed the Thirteen Attributes of Mercy. They are recited in the prayers for forgiveness: the Almighty is “slow to anger and abundant in kindness.”
- Fri: After the calf, the Torah repeats the laws of the festivals. Returning to G-d means returning to the living rhythm of the mitzvot.
- Shabbat: The second tablets were given after repentance, and the Sages connect this with Yom Kippur. A new beginning is always possible.
- [ ] checked

#### `vayakhel` (Shemot 35:1–38:20)
- **In brief:** Moshe gathers the whole people and, first of all, reminds them about Shabbat. Then he calls on them to bring materials for the Mishkan, and the people bring with such generosity that they have to be stopped. Betzalel, Oholiav and all the wise-hearted make the coverings, the walls, the Ark, the table, the menorah, the altars and the laver.
- **Theme:** A community gathered together and giving from the heart can build a place for the Divine Presence.
- Sun: Before the building of the Mishkan, Moshe reminded the people about Shabbat. From the kinds of work done in building the Mishkan, the Sages derive the 39 categories of work forbidden on Shabbat.
- Mon: The gifts were brought by “everyone whose heart moved him.” What the Torah values is not the size of the gift but the sincerity of the giver.
- Tue: The people brought more than was needed, and Moshe stopped the collection. Generosity is beautiful, and an honest leader does not take more than is needed.
- Wed: The work was carried out by the “wise-hearted.” Talent is a gift from the Almighty, and it can be dedicated to a holy purpose.
- Thu: The Torah repeats the description of the Mishkan almost word for word. Carrying out a plan is just as important as the plan itself.
- Fri: Once again the menorah was hammered out of a single piece of gold. Beauty and unity are part of divine service.
- Shabbat: The laver was made from the copper mirrors of the women who kept up their families' hope in Egypt. What had served to care for the family became something holy.
- [ ] checked

#### `pekudei` (Shemot 38:21–40:38)
- **In brief:** Moshe gives a detailed account of all the gold, silver and copper collected for the Mishkan. The garments of the kohanim are made, and everything is done “as G-d commanded Moshe.” Moshe sees the finished work and blesses the people. The Mishkan is set up on the first day of the first month, the cloud covers it, and the glory of the Almighty fills the sanctuary.
- **Theme:** When work is done honestly and according to G-d's word, His presence fills what people have made.
- Sun: Moshe gave a full account of the donations. Even the most honest person must be transparent with public money.
- Mon: Every part of the garments of the kohanim was made “as G-d commanded.” The power of a mitzvah lies in faithfulness to both its form and its meaning.
- Tue: The Torah notes every detail of the work. No small detail in divine service is ever superfluous.
- Wed: Moshe saw the completed work and blessed the people. It is important to notice the labor of others and to thank them for it.
- Thu: Moshe was commanded to set up the Mishkan on the first day of the month. Rosh Chodesh is a time of new beginnings.
- Fri: Moshe himself put all the parts of the Mishkan in place. A leader does not stand aside but takes part in the work with his own hands.
- Shabbat: The cloud covered the Mishkan, and the book of Shemot ends with G-d's presence among the people. When a book of the Torah is completed, the synagogue says: “Chazak, chazak, venitchazek: be strong, be strong, and let us be strengthened!”
- [ ] checked

#### `vayikra` (Vayikra 1:1–5:26)
- **In brief:** G-d calls to Moshe from the Tent of Meeting and gives him the laws of the offerings (korbanot). The parashah describes the burnt offering (olah), the meal offering (minchah), the peace offering (shelamim), the sin offering (chatat) for an unintentional sin, and the guilt offering (asham). The size of some offerings depends on a person's means: a poor person may bring birds or a little flour. The section ends with the laws concerning a person who deceived his fellow and took someone else's property.
- **Theme:** Korban comes from the root meaning “drawing near”: the Torah gives every person, rich and poor, a way to draw near to G-d.
- Sun: The book opens with the word “Vayikra,” “And He called.” Before giving Moshe the laws, G-d calls him by name. Real conversation begins with attention to the person.
- Mon: About the poor man who brings a little flour, the Torah uses the word “nefesh,” “soul.” Rashi explains: G-d accepts his modest gift as if the person had offered his very soul. What matters is not the size of the gift but the heart.
- Tue: Every offering was salted: this is the “salt of the covenant” (2:13). Salt does not spoil, and the covenant between G-d and His people is just as enduring. This verse is linked to the custom of putting salt on the table when bread is broken after the berachah.
- Wed: Shelamim is the peace offering. Part of it was burned on the altar, part went to the kohanim, and part was eaten by the owner himself. Rashi says that it brings peace to everyone involved. A festive meal can also be divine service.
- Thu: About a tribal leader who sinned by mistake, the Torah says “asher,” “when.” Rashi reads this as “ashrei”: “fortunate is the generation whose leader admits his mistake.” Admitting a slip is a strength, not a weakness.
- Fri: The chatat was brought for an unintentional sin. This means that a person is responsible even for carelessness. Today, when there is no Temple, the way to make amends is teshuvah, prayer and tzedakah.
- Shabbat: The Torah calls deceiving one's fellow “a betrayal of the Almighty” (5:21). First one must return what was taken and add a fifth, and only then bring an offering. Relationships between people are part of one's relationship with G-d.
- [ ] checked

#### `tzav` (Vayikra 6:1–8:36)
- **In brief:** The Torah continues the laws of the offerings, now as instructions to the kohanim: how to perform the burnt offering, the minchah, the chatat, the asham and the shelamim, including the thanksgiving offering (todah). The mitzvah of the perpetual fire on the altar is given. Then, over seven days, Moshe inducts Aharon and his sons into the service: he washes them, dresses them in the garments of the kohanim, anoints them with oil and brings the offerings of consecration.
- **Theme:** Holiness rests on constancy: the fire on the altar never goes out, and divine service begins with simple daily tasks.
- Sun: Every morning the kohen began the service by removing the ashes from the altar (terumat hadeshen). Even the simplest task can become divine service if it is done for the sake of Heaven.
- Mon: “A perpetual fire shall burn on the altar; it shall not go out” (6:6). The inner fire must also be kept up every day: a little prayer, a little study, one good deed.
- Tue: The chatat was slaughtered in the same place as the burnt offering. The Sages explain: this way no onlooker knew who had come because of a sin. The Torah protects the dignity of the person who is making amends.
- Wed: The thanksgiving offering (todah) was brought by a person who had survived danger. The Midrash says that in the future all offerings will be abolished except the offering of thanksgiving. Today, in a similar situation, the blessing of HaGomel is recited.
- Thu: The consecration of the kohanim took place before the whole people, at the entrance to the Tent of Meeting (8:3). The service of some people concerns everyone, and the community becomes its witness.
- Fri: The blood of the consecration offering was placed on Aharon's ear, the thumb of his hand and the big toe of his foot (8:23). The Sages see an image in this: what we hear, what we do and where we go must all be sanctified.
- Shabbat: For seven days the kohanim did not leave the entrance of the Tent (8:33). Great changes require time and preparation. The path to giyur is never quick either, and that is part of its value.
- [ ] checked

#### `shmini` (Vayikra 9:1–11:47)
- **In brief:** On the eighth day, Aharon performs the service for the first time, and fire from G-d comes down onto the altar before the eyes of the whole people. On the same day, Aharon's sons Nadav and Avihu bring a “strange fire” that they had not been commanded to bring, and they die; Aharon is silent. The kohanim are forbidden to serve while intoxicated. Then the Torah gives the laws of kashrut: the signs of kosher animals and fish, the list of forbidden birds, and the laws about insects.
- **Theme:** Closeness to the Holy requires awe and faithfulness to the commanded path, and kashrut carries this discipline into everyday life.
- Sun: “Shemini” means “eighth.” Seven days are a complete natural cycle, and the eighth leads beyond it. It is no accident that brit milah, too, is performed on the eighth day.
- Mon: Moshe and Aharon entered the Tent, and when they came out they blessed the people, and then the glory of G-d appeared (9:23). Blessing others is also part of divine service.
- Tue: “And Aharon was silent” (10:3). Sometimes in grief there are no words, and silence, too, can be a response of faith. When you are with someone who is mourning, there is no need to rush with explanations.
- Wed: The kohanim who continued the service were told not to mourn openly, while the whole house of Israel would weep for those who had died (10:6). The grief of one becomes the grief of the community, and a person is not left alone with it.
- Thu: The kohanim are forbidden to serve after drinking wine, so as “to distinguish between the holy and the ordinary” (10:10). A clear head is needed for any serious undertaking, and above all for prayer.
- Fri: Moshe was angry with Aharon's sons, listened to Aharon's explanation and admitted that Aharon was right (10:20). Rashi emphasizes that Moshe was not ashamed to say, “I had not heard this.” A teacher, too, can admit a mistake.
- Shabbat: Kosher animals have split hooves and chew the cud; kosher fish have fins and scales. The law concludes with the words “be holy, for I am holy” (11:44). Kashrut teaches you to think about what you eat and why.
- [ ] checked

#### `tazria` (Vayikra 12:1–13:59)
- **In brief:** The parashah begins with the laws concerning a woman after childbirth and the mitzvah of brit milah on the eighth day of a boy's life. Most of it is devoted to tzaraat, a special affliction of the skin, hair and clothing that is not the same as an ordinary illness. Only a kohen decides whether a mark is “pure” or “impure”; a person with confirmed tzaraat lives outside the camp until he is purified. The Sages connect tzaraat above all with evil speech (lashon hara).
- **Theme:** A person's body, clothing and speech are connected with their spiritual state, and the Torah teaches us to see the inner meaning behind an outward sign.
- Sun: “And on the eighth day the flesh of his foreskin shall be circumcised” (12:3). Brit milah is the covenant of Avraham; it is performed on time even on Shabbat. In traditional giyur for men, it is one of the main steps.
- Mon: After the birth of a child, the mother brought an offering, and a poor family brought two birds (12:8). Birth is a time of gratitude, and the Torah makes it accessible to everyone.
- Tue: Only a kohen can declare a mark impure (13:3), even if a Torah scholar is present. A word that changes a person's fate must be spoken with care and full responsibility.
- Wed: When a case is unclear, the kohen waits seven days and looks again (13:4–5). Do not rush to conclusions about a person: sometimes it takes time to see the truth.
- Thu: A person with tzaraat lives apart, “outside the camp” (13:46). The Sages explain: through evil speech he divided people, so he himself is set apart for a time. The punishment teaches; it does not take revenge.
- Fri: Miriam was struck with tzaraat after she spoke badly about Moshe (Bamidbar 12). That is why the Sages see in tzaraat a lesson about the power of speech. A word can wound no less than a deed.
- Shabbat: The Rambam writes that tzaraat came gradually: first on the walls of the house, then on the clothing, and only then on the body. It is a series of gentle warnings. It is worth noticing the small signs and changing in time.
- [ ] checked

#### `metzora` (Vayikra 14:1–15:33)
- **In brief:** The Torah describes the purification of a person healed of tzaraat: the kohen goes out to him beyond the camp; two birds, cedar wood, a crimson thread and hyssop are used; then come immersion, shaving and offerings. Next it speaks of tzaraat of houses in Eretz Israel: before the kohen inspects the house, its belongings are taken out. The section concludes with the laws of ritual impurity connected with bodily discharges and of purification by immersion in water.
- **Theme:** There is a way back from impurity: the Torah not only gives a diagnosis but also shows the steps of returning to the community.
- Sun: It says “he shall be brought to the kohen,” but right away, “and the kohen shall go out beyond the camp” (14:2–3). Someone who has ended up far away is waiting for others to come out to meet him. Do not wait for the one who has drifted away to come back on his own.
- Mon: For purification, live birds, cedar and hyssop are taken. Rashi explains: birds chirp, a reminder of idle chatter; the tall cedar recalls arrogance, and the lowly hyssop the humility that heals.
- Tue: The ear, thumb and big toe of the person being purified are daubed with blood and oil (14:14), almost as in the consecration of the kohanim. A person who returns is not second-class: he is brought back with dignity.
- Wed: If the person being purified is poor, he brings a smaller offering (14:21). The Torah closes the way back to no one because of money.
- Thu: “I will put the affliction of tzaraat in a house in the land of your possession” (14:34). Rashi cites a midrash: the former inhabitants hid treasures in the walls, and when the owner took apart the afflicted wall, he found them. Even misfortune can reveal something valuable.
- Fri: Before the kohen enters the house, its belongings are taken out so that they will not become impure (14:36). The Sages see here how the Torah protects a person's property. Even a strict law contains care.
- Shabbat: Purification concludes with immersion in water (15:13). The mikveh is an image of renewal, and it is also the central moment of giyur: a person comes out of the water with a new status.
- [ ] checked

#### `achrei-mot` (Vayikra 16:1–18:30)
- **In brief:** After the death of Aharon's sons, G-d commands when and how the High Priest may enter the Holy of Holies: once a year, on Yom Kippur. The service of that day is described: the incense, the blood of the offerings, the two goats, one of which is sent “to Azazel” in the wilderness, and the mitzvah to “afflict your souls.” Then come the prohibition of bringing offerings outside the Mishkan and the prohibition of consuming blood. Chapter 18 lists the forbidden intimate relationships and calls on the people not to follow the practices of Egypt and Canaan.
- **Theme:** Atonement is possible, but holiness requires boundaries: in time, in place, in food and in family life.
- Sun: “Let him not come at all times into the Sanctuary” (16:2). Holiness has its own time and its own measure. Shabbat and the festivals teach us the same thing: not every day is the same.
- Mon: On Yom Kippur, the High Priest serves in simple white linen garments, not in golden ones (16:4). A person stands before G-d in humility. This is the source of the custom of wearing white on Yom Kippur.
- Tue: The two goats look identical, and only the lot determines their fate (16:8). People, too, start out alike, but their choices make them different. Yom Kippur is a time to choose one's path anew.
- Wed: “For on this day He will atone for you… before G-d you shall be purified” (16:30). The Mishnah teaches: Yom Kippur atones for sins between a person and G-d, but sins against one's fellow only after you have asked them for forgiveness.
- Thu: Meat could not be offered just anywhere, only in the Mishkan (17:3–5). The desire to serve G-d is directed along the path He gave, not along a path a person has invented for himself.
- Fri: “For the life of the flesh is in the blood” (17:11). That is why eating blood is forbidden, and in kashrut it is removed from meat. Even at an ordinary meal we remember respect for life.
- Shabbat: “Keep My statutes… and live by them” (18:5). From this the Sages derive that almost any mitzvah is set aside to save a life. The Torah was given for life.
- [ ] checked

#### `kedoshim` (Vayikra 19:1–20:27)
- **In brief:** “You shall be holy, for I am holy”: so begins the section where the mitzvot of honoring parents, Shabbat and rejecting idols stand side by side with the laws of leaving part of the harvest for the poor, honest trade, paying wages on time and just judgment. Here we hear “do not go about as a talebearer,” “do not stand by the blood of your fellow,” “do not take revenge and do not bear a grudge” and “love your fellow as yourself.” The Torah commands us to love the ger as ourselves. Chapter 20 speaks of the punishments for grave transgressions.
- **Theme:** Holiness is not withdrawal from the world but a special way of living in it: in the family, in the field, in the marketplace, in court and in words.
- Sun: The words “you shall be holy” were said to “the whole congregation” (19:2), not only to the kohanim. The Ramban explains: holiness is moderation even in what is permitted. Everyone can be holy wherever they live.
- Mon: Honoring parents and Shabbat are named in the same verse (19:3). Rashi: if a parent tells you to violate Shabbat, you do not listen, because both the parent and the child serve the same G-d. Honoring people has its limits.
- Tue: The corner of the field and the fallen ears of grain are left “for the poor and the ger” (19:10). A worker's wages may not be held back until morning (19:13). Generosity and honesty are concrete actions, not just feelings.
- Wed: “In righteousness shall you judge your fellow” (19:15). From this the Sages teach us to judge every person favorably. Before condemning, look for a good explanation.
- Thu: “Love your fellow as yourself” (19:18). Rabbi Akiva called this a great principle of the Torah. Hillel put it more simply: what is hateful to you, do not do to another.
- Fri: “When a ger dwells among you… love him as yourself, for you were strangers in Egypt” (19:33–34). The Sages note that the Torah warns many times against wronging the ger. A people that was itself once a stranger must remember how a stranger feels.
- Shabbat: Scales, weights and measures must be honest (19:35–36). And at the end of the section: “I have set you apart from the peoples to be Mine” (20:26). The special path of Israel is tested in ordinary affairs.
- [ ] checked

#### `emor` (Vayikra 21:1–24:23)
- **In brief:** The parashah begins with special laws for the kohanim: about ritual purity, marriage and the requirement to offer animals without blemish. Chapter 23 lists the “moadim,” the appointed times: Shabbat, Pesach, the omer offering and its counting, Shavuot, Rosh Hashanah, Yom Kippur and Sukkot. Then it speaks of the oil for the Menorah and the showbread in the Mishkan. The parashah concludes with the story of a man who cursed the Name of G-d and with the laws of damage caused to another.
- **Theme:** Time can be sanctified: the Torah turns the calendar into a series of meetings between the people and G-d.
- Sun: “Speak to the kohanim… and say to them” (21:1): the word “say” is repeated. Rashi explains: adults must also teach the children. Holiness is passed on from generation to generation through education.
- Mon: A kohen with a physical blemish does not serve at the altar, but he eats the holy food together with the others (21:22). He remains part of the family of kohanim. A limitation in one area does not take away dignity in everything.
- Tue: “And I shall be sanctified among the children of Israel” (22:32). From this comes the mitzvah of sanctifying G-d's Name: an honest, kind deed by a Jew glorifies Him in people's eyes. Every deed is a testimony.
- Wed: In the middle of the list of festivals, the Torah suddenly reminds us to leave the corner of the field for the poor (23:22). Rashi: whoever does so is as if he built the Temple and brought offerings in it. A festival is incomplete without caring for the poor. This is also where the counting of the omer begins, the road from Pesach to Shavuot.
- Thu: Rosh Hashanah is called a day of “remembrance by the sounding of the shofar” (23:24), and Yom Kippur a day when you “afflict your souls” (23:27). First the sound of the shofar awakens, and then comes the time for teshuvah.
- Fri: “You shall dwell in booths for seven days… so that your generations may know” (23:42–43). Sukkot teaches that our true home is under G-d's protection. A festival is a lesson that a child sees with their own eyes.
- Shabbat: The Oral Torah understands “an eye for an eye” (24:20) as monetary compensation for damage, not as a literal injury. Without the Oral Torah, the Written Torah cannot be understood correctly.
- [ ] checked

#### `behar` (Vayikra 25:1–26:2)
- **In brief:** On Mount Sinai, G-d gives the laws of shemitah: every seventh year the land in Eretz Israel rests. After seven shemitah cycles comes the Jubilee year (yovel): land returns to its original owners, and Jewish servants go free. The Torah forbids deception in trade and hurtful words, commands us to redeem the land of a relative who has become poor, to support someone who is falling into poverty, and to lend to him without interest. The parashah ends with a reminder about Shabbat and the Sanctuary.
- **Theme:** The land and everything a person owns belong to G-d, so prosperity obliges us to honesty and generosity.
- Sun: Why does it say that shemitah was given “on Mount Sinai”? Rashi answers: to teach that all the mitzvot, both the general rules and the details, were given at Sinai. In this way the Written and the Oral Torah come together.
- Mon: “And if you say: what shall we eat in the seventh year?” “I will command My blessing” (25:20–21). Shemitah teaches trust in G-d. Every Shabbat is a small shemitah: we stop and we trust.
- Tue: “And proclaim liberty throughout the land” (25:10). The Yovel (Jubilee) begins with the sound of the shofar on Yom Kippur. Freedom is not only a right but also a goal toward which the Torah leads.
- Wed: The sages apply “You shall not wrong one another” (25:17) to wronging with words. The Mishnah gives an example: one may not remind a ger of his past. A word can wound more deeply than cheating someone out of money.
- Thu: “For the land is Mine; you are strangers and residents with Me” (25:23). Before G-d, every person is a bit of a ger. This teaches humility and respect for those who have arrived recently.
- Fri: “And you shall support him” (25:35). Rashi compares this to a load on a donkey: it is easier to catch it before it falls. The Rambam calls the highest level of tzedakah the kind of help after which a person can stand on their own feet.
- Shabbat: One may not lend to a brother at interest (25:36–37). The chapter ends with words about Shabbat and the Sanctuary (26:2). Honest money and holy time are two pillars of a single life.
- [ ] checked

#### `bechukotai` (Vayikra 26:3–27:34)
- **In brief:** G-d promises blessings — rain, harvest, peace and His presence among the people — if Israel follows His laws. Then comes the tochechah, a stern warning of calamities and exile if the people reject the covenant; yet even then G-d will not reject Israel and will remember His covenant with Yaakov, Yitzchak and Avraham. The last chapter deals with vows and dedications to the Sanctuary: the valuation (erchin) of people, animals, houses and fields, as well as the tithe of livestock. This chapter concludes the book of Vayikra.
- **Theme:** The covenant rests on mutual faithfulness, and even in the hardest times the bond between G-d and the people is never broken.
- Sun: “If you walk in My statutes” (26:3). Rashi explains: this means toiling in the study of Torah. “To walk” means to keep moving: the path of Torah is constant growth.
- Mon: “And I will grant peace in the land” (26:6). Rashi: if there is no peace, there is nothing, and peace is equal to all the blessings together. Peace at home and in the community is a blessing too.
- Tue: “And I will walk among you” (26:12). The highest reward is not harvest or wealth but closeness to G-d. You can feel it on Shabbat, in prayer, at a good meal.
- Wed: The Torah warns about those who “walk with Me by chance” (26:21). The Rambam teaches: to regard misfortunes as mere chance is cruel; it is better to reflect and mend one's ways.
- Thu: “And I will remember My covenant with Yaakov… with Yitzchak… with Avraham” (26:42), “I will not reject them” (26:44). The history of the Jewish people is a history of faithfulness that has outlived exiles.
- Fri: The Torah sets a person's valuation by age, not by strength, wealth or talent (27:1–8). In the eyes of the Torah, a person's worth does not depend on how successful they are.
- Shabbat: When the reading of a book is completed, the community proclaims: “Chazak, chazak ve-nitchazek” — “Be strong, be strong, and let us be strengthened!” A completion is an occasion to gather strength for a new beginning.
- [ ] checked

#### `bamidbar` (Bamidbar 1:1–4:20)
- **In brief:** The book of Bamidbar opens in the Sinai desert, a year after the Exodus: G-d commands Moshe and Aharon to count, tribe by tribe, all the men from twenty years old who are fit for the army. It describes how the tribes are arranged around the Mishkan, each under its own banner, three on each side. The Levites are not included in the general count: they serve at the Mishkan in place of the firstborn, and the family of Kehat is entrusted with carrying the holy objects. This parashah is usually read before Shavuot.
- **Theme:** Every person in the nation is counted and matters, and each has their own place around the shared center — the Torah and the Sanctuary.
- Sun: Why count the people again? Rashi: out of love, G-d counts them often, the way a person counts over and over what is dear to him. Every single person counts.
- Mon: The count is made “by the number of names” (1:2). The nation is not a faceless mass: each person has a name, a family and a story of their own.
- Tue: The Torah was given “in the desert.” The Midrash explains: just as the desert belongs to no one, so the Torah is open to anyone who comes to it. To receive it, one needs humility and openness.
- Wed: Each tribe stands under its own banner (2:2), but all of them surround one Mishkan. We can be different and still remain one people.
- Thu: “These are the generations of Aharon and Moshe” (3:1) — yet only Aharon's sons are listed. Rashi: whoever teaches Torah to his fellow's son is regarded as if he had fathered him. A teacher is a parent too.
- Fri: The Levites took the place of the firstborn in the service (3:12). From here comes the mitzvah of pidyon haben — the redemption of the firstborn son from a kohen — which is still observed today.
- Shabbat: The family of Kehat carried the holiest objects on their shoulders, without wagons. The important things in life cannot be shifted onto others: each person carries the Torah themselves.
- [ ] checked

#### `nasso` (Bamidbar 4:21–7:89)
- **In brief:** The count of the Levites continues: the families of Gershon and Merari receive their duties in carrying the Mishkan. The Torah gives laws about returning stolen property along with confession of guilt, about a wife suspected of infidelity (sotah), and about the nazir, who takes on special vows of holiness. The Priestly Blessing is pronounced. Then the twelve tribal leaders, one after another, bring identical offerings for the dedication of the altar, and Moshe hears the voice of the Almighty from the Holy of Holies.
- **Theme:** Everyone in the community has their own place and their own role, and blessing can come through each of them.
- Sun: The Levites of the family of Gershon carried the curtains and coverings of the Mishkan, not the sacred vessels. The Torah teaches that every kind of service, even the plainest-looking, is necessary and important.
- Mon: The count of the Levites shows that each one was named and counted. In the Jewish people no one gets lost in the crowd, and everyone has their own name and their own share.
- Tue: If a person has wronged another, the Torah requires not only remorse but also returning what was taken and confessing the wrong aloud. There is even a special law about someone who wronged a ger: the debt to him does not disappear. Teshuvah begins with repairing what can be repaired.
- Wed: The nazir gives up wine and haircuts for the sake of closeness to G-d, but at the end he brings an offering. The sages see in this a reminder: holiness is sought within ordinary life, not by renouncing it.
- Thu: “May Hashem bless you and keep you…” — the Priestly Blessing still resounds in the synagogue to this day. The kohanim only pronounce the words; it is the Almighty Himself who blesses.
- Fri: The twelve leaders brought identical offerings, yet the Torah describes each one separately. Even when we do the same as others, what matters to G-d is that you are the one who did it.
- Shabbat: Moshe enters the Tent of Meeting and hears the voice speaking to him. When we study Torah, we too try to hear what it is saying to us personally.
- [ ] checked

#### `behaalotcha` (Bamidbar 8:1–12:16)
- **In brief:** Aharon kindles the lamp of the Mishkan, and the Levites undergo purification and begin their service. People who were unable to bring the Pesach offering ask for a chance, and the Almighty establishes Pesach Sheni — the second Pesach. The people set out on the journey following the cloud, but soon the complaints begin: about hardships, about the manna, about the lack of meat. Moshe receives seventy elders to help him, and Miriam is punished for her words about her brother.
- **Theme:** The way forward requires light, patience and careful speech, and there is always room for a second chance.
- Sun: Aharon kindled the lamp so that the flame would burn on its own. A good teacher, too, kindles a light in the student that will keep burning without him.
- Mon: The Levites were set apart for the service of G-d. Everyone who dedicates themselves to the Torah follows their path in some way: purifying oneself and taking on responsibility.
- Tue: People who were impure said, “Why should we be deprived?” — and the Almighty gave them Pesach Sheni. A sincere desire to draw near opens doors that seemed closed.
- Wed: The people journeyed and encamped at G-d's word, following the cloud. Sometimes a stop lasted a long time, sometimes a single day. Trust means going when it is time to go and waiting when it is necessary to wait.
- Thu: Moshe calls to his father-in-law: “Come with us, and we will do you good.” Whoever joins the people of Israel receives a share in all the good that the Almighty gives His people.
- Fri: The people complained about the manna, though they lacked nothing. The Torah teaches us to notice what we already have and to give thanks for it.
- Shabbat: Miriam spoke about Moshe, and the Torah regards even such words as a mistake. Moshe, however, prays for her briefly: “G-d, please, heal her.” The ethics of speech and the ability to forgive are two sides of the same lesson.
- [ ] checked

#### `shlach` (Bamidbar 13:1–15:41)
- **In brief:** Moshe sends twelve scouts into the Land of Israel. Ten of them return with a frightening report, and the people lose faith; only Calev and Yehoshua urge them not to be afraid. The generation that left Egypt is condemned to wander in the desert for forty years. Then the Torah gives the laws of libations, of challah, of one law for the ger and the born Jew, and the mitzvah of tzitzit.
- **Theme:** Our view of the world determines whether we see a trial as a threat or as a gift.
- Sun: The scouts were respected men, and still they went wrong. A high position does not protect against fear once trust in G-d is lost.
- Mon: They saw a beautiful land and large fruit, but what they spoke about first and foremost was fear. The same facts can be told in a way that inspires or in a way that destroys.
- Tue: “We were like grasshoppers in our own eyes, and so we were in their eyes.” How we see ourselves affects how others see us.
- Wed: Calev and Yehoshua said: “If G-d delights in us, He will bring us in.” Courage is not the absence of fear but faith that is stronger than it.
- Thu: The people repented and went off to war on their own, but Moshe warned them: G-d is not with you. Teshuvah is necessary, but it does not mean acting against the word of the Almighty.
- Fri: “One law and one ordinance shall be for you and for the ger who lives with you.” Someone who has gone through giyur is a full member of the people, with the same mitzvot and the same closeness to G-d.
- Shabbat: Tzitzit remind us of all the commandments: “You shall see and remember.” We say these words every day in the Shema — a small thread that holds an entire life together.
- [ ] checked

#### `korach` (Bamidbar 16:1–18:32)
- **In brief:** Korach, a Levite from a distinguished family, together with Datan, Aviram and two hundred and fifty men, rises up against Moshe and Aharon. The earth swallows the instigators, and fire consumes those who offered incense. To settle the dispute, Aharon's staff blossoms overnight and yields almonds. The Torah lists the gifts to the kohanim and the Levites and the Levites' obligation to set aside a tithe.
- **Theme:** A dispute for the sake of personal glory destroys, while service for the sake of Heaven builds.
- Sun: Korach said, “The whole community is holy,” and it sounded beautiful. But the sages teach that behind his words lay envy. It is important to examine not only words but also motives.
- Mon: In response to the attacks, Moshe does not shout; he falls on his face and proposes settling the dispute in the morning. Patience and a pause often save us from great mistakes.
- Tue: The Mishnah in Pirkei Avot calls Korach's dispute the example of a dispute not for the sake of Heaven. A dispute for the sake of truth is beneficial; a dispute for the sake of power is doomed.
- Wed: When disaster struck, Moshe and Aharon prayed for the whole people, even for those who accused them. A true leader cares even for those who stand against him.
- Thu: Aharon runs with the incense and stands “between the dead and the living.” Sometimes one person is given the power to stop a disaster, if they are not afraid to step into the middle.
- Fri: Aharon's staff blossomed and yielded almonds. Dry wood can come back to life: true service bears fruit even where none is expected.
- Shabbat: The Levites received the tithe and themselves set aside a portion of it for the kohanim. Even the one who receives also gives — that is how tzedakah works in Jewish life.
- [ ] checked

#### `chukat` (Bamidbar 19:1–22:1)
- **In brief:** The Torah gives the law of the red heifer, whose ashes purify from the impurity of death. Miriam dies, and the people's water disappears; Moshe strikes the rock instead of speaking to it, and learns that he will not enter the Land. Aharon dies, and the whole people mourns him for thirty days. The people go through trials, the copper serpent heals those who were bitten, and Israel defeats Sichon and Og.
- **Theme:** There are laws that are beyond our understanding, and faith is tested in losses and trials.
- Sun: The law of the red heifer is called a “chukah” — a law whose reason is not explained. Mitzvot are kept not only when you understand them, but also because you trust the One who gave them.
- Mon: The Torah speaks of the impurity that comes from the dead and of the path of purification. Judaism treats death and the memory of a person with great respect, but it directs us back toward life.
- Tue: Miriam died — and the water vanished. The sages teach that the well accompanied the people in her merit. Often we only understand what a person gave us once they are gone.
- Wed: Moshe struck the rock instead of speaking to it. Even a great person is responsible for every action, and the Torah does not hide the mistakes of its heroes.
- Thu: “The whole house of Israel” mourned Aharon for thirty days. The sages explain: he loved peace and knew how to make peace between people. That kind of love is what the people remember longest.
- Fri: Those who were bitten looked at the copper serpent and were healed. The Mishnah explains: it was not the serpent that healed, but the fact that people turned their hearts toward Heaven.
- Shabbat: The people sing a song to the well: “Spring up, O well!” After losses comes a time for gratitude, and the Torah teaches us to find it even in the desert.
- [ ] checked

#### `balak` (Bamidbar 22:2–25:9)
- **In brief:** Balak, king of Moav, hires the prophet Bilam to curse Israel. On the way, Bilam's donkey sees an angel that the prophet himself does not see, and begins to speak. Three times Bilam tries to curse the people, and three times a blessing comes out of his mouth, including the words “Mah tovu.” At the end, the people fall into sin with the Moabite women and the idol Peor.
- **Theme:** No curse has power over a people the Almighty has blessed, but the main danger comes from within.
- Sun: Balak feared Israel, although the people had no intention of attacking him. Fear and hatred are often born not of a real threat but of what a person has imagined.
- Mon: Bilam said he would do only what G-d commanded, yet he still wanted to go. The sages teach: a person is led along the road they themselves wish to choose.
- Tue: The donkey saw the angel before the prophet did. Sometimes what is simple and humble sees the truth more clearly than pride and intellect.
- Wed: “Behold, a people that dwells apart.” Israel's special path is not estrangement but faithfulness to its calling among the other nations.
- Thu: “He has not seen iniquity in Yaakov.” The sages explain: the Almighty looks upon His people with love and sees in them, above all, the good.
- Fri: “How goodly are your tents, O Yaakov!” With these words of Bilam we begin the morning prayer as we enter the synagogue. The blessing came even from the mouth of one who wanted to curse.
- Shabbat: The external enemy could not harm Israel, but the sin of Peor did. The spiritual strength of the people is preserved through faithfulness, modesty and keeping the mitzvot.
- [ ] checked

#### `pinchas` (Bamidbar 25:10–30:1)
- **In brief:** Pinchas receives from the Almighty a “covenant of peace” for stopping the plague. A new census of the people is taken before entering the Land. The daughters of Tzelofchad ask for their father's inheritance, and the Torah establishes the laws of inheritance. Moshe appoints Yehoshua as his successor, and then the Torah lists the daily, Shabbat and festival offerings.
- **Theme:** Each generation takes up the baton of service, and every voice has a place in the people.
- Sun: Pinchas received a “covenant of peace.” The sages emphasize that zealotry for G-d's sake is a rare exception, while the ordinary path of the Torah is the path of peace.
- Mon: A new census: the old generation has passed, and a new one has grown up. Each generation takes the Torah and responsibility for the people upon itself anew.
- Tue: The daughters of Tzelofchad asked for justice politely and boldly, and G-d said: “They speak rightly.” A question asked with respect can change the law for the better.
- Wed: Moshe himself did not know the answer and turned to the Almighty. Even the greatest teacher is not ashamed to say: “I will ask.”
- Thu: Moshe asks for a shepherd to be appointed “so that the community will not be like sheep without a shepherd.” Caring for the community's future is part of true leadership.
- Fri: The Torah lists the offerings for every day, for Shabbat and for Rosh Chodesh. Jewish time has a rhythm: weekdays, Shabbat, months and festivals.
- Shabbat: The festival offerings are described separately — for Pesach, Shavuot, Rosh Hashanah, Yom Kippur and Sukkot. In memory of them, we recite the Musaf prayer today.
- [ ] checked

#### `matot` (Bamidbar 30:2–32:42)
- **In brief:** Moshe teaches the tribal leaders the laws of vows and oaths: a person must fulfill what they have promised. Israel wages war against Midian, and afterward the Torah explains how to purify the captured vessels. The tribes of Reuven and Gad ask to remain on the eastern bank of the Jordan. Moshe agrees on condition that they go to war together with all their brothers.
- **Theme:** A person's word has power, and responsibility to the community outweighs personal convenience.
- Sun: “He shall not break his word; he shall do all that has come out of his mouth.” In Judaism, a word is a commitment, which is why the sages advise against making unnecessary promises.
- Mon: The Torah explains how certain vows can be annulled. From here comes the custom of annulling vows before Rosh Hashanah: we try to begin the year with a clear conscience.
- Tue: The war with Midian was Moshe's last task before his passing. He did not postpone it and carried out what he was charged with, even though he knew that afterward his life would come to an end.
- Wed: After the war, the soldiers needed to purify themselves. Even a necessary war does not pass without leaving a mark on the soul.
- Thu: Vessels taken from the Midianites were purified by fire or by boiling water. From here come the laws of kashering utensils, which are observed to this day.
- Fri: The tribes of Reuven and Gad spoke first about their flocks and only then about their children. Moshe corrected the order: children first, then property. It is important to remember what matters most.
- Shabbat: “You shall be clear before G-d and before Israel.” It is not enough to be in the right — people must also see the honesty of our actions.
- [ ] checked

#### `masei` (Bamidbar 33:1–36:13)
- **In brief:** The Torah lists the forty-two encampments of the people of Israel from Egypt to the Jordan. The Almighty sets the borders of the Land of Israel and the leaders who will divide it. The Levites are given cities, and six of them become cities of refuge for someone who killed a person unintentionally. The book of Bamidbar ends with the law on the marriage of Tzelofchad's daughters, so that the inheritance stays within the tribe.
- **Theme:** Every step along the way has meaning, and even a mistake does not take away a person's place among the people.
- Sun: Moshe recorded all the encampments at G-d's word. Every stage of our journey, even a difficult one, is part of a shared story and teaches us something.
- Mon: The list of encampments also recalls the hard places. Each person's path to the Torah is their own, with stops and turns, and the Almighty remembers every step.
- Tue: The people are commanded to enter the Land and not to leave any idols in it. A new life requires parting with the old things that contradict it.
- Wed: The Torah clearly describes the borders of the Land of Israel. Holiness has a place and a form, not just a feeling.
- Thu: The Levites received no portion of land, but they received cities among all the tribes. Teachers of Torah live among the people so as to be accessible to everyone.
- Fri: The cities of refuge were open “for the children of Israel, and for the ger, and for the resident.” The Torah's protection and mercy extend to everyone.
- Shabbat: “Do not defile the land in which you live, for I dwell in its midst.” Human life is sacred, and we are responsible for the land where the Shechinah dwells. “Chazak, chazak ve-nitchazek!”
- [ ] checked

#### `devarim` (Devarim 1:1–3:22)
- **In brief:** In the last weeks of his life, Moshe begins his great address to the people on the eastern bank of the Jordan. He reminds them of the appointment of judges, of the episode of the scouts and of the forty years in the desert. Moshe recounts how Israel went around Edom, Moav and Ammon and defeated Sichon and Og. This parashah is always read on the Shabbat before Tisha B'Av, which is called Shabbat Chazon.
- **Theme:** To move forward, we must honestly remember the past and learn its lesson.
- Sun: Moshe reminds the people of their mistakes gently, by hinting at the places where they happened. A rebuke that protects a person's dignity is heard better.
- Mon: Moshe admits: “I cannot carry you alone.” Wisdom means knowing your limits and sharing responsibility.
- Tue: The judges are told: “Judge righteously between a man and his brother and the ger with him… fear no one.” Justice is the same for all, and the ger is protected on equal footing with everyone.
- Wed: Moshe retells the story of the spies. The mistakes of the past are not hidden away but remembered, so that we do not repeat them.
- Thu: Israel is forbidden to attack Edom, Moab and Ammon: every nation has its allotted portion from the Almighty. Respect for the borders of others is also part of the Torah.
- Fri: The victory over Sichon and Og showed that what had seemed impossible came to pass with G-d's help. Remembering past miracles strengthens faith in the future.
- Shabbat: “Fear them not, for the L-rd your G-d fights for you.” Before Tisha B'Av, the day of mourning for the destruction of the Temple, the Torah reminds us that comfort will follow grief.
- [ ] checked

#### `vaetchanan` (Devarim 3:23–7:11)
- **In brief:** Moshe tells how he pleaded with the Almighty to let him enter the Land of Israel and was refused. He calls on the people to keep the mitzvot, adding nothing and subtracting nothing, and recalls the revelation at Mount Sinai, where the people heard a voice but saw no form. Moshe repeats the Ten Commandments and recites the first passage of the Shema Yisrael, about loving G-d with all your heart, all your soul and all your possessions. This parashah is read on Shabbat Nachamu, the first Shabbat after the 9th of Av, the Shabbat of comfort.
- **Theme:** G-d is One, and love for Him is expressed in studying and doing His words every day.
- Sun: Moshe prayed many times, although he knew the decision had already been made. Prayer has value in itself, even when the answer is not the one we hoped for.
- Mon: “You shall not add to it and you shall not subtract from it” (4:2): the Torah is a complete system. Studying it, we try to accept it as it is rather than reshape it to fit ourselves.
- Tue: Moshe designates three cities of refuge east of the Jordan. Even one who caused harm through carelessness has the right to protection and a fair trial.
- Wed: The Ten Commandments begin with the words “I am the L-rd your G-d, who brought you out of the land of Egypt.” Faith in Judaism rests on the memory of G-d's relationship with the people, not on an abstract idea.
- Thu: The people were frightened by the direct voice of G-d and asked Moshe to be an intermediary. Hence the special role of the teacher, through whom the Torah is passed from generation to generation.
- Fri: “Hear, O Israel” (6:4) — the central words of the Jewish faith. They are recited morning and evening, before sleep, and in the final minutes of life. Start by learning at least the first line.
- Shabbat: The people were chosen not because they are numerous (7:7), but out of love and faithfulness to the oath made to the patriarchs. Chosenness is not superiority but responsibility.
- [ ] checked

#### `eikev` (Devarim 7:12–11:25)
- **In brief:** Moshe promises the people blessing for keeping the mitzvot and reminds them of the forty years in the desert, when the Almighty fed them with manna. He warns: in plenty it is easy to forget G-d and say “my own power won me this wealth.” Moshe recalls the sin of the golden calf, the broken tablets and the second tablets. The parashah contains the mitzvah of blessing after a meal and the second passage of the Shema.
- **Theme:** Gratitude to G-d must be kept not only in hardship but also in plenty.
- Sun: The name of the parashah, Eikev, can be understood as “heel”: Rashi teaches that one should not neglect even those mitzvot that seem small and are “trampled underfoot.”
- Mon: “Man does not live by bread alone” (8:3). The manna taught the people that sustenance comes from the Almighty. A person needs spiritual food as well.
- Tue: “You shall eat, and be satisfied, and bless” (8:10) — the source of the blessing after the meal, Birkat HaMazon. After a meal, try at least briefly giving thanks.
- Wed: Moshe honestly reminds the people of their mistakes. The Torah does not idealize its heroes: admitting a mistake is the beginning of correction.
- Thu: After the broken tablets, G-d gave the second ones. The Sages say that both the whole and the broken tablets were kept in the Ark: the past is not erased, but it does not prevent a fresh start.
- Fri: “You shall love the stranger (the ger), for you were strangers in the land of Egypt” (10:19). The Torah directly commands love for one who has joined the people of Israel.
- Shabbat: The second passage of the Shema (11:13–21) speaks of love for G-d and of the connection between our actions and their consequences. It is read daily together with the first.
- [ ] checked

#### `reeh` (Devarim 11:26–16:17)
- **In brief:** Moshe sets before the people a choice between blessing and curse. He commands that sacrifices be brought only in the place that the Almighty will choose, and warns against false prophets and idolatry. The parashah lists the signs of kosher animals, fish and birds, the laws of tithes, debt release in the seventh year and help for the poor. It ends with the laws of the three pilgrimage festivals.
- **Theme:** Freedom of choice: a person chooses the path of blessing through daily actions.
- Sun: “See, I set before you today a blessing and a curse” (11:26). The word “today” reminds us that the choice is made anew every day.
- Mon: Service of G-d is tied to the place He will choose — later, Jerusalem and the Temple. Today worshippers all over the world face Jerusalem in their prayers.
- Tue: Even a miracle does not prove a prophet right if he calls us to turn away from the Torah (13:2–4). Loyalty to the Torah matters more than impressive wonders.
- Wed: “You are children of the L-rd your G-d” (14:1). From this follows both respect for one's own body and the laws of kashrut, which set apart a Jew's table.
- Thu: Kosher mammals have split hooves and chew the cud; fish have fins and scales (14:4–10). Kashrut begins with these simple signs.
- Fri: “Open your hand to your brother, to the poor” (15:8). Tzedakah is not only charity, but the duty to help a person get back on their feet.
- Shabbat: “You shall rejoice in your festival — you, and your son, and your daughter, and the Levite, and the stranger, and the orphan, and the widow” (16:14). Festive joy is incomplete if someone has been forgotten.
- [ ] checked

#### `shoftim` (Devarim 16:18–21:9)
- **In brief:** The parashah opens with the mitzvah to appoint judges and officers of order in every town and to judge fairly, taking no bribes. Moshe speaks of the supreme court whose rulings must be followed, and of the laws of the king, the kohanim, the Levites and the prophets. Next come the laws of the cities of refuge, of witnesses and of waging war, including the prohibition on cutting down fruit trees. The parashah ends with the ceremony for a murdered person found whose killer is unknown.
- **Theme:** Justice is the foundation of society, and it must be pursued by honest means.
- Sun: “Justice, justice shall you pursue” (16:20). The commentators explain the repetition this way: both the goal and the means must be just.
- Mon: “Do not turn aside from what they tell you” (17:11) — the foundation of the authority of the Sages and of the Oral Torah. We study the Torah together with its traditional interpretation.
- Tue: The king must write a Torah scroll for himself and read it all his life (17:18–19). The higher a person's position, the more humility they need.
- Wed: “Be wholehearted with the L-rd your G-d” (18:13). Rashi explains: do not try to divine the future; trust G-d with a simple heart.
- Thu: A charge is established only by the words of two witnesses (19:15). The Torah demands caution before condemning another person.
- Fri: Before battle, those who had built a house, planted a vineyard or become engaged were sent home, as was the one who was afraid (20:5–8). The Torah remembers a person's feelings even in war.
- Shabbat: “Do not destroy the trees” (20:19) — the source of the prohibition on needless destruction, bal tashchit. Caring for the world is also a mitzvah.
- [ ] checked

#### `ki-teitzei` (Devarim 21:10–25:19)
- **In brief:** This parashah contains more mitzvot than any other in the Torah: by the count of the Sefer HaChinuch, seventy-four. They concern family and marriage, returning lost items, treatment of animals, home safety, honest trade and care for the worker and the poor. Here too are the laws of tzitzit, divorce and levirate marriage. The parashah ends with the mitzvah to remember what Amalek did.
- **Theme:** Holiness shows itself in everyday life: at home, at work, in trade, and in the treatment of the weak.
- Sun: The Torah protects the rights of the firstborn, even if the father loves another wife more (21:15–17). Personal feelings must not violate justice.
- Mon: Having found a lost item, “you cannot hide yourself” (22:3). Returning lost property is an active mitzvah, not mere politeness.
- Tue: When you build a house, put a railing on the roof (22:8). We are responsible for the safety of those who come to us. This parashah also has the mitzvah of tzitzit on the corners of garments (22:12).
- Wed: “Do not abhor an Egyptian, for you were a stranger in his land” (23:8). Memory of one's own past teaches gratitude and compassion.
- Thu: “Whatever comes from your lips, you shall keep” (23:24). Promises and vows are taken very seriously in Judaism, so it is worth being careful with words.
- Fri: A newlywed is released from the army for a year to bring joy to his wife (24:5). The Torah values family happiness as an important goal.
- Shabbat: Pay a worker on the same day (24:15) and keep honest measures and scales (25:13–16). Honesty in business is part of serving G-d.
- [ ] checked

#### `ki-tavo` (Devarim 26:1–29:8)
- **In brief:** The parashah opens with the mitzvah of bringing the first fruits, the bikkurim, to the Temple with a thanksgiving declaration telling the history of the people from the patriarchs to the Exodus from Egypt. Moshe commands that the words of the Torah be written on large stones and that the blessings and curses be proclaimed on Mounts Gerizim and Eival. Then follows a detailed warning about the consequences of abandoning the mitzvot. At the end Moshe reminds them of the miracles of the forty years in the desert.
- **Theme:** Gratitude and memory of the past turn ordinary life into service of G-d.
- Sun: When bringing the first fruits, a person told the story of the people: “An Aramean sought to destroy my father…” (26:5). These words entered the Pesach Haggadah.
- Mon: The Rambam rules that a ger also brings the first fruits and says “our fathers,” for Avraham is the father of all who come under the wings of the Shechinah.
- Tue: The people and G-d “choose” one another (26:17–18). The covenant is a two-sided relationship, not a one-sided demand.
- Wed: The words of the Torah had to be written on stones “very clearly” (27:8). The Sages understood this as a translation into many languages: the Torah is open to every nation.
- Thu: Blessings are promised to one who listens to the voice of the Almighty (28:1–14). Blessing is not only prosperity, but also peace and confidence.
- Fri: The warning explains troubles by saying that G-d was served “without joy” (28:47). Joy in doing mitzvot is not decoration but essence.
- Shabbat: “Until this day the L-rd has not given you a heart to understand” (29:3). Understanding comes gradually, with years of study and life according to the Torah.
- [ ] checked

#### `nitzavim` (Devarim 29:9–30:20)
- **In brief:** Moshe gathers the whole people — from the elders to the woodcutters and water drawers, including the gerim — to enter into the covenant with the Almighty. The covenant is made also with those “who are not here today,” that is, with future generations. Moshe speaks of returning to G-d, teshuvah, and of how the Torah is not in heaven and not beyond the sea, but very close. The parashah ends with a call to choose life; it is read before Rosh Hashanah.
- **Theme:** The Torah is close to everyone, and the way back to G-d is always open.
- Sun: “You stand today, all of you” (29:9). Everyone takes part in the covenant regardless of status, and the ger is named in this list explicitly.
- Mon: The covenant was made also with those “who are not here today” (29:14). The Sages teach that at Sinai the souls of future gerim were present as well.
- Tue: Each person answers for their own choice: one cannot believe it possible to turn away from the covenant in secret and remain apart (29:17–19).
- Wed: “The hidden things belong to the L-rd, but the revealed things belong to us and our children” (29:28). We are not required to know everything, but we are required to do what is revealed to us.
- Thu: Chapter 30 speaks of teshuvah: the people will return to G-d, and G-d will return the people. It is a good theme for reflection before Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur.
- Fri: “It is not in heaven… nor is it beyond the sea” (30:12–13). The Torah was given to people, and it can be understood and kept here, on earth.
- Shabbat: “Choose life” (30:19). This is the main decision a person makes again and again, especially at the start of a new year.
- [ ] checked

#### `vayelech` (Devarim 31:1–31:30)
- **In brief:** On the last day of his life, at the age of 120, Moshe hands the leadership over to Yehoshua and encourages the people: “Be strong and courageous.” He writes down the Torah and entrusts it to the kohanim and the elders. Moshe commands that once every seven years, on the festival of Sukkot, the whole people be gathered to hear the Torah read. The Almighty warns that in the future He may “hide His face,” and commands that a song of witness be written.
- **Theme:** The Torah is passed from generation to generation, and each person becomes its bearer.
- Sun: On the last day of his life Moshe himself goes out to the people (31:1). A leader goes to the people; he does not wait for them to come to him.
- Mon: “Be strong and courageous” (31:6). Resolve is needed not only in battle, but in any new beginning, including the path to giyur.
- Tue: Moshe publicly transfers authority to Yehoshua (31:7–8). A good transition of generations is prepared in advance and with respect.
- Wed: Once every seven years the whole people — men, women, children and strangers — gathered to hear the Torah (31:12). This is the mitzvah of hakhel.
- Thu: “I will hide My face” (31:17–18). Even when G-d's presence is not felt, the connection is not broken.
- Fri: “Write this song for yourselves” (31:19) — the source of the mitzvah for every Jew to write a Torah scroll or take part in writing one.
- Shabbat: The Torah scroll was placed next to the Ark of the Covenant (31:26). The Torah written then is the same text that is read in the synagogue today.
- [ ] checked

#### `haazinu` (Devarim 32:1–32:52)
