- **In brief:** Almost the whole parashah is Moshe's poetic song, in which heaven and earth are called as witnesses. The song tells of G-d's care for the people, of how the people forget Him in plenty, of punishment and of future redemption. In the Torah scroll this song is written in a special way, in two columns. At the end the Almighty tells Moshe to ascend Mount Nebo, to see the Land of Israel from afar and to die there.
- **Theme:** The history of the people is a history of relationship with G-d, in which distance is always followed by return.
- Sun: “Give ear, O heavens… hear, O earth” (32:1). Heaven and earth are the eternal witnesses of the covenant, because they remain when generations pass away.
- Mon: “The Rock — perfect is His work” (32:4). These words are recited at funerals, accepting the judgment of the Almighty even in grief.
- Tue: “Ask your father, and he will tell you; your elders, and they will say to you” (32:7). The Jewish tradition lives through questions to elders and teachers.
- Wed: As an eagle hovers over its chicks (32:11), so does G-d care for the people. The commentators see in this an image of tender, careful protection.
- Thu: “Yeshurun grew fat and kicked” (32:15). Plenty tests a person no less than need.
- Fri: The song ends with the promise that G-d will stand up for His people (32:36–43). Even after long estrangement, hope remains.
- Shabbat: Moshe sees the Land but does not enter it (32:52). Sometimes our task is to prepare the path along which others will go.
- [ ] checked

#### `vezot-haberacha` (Devarim 33:1–34:12)
- **In brief:** The last parashah of the Torah is read not on an ordinary Shabbat but on the festival of Simchat Torah, right after which the reading begins again with the parashah of Bereshit. Before his death, Moshe blesses each of the tribes of Israel. Then he ascends Mount Nebo, sees the whole Land of Israel and dies; the Almighty buries him, and no one knows his burial place. The Torah concludes with the words that there never again arose a prophet like Moshe.
- **Theme:** The Torah is the heritage of the entire people, and its study never ends.
- Sun: The Torah ends with a blessing (33:1). Moshe's last words to the people are not a reproach but love.
- Mon: “Moshe commanded us the Torah, the heritage of the congregation of Yaakov” (33:4). Tradition bids us teach this verse to a child as soon as he begins to speak.
- Tue: Each tribe receives its own blessing. Every person in the nation has their own path and their own contribution.
- Wed: “Happy are you, Israel! Who is like you, a people saved by the L-rd?” (33:29). The joy of belonging to the people is the theme of the festival of Simchat Torah.
- Thu: No one knows where Moshe is buried (34:6). The Sages see in this a lesson: we honor the teaching of Moshe, not his grave.
- Fri: “There never again arose a prophet in Israel like Moshe” (34:10). This is one of the principles of faith according to the Rambam.
- Shabbat: On Simchat Torah, when the reading is finished, Bereshit begins right away. Torah study is a circle without end — a good time to start a new cycle yourself.
- [ ] checked

## New beit din questions (the “Read / Prepare” structure)

In November 2026 the blocks “The main points” and “What they ask at the beit din” were added to every lesson, and “What the section is about” and “Go deeper” were added to every section (see `docs/STRUCTURE.md`). “The main points” only retells the lesson text; the new model answers and the reading recommendations are new content. Check each item: whether the model answer is correct, and whether it suits your community and your movement. The level is shown in parentheses.

### 1. Foundations

- [ ] `osnovy-bd1` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: read the words of Ruth, ‘your people are my people, and your G-d is my G-d,’ and explain what they mean to you personally. Why is the people named before G-d?” — a new question about the words of Ruth; the order “the people first” is in the lesson, but the personal wording of the model answer should be confirmed with a rabbi.
- [ ] `osnovy-bd2` (all levels): “The rabbi asks: The Mishnah says that Moshe received the Torah from Sinai and passed it on down the chain of generations. What does it mean to you to enter this chain of transmission?” — a new question about the chain of Avot 1:1; the note for Reform is taken from the lesson — check whether this is how it sounds at a Reform beit din.
- [ ] `osnovy-bd3` (all levels): “At the beit din they ask: how do you personally understand the Rambam’s last principles — the coming of Mashiach and the resurrection of the dead? Do you believe this literally?” — a new question about principles 12–13. The notes on the movements draw on the Pittsburgh Platform of 1885 and “Emet Ve-Emunah” (1988), as in the lesson; later Reform documents are milder — the rabbi will confirm whether it is fitting to answer this way today.
- [ ] `osnovy-bd4` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: what is the covenant between G-d and the people of Israel, and what does it mean to you personally to enter this covenant through giyur?” — a new question about the covenant and “na'aseh ve-nishma”; check that the answer speaks of readiness to enter the covenant, and does not claim that all the mitzvot have already been accepted.
- [ ] `osnovy-bd5` (all levels): “The rabbi asks you to tell, in your own words, the main milestones in the history of the Jewish people — from Avraham to our own day.” — a new question with a brief list of milestones. The dates 586 BCE and 70 CE are given in the lesson according to scholarly chronology; the traditional Jewish reckoning is different — the rabbi will say which dates to expect.
- [ ] `osnovy-bd6` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: which mitzvot are you already trying to keep in everyday life? Give examples of ‘between a person and G-d’ and ‘between a person and a fellow.’” — a new question about personal practice; the examples are deliberately modest. The community’s rabbi will check what his movement expects of a candidate at this stage.
- [ ] keyIdeas:osnovy-1: the formula “Masorti recognizes a Reform giyur if there were brit milah and tevilah” compresses the lesson’s caveat (“it depends on whether there were”); the rabbi will confirm whether there are other conditions.

### 2. Hebrew and prayer

- [ ] `ivrit-bd1` (all levels): “Why should an adult, a candidate for giyur, learn the Hebrew alphabet? Tell how you yourself are learning the letters.” — the beit din’s expectations and the timeframe “two to three weeks, 15 minutes at a time” are taken from the lesson with the caveat “usually.” Check against the practice of the particular beit din.
- [ ] `ivrit-bd2` (all levels): “Read the word בָּרוּךְ aloud and tell everything you know about it.” — the analysis of בָּרוּךְ has been corrected according to the lesson (bet with a kamatz, resh, shuruk, final kaf; a sheva under the kaf is written but not read). Please confirm that this is a convenient way to explain it to beginners.
- [ ] `ivrit-bd3` (all levels): “What are nekudot, and why are they needed if the letters are already enough? How do you practice reading with the vowel points?” — a daily line from the siddur, the centuries of the Masoretes, and the caveat about an unfamiliar text — according to the text of the lesson.
- [ ] `ivrit-bd4` (all levels): “Which pronunciation is customary in your community — Ashkenazi or Israeli? Why do you keep to that one in particular?” — the Ashkenazi “o,” “oy” (Lithuanian “ey”) and “s” have been checked against the lesson. The model answer does not choose a nusach for the candidate: at the beit din the person names the custom of their own community.
- [ ] `ivrit-bd5` (all levels): “What do the words of ‘Ve-ahavta’ mean to you — ‘and love the L-rd with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your substance’? How does this show in your life?” — the three measures of love have been restored to the lesson’s wording (the good and the evil inclination, Mishnah Berachot 9:5). The personal part is “I try,” without a claim that the candidate already puts on tefillin.
- [ ] deeper, Pirkei Avot 2:13: the quotation has been brought into line with the verbatim text of the lesson; the mishnah number is as in the section.
- [ ] deeper, “Shaarei Shalom”: the school page does not claim a separate analysis of the siddur; “Mishkan T'filah” is named, according to the lesson, as the movement’s siddur.
- [ ] deeper, R. Donin: the topics are named in general terms, without chapter or page numbers; the book is in sources, minLevel — orthodox. The exact chapter titles of the Russian edition were not checked.

### 3. Shabbat

- [ ] `shabbat-bd1` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘The mitzvah of Shabbat is stated in two words — “remember” and “keep.” Explain in your own words what each of them means and how you fulfill both in your life.’” — a new question about “remember” and “keep”; in differs the Reform understanding of “keep” is briefly conveyed — check that it does not narrow the answer for an Orthodox candidate.
- [ ] `shabbat-bd2` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘Tell about lighting the Shabbat candles: when and how you do it, why at least two candles are lit, and what this moment means to you.’” — a new question about the candles; the differs about lighting after sunset in Reform practice is not stated directly in the lesson text — check the wording.
- [ ] `shabbat-bd3` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘Imagine that on Friday evening you are invited to dinner at the home of relatives who do not keep Shabbat. What will you do?’” — the scenario of dinner with relatives who do not observe is not discussed directly in the lesson (there, one is a guest in a religious family); check the boundary “do not light after sunset” and the tactfulness of the answer.
- [ ] `shabbat-bd4` (Conservative and Orthodox): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘Tell how you yourself keep Shabbat: what you refrain from, how you handle work, the phone and travel, and what is hardest for you.’” — a new practice question at the conservative level; check the differs on the CJLS responsum of 1950 (light and travel only for prayer; Israeli Masorti did not accept travel) against the lesson on the movements.
- [ ] `shabbat-bd5` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘Why is Shabbat not simply ended, but seen off with a separate ceremony? What does havdalah mean to you?’” — a new question about the meaning of havdalah; check that the spices and the fire are not mixed up, and that the reference to the Rambam matches the lesson.
- [ ] deeper:1: in “To Be a Jew,” Shabbat is one chapter, not several; the rabbi should confirm that the candidate has the same edition.
- [ ] deeper:4: on ru.chabad.org the topics of the melachot, electricity, muktzeh and the eruv are present, but the set of articles changes — check that what is found will be enough for the candidate.
- [ ] deeper:5: a brief Masorti overview may not discuss the 1950 responsum; this is already stated in the text, and the rabbi should confirm that the reference does not promise anything extra.
- [ ] keyIdeas:shabbat-4: muktzeh is compressed to “one does not move them without need” — check against the lesson’s caveat about tools.
- [ ] keyIdeas:shabbat-6: the wording of the CJLS responsum and of the Israeli Masorti position is sensitive with regard to the movements — check against the text of the lesson.

### 4. Kashrut

- [ ] `kashrut-bd1` (all levels): “How will kashrut enter your everyday life? If the home is not yet fully kosher, where will you begin?” — a new question; check the gradual order of entering kashrut and the wording of the Reform distinction, so that it does not cancel the obligation for the other movements.
- [ ] `kashrut-bd2` (all levels): “When you are a guest, you are offered pork, shrimp and a fish fillet without skin. What will you choose, and why?” — a new question; check the everyday examples — pig, shrimp, a fillet without skin — and that the Reform distinction is stated as a personal choice, and not as other signs.
- [ ] `kashrut-bd3` (all levels): “You are told that the special slaughter is cruelty and that it is simpler to buy ordinary meat. What will you answer about shechitah and about the prohibition of blood?” — a new question; check that shechitah is explained as a mitzvah and as an expression of the prohibition of needless suffering, without details that are not in the lesson.
- [ ] `kashrut-bd4` (all levels): “Is it permitted to cook meat with milk if you yourself will not eat it, but will give it to an animal or sell it?” — a new question; check the three prohibitions, including the prohibition of benefit, and the Reform distinction about personal choice, without softening the norm of the other movements.
- [ ] `kashrut-bd5` (Conservative and Orthodox): “Cold cheese was placed on a clean meat plate, and a non-kosher soup was cooked earlier in a metal pot. What will you do in each case?” — a new question; check the boundary of cold contact and the advice not to decide for oneself cases with a sharp food or with lying for a long time.
- [ ] deeper:2: the text has been brought into line with the lesson’s quotation — Avot 3:3 (in some editions 3:4); leave the reader both numberings, as in the section.
- [ ] deeper:3: in the edit, shechitah was removed — the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch does not set out the laws of slaughter; only the salting of meat (ch. 36) and utensils (ch. 37) were kept, which the section attributes to the Kitzur; the practical details are still to be checked with a rabbi, and the concise code does not replace asking in a particular case.
- [ ] deeper:5: the Masorti overview of the more lenient decisions on wine, cheese and the food of non-Jews should be checked against the position of the particular community.
- [ ] keyIdeas:kashrut-3: the retelling includes salting and the broiling of liver — in the lesson this block is marked as Orthodox; check how to present it at the other levels.
- [ ] keyIdeas:kashrut-5: the retelling includes tevilat kelim and the prohibitions on food prepared by a non-Jew; in the lesson they are of the Orthodox level, and the wording should be checked.
- [ ] keyIdeas:kashrut-6: the positions of the three movements and the prohibition of stam yeinam are brought together; check that the differences are conveyed as the community holds them.

### 5. Festivals

- [ ] `prazdniki-bd1` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘How do you find out when a festival begins and ends, and for how many days you keep it?’” — a new question; check the boundaries of the day, the custom of the second day, and that the candidate takes the number of days from their own community.
- [ ] `prazdniki-bd2` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘How will you spend Rosh Hashanah, and why listen to the shofar on this day?’” — a new question beyond the lesson’s minimum: there were no open questions about the shofar; check the mitzvah of hearing and the caveat about Shabbat.
- [ ] `prazdniki-bd3` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘How will you welcome Sukkot: what will you do in the sukkah and with the four species?’” — a new question; check the description of the sechach, the six directions of the four species, and the date of Simchat Torah in Reform communities.
- [ ] `prazdniki-bd4` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘What will you do at the seder, and what does it mean to you to see yourself as having come out of Egypt?’” — a new question beyond the minimum: there was no personal seder among the lesson’s open questions; check the number of seders and the connection with Shavuot.
- [ ] `prazdniki-bd5` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘How will you mark Purim? Which mitzvot will you fulfill on this day?’” — a new question; check the four mitzvot of Purim and the words of the Sages that it is better to spend more on the poor.
- [ ] `prazdniki-bd6` (all levels): “A member of the beit din asks: ‘How will you keep the fasts, and how will you mark Yom HaShoah and Yom HaAtzmaut?’” — a new question; check the distinction among the fasts and the retelling of the communities’ positions on Yom HaAtzmaut, including Reform Jews’ personal choice about the fast.
- [ ] deeper:2: the titles of articles in the ORT Electronic Jewish Encyclopedia (EEE) are taken from the links of the questions in this section; check that these articles are enough for going deeper.
- [ ] deeper:3: “Jewish Literacy” is recommended without page numbers; check that the Russian edition has a coherent overview of the festivals.
- [ ] deeper:4: the Ashkenazi-Chabad siddur “Gates of Prayer” is named; check whether the caveat is sufficient for communities with another siddur.
- [ ] deeper:5: the chapter numbers of the Kitzur are gathered from the section’s citations; check the numbering in A. Kutukov’s translation.
- [ ] keyIdeas:prazdniki-1: the second day in the Diaspora and the Reform calendar are summed up in one sentence; check that both parts are stated accurately.
- [ ] keyIdeas:prazdniki-2: check the brief sentence about selichot (the end of Elul and the Sephardi custom), and that the shofar on Shabbat is treated in the bd2 answer, and not in the brief thoughts.
- [ ] keyIdeas:prazdniki-4: kitniyot and the difference among the movements are not in the brief thoughts; check whether a difference important for the beit din is lost.
- [ ] keyIdeas:prazdniki-5: the custom that women do not work is placed beside the prohibition on using the light of the candles; check that this is precisely a custom, and not a prohibition of work.
- [ ] keyIdeas:prazdniki-6: the communities’ positions on Yom HaAtzmaut are compressed into one sentence; check that the retelling does not distort anyone.

### 6. Life cycle and family

- [ ] `semya-bd1` (all levels): “If a son is born in your family, how will you arrange the brit milah, and why will you not turn it into a celebration for the sake of the guests?” — a new question; check that the personal plan for the brit milah matches the eighth day, the role of the father and the mohel, and the prohibition on postponing the day for the sake of the guests.
- [ ] `semya-bd2` (all levels): “You are accepting Judaism as an adult, and a child may lie ahead. What does the age of the mitzvot mean to you, and how will you prepare a son or a daughter for it?” — a new question; check the boundary of 13 years and one day and of 12 years and one day, and the wording that for an adult ger the duty of the mitzvot begins when giyur is completed.
- [ ] `semya-bd3` (all levels): “Why do you need a Jewish marriage in particular, and not only a life together? How do you picture your chuppah?” — a new personal question about one’s own chuppah, which was not in the lesson; check that kiddushin, the ketubah, the seven blessings and the broken glass are conveyed without extra details.
- [ ] `semya-bd4` (all levels): “What is shalom bayit to you, and how will you keep peace in the home on an ordinary weekday?” — a new question; check that the words about the Shechinah and about the honor of a wife are close to Sotah 17a, Bava Metzia 59a and Yevamot 62b.
- [ ] `semya-bd5` (Orthodox only): “How will the laws of family purity be reflected in your married life, and from whom will you learn the details?” — a new question about intimate halacha; check the tone, the scope of the harchakot, and the advice to learn from a woman mentor, and not alone from a book.
- [ ] `semya-bd6` (all levels): “If a marriage has to be dissolved, what role does a get play for you alongside a civil divorce, and how will you protect a woman from the position of an agunah?” — a new question; check the balance of the get and a civil divorce, the status of an agunah, and the Reform caveat.
- [ ] `semya-bd7` (all levels): “If someone close to you passes away, how will you arrange the funeral and the days of shiva? What will you do if you are called to offer comfort even before giyur is completed?” — a new question; check the words about cremation according to the movements, and the advice to comfort by one’s presence even before giyur is completed.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-1: the Reform caveat about brit milah for a ger is compressed into one sentence — check that it does not sound stricter or milder than the lesson.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-2: the difference in bat mitzvah among Masorti, Reform and Orthodoxy — check the wording about an aliyah and a tallit.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-3: mixed marriages and the prohibition on a kohen marrying a giyoret — check that the movements’ positions are not mixed together.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-5: the three positions on taharat ha-mishpachah — check that what is obligatory in Orthodoxy has not been carried over to Masorti and Reform.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-6: the Lieberman clause, the Reform view of the get, and the abolition of yibbum together with chalitzah — check the wording.
- [ ] keyIdeas:semya-7: cremation in Orthodoxy, Masorti and Reform — check that no position has been made stricter than the lesson.
- [ ] deeper:4: the reference to Telushkin’s “Jewish Literacy,” without page numbers — check that the Russian edition has an account of the life cycle.
- [ ] deeper:5: chapters 163, 164 and 11 of the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch are named according to the lesson’s references — check the numbering in A. Kutukov’s translation.

### 7. Ethics

- [ ] `etika-bd1` (all levels): “Shimon HaTzaddik says that the world stands on the Torah, on service, and on good deeds. How will these three pillars look in your own life?” — a new question; avodah is explained according to the lesson — service in the Temple and, in a later understanding, prayer; check whether this wording is enough for the beit din.
- [ ] `etika-bd2` (all levels): “Imagine that after giyur someone in the community reproaches you for your past, or says that you are ‘not a real Jew.’ How will you take this, and what will you answer?” — a new question; check the tone of the answer to a reproach in the community, and the link between love of the ger and the prohibition of revenge in the portion Kedoshim.
- [ ] `etika-bd3` (all levels): “How will you give tzedakah in practice? How much, to whom, and in what way?” — a new question; the maaser in the answer is a personal plan of a tithe, and differs repeats the lesson’s caveat for Reform Judaism.
- [ ] `etika-bd4` (all levels): “In the community’s group chat people have begun to discuss an unpleasant story about one of its members. What will you do?” — a new question; the conditions of the exception have been checked against the lesson (verified facts, no other way, the aim is protection, in private); the rabbi should confirm the brevity of the wording.
- [ ] `etika-bd5` (all levels): “How will accepting Judaism affect your work and your money matters? What does kiddush Hashem at work mean to you?” — a new question; payment “on the same day” is narrowed to a day laborer, as in the lesson; check the everyday example of kiddush Hashem.
- [ ] `etika-bd6` (all levels): “Your parents are not Jews. How will you honor them after giyur?” — a new question beyond the two open ones already present; the lesson does not give a separate halacha on honoring a ger’s non-Jewish parents — the answer is softened to gratitude and the example of Dama ben Netina, and it is for the rabbi to decide whether a more precise caveat is needed.
- [ ] deeper:5: the Masorti overview is recommended in keeping with the sense of the lesson; the about-masorti page itself does not discuss tikkun olam, and the principle is in the movement’s FAQ. I did not check the Russian Derakhim page line by line.
- [ ] keyIdeas:etika-6: the emphases of the three movements are retold according to the lesson (Reform — alongside the Torah and prayer; Masorti — within halacha; Orthodox — the mitzvot and holiness); check the wording.

### 8. Beit Din

- [ ] `beit-din-bd1` (all levels): “What does immersion in the mikveh mean to you? Why does giyur end precisely with tevilah?” — a new question about the meaning of tevilah — check that the answer speaks of giyur being completed by immersion and of the blessing after it, and that the note about Reform communities in which tevilah is not required is correct for your community.
- [ ] `beit-din-bd2` (all levels): “Do you have fillings, a tattoo, or a medical device? What will you do about this before the immersion?” — a new question about chatzitzah — check how your beit din decides questions of fillings, tattoos, and medical devices. The lesson says that many of them are not considered a chatzitzah.
- [ ] `beit-din-bd3` (all levels): “What Hebrew name have you chosen, and why?” — a new question about choosing a name — check the naming custom (“bat Avraham Avinu” or “bat Avraham ve-Sarah”) in your community.
- [ ] `beit-din-bd4` (all levels): “You were already circumcised in childhood. Why, then, do you need hatafat dam brit?” — a new question about hatafat dam brit. The level has been left as reform, because in ROOPI and in Israel brit milah for men is part of giyur, while in North American Reform it is formally not required; the lesson flashcard about medical circumcision is marked as conservative. Check whether the level should be raised.
- [ ] `beit-din-bd5` (all levels): “Your relatives celebrate their religious holidays and invite you. What will you do after giyur?” — a new question about family religious holidays — the limits of participation depend on the movement and on the particular situation.
- [ ] deeper:4: “Giyur school of a Progressive community” — this is a description of the Shaarei Shalom school (ROOPI), and not of all Progressive communities; requirements in other countries may differ.
- [ ] keyIdeas:beit-din-1: the wording of kabbalat mitzvot has been softened: according to the Talmud one accepts the whole system, while the Reform movement understands the commitment differently. Check the balance.
- [ ] keyIdeas:beit-din-4: in the lesson the volume of the mikveh is “40 se'ah, by various calculations several hundred liters”; in the flashcards this point is marked as Orthodox. Check whether it is appropriate among the general key ideas.

<!-- new-questions:start -->
## New questions in the bank (November 2026)

The question bank has roughly doubled. Each new question is tied to a lesson in its section; one AI tool wrote it and another checked it, but a rabbi has not yet seen them. Below are all the new questions with their answers; mark the ones you have checked with `[x]`. Corrections may be sent as text.

<!-- sec:01-osnovy -->
### Foundations

- [ ] `osnovy-n1` (easy, reform) Of what origin was Rut, who became the model giyoret in the Tanakh? — answer: “A Moabite woman” *Why to check:* the fact of Rut’s origin — check the wording of the question on the Tanakh
- [ ] `osnovy-n2` (medium, conservative) What, according to the Talmud (Yevamot 47a), does the beit din ask a candidate for giyur first of all? — answer: “What did you see, that you came to accept giyur? Do you not know that the people of Israel are now oppressed and persecuted?” *Why to check:* the accuracy of the translation of the beit din’s question from the Talmud (Yevamot 47a)
- [ ] `osnovy-n3` (hard, reform) What did the CCAR resolution of 1983 change in the Reform definition of who is a Jew? — answer: “It recognized as a Jew the child of either parent who is a Jew, when the child is raised in a Jewish identity” *Why to check:* a difference among the movements — the CCAR decision of 1983 on patrilineal descent
- [ ] `osnovy-n4` (medium, reform) What, in the words of the Sages, did the whole people of Israel “undergo” at Mount Sinai on the day the Torah was given? — answer: “Giyur” *Why to check:* a midrashic saying about giyur at Sinai — check the source
- [ ] `osnovy-n5` (easy, reform) The Torah forbids wronging a ger by word or by deed, and reminding him of his origin. — answer: true *Why to check:* the verses forbidding the oppression of the ger (Shemot 22:20; Vayikra 19:33–34)
- [ ] `osnovy-n6` (hard, reform) According to one opinion in the Talmud, the Torah cautions about careful treatment of the ger only three times. — answer: false *Why to check:* the number 36 of warnings about the ger (Bava Metzia 59b) — the number and the source
- [ ] `osnovy-n7` (medium, conservative) What is the name of the symbolic drawing of a drop of blood in place of brit milah, for a man who was circumcised before giyur? — answer: “Hatafat dam brit” *Why to check:* an Orthodox detail of the rite — hatafat dam brit
- [ ] `osnovy-n8` (easy, reform) Match the concept from the topic of giyur with its meaning. — pairs: Rut → Great-grandmother of King David; Korban → A sacrifice at giyur, only while the Temple stood; Devarim 10:19 → The mitzvah to love the stranger; Shemaya and Avtalyon → Teachers descended from gerim *Why to check:* the terminology of the statuses of the ger — clarify the definitions
- [ ] `osnovy-n9` (hard, conservative) A member of the beit din asks: “Why did they at first try to dissuade you from giyur? What custom is this?” Answer. *Why to check:* the talmudic custom of dissuading the candidate (Yevamot 47a; Rambam 14:1–2)
- [ ] `osnovy-n10` (easy, reform) Who writes a sefer Torah — the scroll for reading in the synagogue? — answer: “A specially trained scribe — a sofer” *Why to check:* the term “sofer” — check the accuracy
- [ ] `osnovy-n11` (easy, reform) What are the five books of Ketuvim called — Shir HaShirim, Rut, Eichah, Kohelet, and Esther? — answer: “The Five Megillot” *Why to check:* the makeup of the Five Megillot — check against the lesson
- [ ] `osnovy-n12` (medium, reform) What are the sages of the Mishnah era called? — answer: “Tannaim” *Why to check:* periodization — the names of the generations of sages
- [ ] `osnovy-n13` (hard, reform) Which part of the Shulchan Aruch is devoted to marriage? — answer: “Even HaEzer” *Why to check:* the exact correspondence of the parts of the Shulchan Aruch — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n14` (easy, reform) The yearly cycle of Torah reading in the synagogue is completed and begun again on the festival of Simchat Torah. — answer: true *Why to check:* a detail of custom — the completion of the reading cycle on Simchat Torah
- [ ] `osnovy-n15` (hard, reform) The Jerusalem Talmud is more extensive and more authoritative than the Babylonian Talmud. — answer: false *Why to check:* the comparison of the Jerusalem and Babylonian Talmuds — the assessment of authority
- [ ] `osnovy-n16` (easy, reform) What is the name of the Torah scroll written by hand on parchment for reading in the synagogue? — answer: “Sefer Torah” *Why to check:* the term “sefer Torah” — check the spelling of the answer options
- [ ] `osnovy-n17` (hard, reform) Match the megillah with the day on which it is read. — pairs: Shir HaShirim → Pesach; Rut → Shavuot; Eichah → 9 Av; Kohelet → Sukkot; Esther → Purim *Why to check:* the periodization of the scholars (Tannaim, Amoraim, Rishonim, Acharonim) — the boundaries of the eras
- [ ] `osnovy-n18` (medium, reform) A member of the beit din asks: “After the Mishnah there were further generations of teachers. Who are the Amoraim, the Rishonim, and the Acharonim?” Answer. *Why to check:* the status of the Oral Torah — a difference among the movements, a sensitive formulation
- [ ] `osnovy-n19` (easy, reform) What, according to the Mishnah (Sanhedrin 10:1), does all Israel have? — answer: “A share in the World to Come” *Why to check:* the quotation from Mishnah Sanhedrin 10:1 — check the accuracy
- [ ] `osnovy-n20` (medium, reform) Which of the 13 principles speak of the Torah and of prophecy? — answer: “Principles 6–9” *Why to check:* the numbering of the groups of principles (6–9) — check that it matches the Rambam
- [ ] `osnovy-n21` (hard, reform) Which of these teachers, according to the lesson, built a system of the foundations of faith in the 14th–15th centuries differently from the Rambam? — answer: “Rabbi Hasdai Crescas” *Why to check:* a historical detail about Rabbi Hasdai Crescas — check the dating
- [ ] `osnovy-n22` (easy, reform) Judaism is purely a “religion of deed”: Jewish life has no worldview as its foundation. — answer: false *Why to check:* the formulation about a “religion of deed” — check that it matches the lesson
- [ ] `osnovy-n23` (hard, reform) Faith in the resurrection of the dead is expressed in the second blessing of the Amidah: “…You revive the dead.” — answer: true *Why to check:* a ritual detail — the second blessing of the Amidah, on the resurrection
- [ ] `osnovy-n24` (easy, reform) What is the one Hebrew word for the foundations of faith (the principles)? — answer: “Ikkarim” *Why to check:* the term “ikkarim” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n25` (hard, reform) Match the number of a principle of the Rambam with its content. — pairs: 2 → Unity: G-d is one, and there are no other gods; 4 → Eternity: He was before everything and will endure forever; 6 → Prophecy: G-d reveals His will through the prophets; 9 → Immutability: this Torah will not be replaced by another; 11 → Reward for those who fulfill and punishment for those who transgress *Why to check:* a comparison of the thinkers’ views — check the sources
- [ ] `osnovy-n26` (medium, reform) A member of the beit din asks: “Explain in your own words the first of the 13 principles — the existence of the Creator.” Answer. *Why to check:* a personal formulation of the first principle for the beit din
- [ ] `osnovy-n27` (hard, reform) How, according to the Rambam, should one regard a Jew who accepts the foundations of faith but sins out of weakness? Answer and explain. *Why to check:* the Rambam’s position on one who sins out of weakness — check the quotation
- [ ] `osnovy-n28` (easy, reform) When, by tradition, is the Shema recited? — answer: “In the morning and in the evening” *Why to check:* the custom of the time for reciting the Shema — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n29` (easy, reform) What sign did G-d set for the covenant with Noach and with all humanity? — answer: “The rainbow” *Why to check:* the verses about the covenant with Noach (Bereshit 9:8–17) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n30` (medium, reform) In what do the Sages see the special merit of the people’s answer at Sinai, “na'aseh ve-nishma”? — answer: ““We will do” was spoken before “we will hear” — the commitment was taken before the details were known” *Why to check:* a talmudic detail about “na'aseh ve-nishma” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n31` (hard, orthodox) What does the majority of halachic authorities say about women reciting the Shema? — answer: “Women are exempt from this time-bound mitzvah, but they should at least recite the first verse” *Why to check:* women’s obligations in reciting the Shema — the difference of opinions and customs
- [ ] `osnovy-n32` (medium, reform) The phrase “Baruch shem kevod malchuto le'olam va'ed” after the first verse of the Shema is usually said in a whisper. — answer: true *Why to check:* a ritual custom — “Baruch shem” in a whisper
- [ ] `osnovy-n33` (medium, reform) It is permitted to depict G-d in human form if the image is respectful. — answer: false *Why to check:* the prohibition of depicting G-d — the halachic formulation
- [ ] `osnovy-n34` (easy, reform) What is the name of an act that glorifies G-d in the eyes of people (two words)? — answer: “Kiddush Hashem” *Why to check:* the term “kiddush Hashem” — check the spelling
- [ ] `osnovy-n35` (medium, reform) Match the concept with its meaning. — pairs: Elohim → G-d as Judge and Master of the forces of nature; Genizah → Burial of texts that contain holy Names; Chillul Hashem → An act that brings disgrace upon G-d; Kabbalat ol malchut shamayim → Accepting the yoke of the Kingdom of Heaven when reciting the Shema *Why to check:* the definitions of the names and concepts (Elohim, genizah, kiddush/chillul Hashem) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n36` (hard, reform) A member of the beit din asks: “The Talmud teaches one to imitate G-d. What does this mean in ordinary life?” Answer. *Why to check:* the talmudic quotation about imitating G-d (Sotah 14a) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n37` (easy, reform) From where, according to tradition, did Avraham, the forefather of the Jewish people, come? — answer: “From Ur Kasdim in Mesopotamia” *Why to check:* a historical detail about Ur Kasdim — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n38` (easy, reform) Who led the people of Israel after Moshe and brought them into Canaan? — answer: “Yehoshua bin Nun” *Why to check:* a historical detail about Yehoshua bin Nun — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n39` (medium, reform) From where, according to the lesson, does the word “Jew” (Yehudi) come? — answer: “From the tribe of Yehudah (Judah)” *Why to check:* the etymology of the word “Jew” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n40` (easy, reform) G-d brought the people out of Egyptian slavery after the ten plagues and the splitting of the Red (Reed) Sea. — answer: true *Why to check:* the verses about the ten plagues and the Exodus (Shemot 12–14) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n41` (hard, reform) Egypt destroyed the northern Kingdom of Israel in 722 BCE, and its inhabitants were scattered. — answer: false *Why to check:* the date 722 BCE and Assyria — a historical detail
- [ ] `osnovy-n42` (medium, reform) What is the Hebrew word for exile, the diaspora (one word)? — answer: “Galut” *Why to check:* the term “galut” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n43` (hard, reform) Match the center of Jewish life with its distinctive feature. — pairs: Babylonia → The Babylonian Talmud was created here; Muslim Spain → The “Golden Age” of poetry and philosophy; Ashkenazim → Germany, France, then Poland, Lithuania, Russia; Sephardim → Descendants of the Jews of Spain and Portugal after the expulsion of 1492 *Why to check:* the geography and the terms of the diaspora (Ashkenazim, Sephardim) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n44` (hard, reform) How did the people preserve the teaching after the Romans destroyed the Second Temple? Answer as you would at the beit din. *Why to check:* the ger’s personal readiness to accept the history of the people
- [ ] `osnovy-n45` (hard, reform) What does the Haggadah’s requirement mean, to see oneself “as if you yourself had come out of Egypt”? Answer and explain. *Why to check:* the interpretation of the Haggadah’s requirement — check the wording
- [ ] `osnovy-n46` (easy, reform) What is a mitzvah in the exact sense of the word? — answer: “A commandment, a command from G-d” *Why to check:* the definition of a mitzvah — terminological accuracy
- [ ] `osnovy-n47` (medium, reform) Why does each Jew fulfill only a part of the 613 mitzvot? — answer: “Many are connected with the Temple, with sacrifices, with Eretz Israel in the Temple era, with the king and the Sanhedrin, or apply only to Kohanim, to men, or to women” *Why to check:* a halachic detail about the portion of the mitzvot that are fulfilled — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n48` (hard, reform) With which Hebrew letters is the number 613 written, from which comes the expression “taryag mitzvot”? — answer: “With the letters TARYAG” *Why to check:* the number 613 and the letters TARYAG — the number and the source
- [ ] `osnovy-n49` (hard, reform) What does the law of “ever min ha-chai,” one of the seven laws of the descendants of Noach, forbid? — answer: “Eating flesh cut from a living animal” *Why to check:* the exact translation of “ever min ha-chai” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n50` (easy, reform) The blessing “…who has sanctified us with His mitzvot and commanded us…” is recited only on Yom Kippur. — answer: false *Why to check:* the custom of the blessing over the mitzvot — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n51` (hard, conservative) In a doubt concerning a mitzvah of the Torah one acts stringently, and in a doubt concerning a mitzvah of the Sages one acts leniently. — answer: true *Why to check:* the rule of doubts de-oraita/de-rabbanan — a halachic detail
- [ ] `osnovy-n52` (easy, reform) What are the mitzvot instituted by the Sages called, as distinct from the mitzvot of the Torah itself? — answer: “De-rabbanan” *Why to check:* the term “de-rabbanan” — check the spelling
- [ ] `osnovy-n53` (medium, reform) Match the term with its meaning. — pairs: Mitzvot aseh → Prescriptive mitzvot — “do”; Mitzvot lo ta'aseh → Prohibitive mitzvot — “do not do”; Dinim → Establish courts and just laws; Bnei Noach → Descendants of Noach, for whom the seven laws were given *Why to check:* the classification of the mitzvot — clarify the definitions
- [ ] `osnovy-n54` (hard, reform) A member of the beit din asks: “Tell us about Akiva’s “great principle of the Torah” and about Hillel’s words. How do they differ?” Answer. *Why to check:* the comparison of the sources of Akiva and Hillel — check the quotations
- [ ] `osnovy-n55` (easy, reform) Which events of the 18th–19th centuries led to the rise of the movements of Judaism? — answer: “The emancipation of the Jews in Europe and the Haskalah — the Jewish Enlightenment” *Why to check:* history — emancipation and the Haskalah as the cause of the movements
- [ ] `osnovy-n56` (easy, reform) Who founded the movement of Reconstructionism? — answer: “Rabbi Mordecai Kaplan” *Why to check:* the term Reconstructionism — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n57` (medium, reform) Which body decides questions of Jewish law in Conservative (Masorti) Judaism? — answer: “The Committee on Jewish Law and Standards of the Rabbinical Assembly (CJLS)” *Why to check:* the structure of the CJLS in Masorti — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n58` (hard, reform) How does Sephardi Orthodoxy differ from Ashkenazi Orthodoxy in its halachic sources? — answer: “It follows the Shulchan Aruch without the glosses of the Rema, and the traditions of the communities of the East” *Why to check:* Sephardi custom (the Shulchan Aruch without the Mappah) — a detail of custom
- [ ] `osnovy-n59` (hard, reform) Since 1950 the Conservative movement has permitted driving a car to the synagogue on Shabbat. — answer: true *Why to check:* the date and the Masorti practice of 1950 (a car on Shabbat) — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n60` (medium, reform) Modern Orthodoxy observes more lenient laws of Shabbat and kashrut than Haredi communities. — answer: false *Why to check:* a comparison of Modern Orthodoxy and Haredim — a sensitive formulation
- [ ] `osnovy-n61` (easy, reform) What are traditional Jews in Israel called who observe much of the tradition but do not belong to the movement (one word)? — answer: “Masortim” *Why to check:* the term “Masortim” — check
- [ ] `osnovy-n62` (medium, reform) The rabbi asks: “Explain how the movements of Judaism differ in their understanding of the Torah and in the binding force of halacha.” Answer. *Why to check:* the difference among the movements in the understanding of revelation — a sensitive formulation
- [ ] `osnovy-n63` (hard, reform) An acquaintance says: “I want giyur, but I will choose the shortest path — after all, all giyurim are the same.” What will you answer him? *Why to check:* recognition of the giyur of the different movements — check the accuracy
<!-- /sec -->
<!-- sec:02-ivrit -->
### Hebrew and Prayer

- [ ] `ivrit-n1` (easy, reform) What is the Hebrew alphabet called — by its first two letters? — answer: "Alef-bet" *Why check:* the name "alef-bet" and the comparison with the Greek sequence — check that the wording is appropriate.
- [ ] `ivrit-n2` (easy, reform) Which letter is the smallest, and the word "Yisrael" begins with it? — answer: "Yud — the letter 'y'" *Why check:* the image of yud as a symbol of humility — this is the lesson's traditional interpretation; the wording should be checked.
- [ ] `ivrit-n3` (medium, reform) Why, in Russian spelling, does one say "Rosh a-Shana," although in Hebrew the letter hei is there? — answer: "Hei is a light aspiration, and in writing it often drops out" *Why check:* the rendering of hei in the transcription "Rosh a-Shana" and "a-motzi" — check it against the spelling accepted in the community.
- [ ] `ivrit-n4` (hard, reform) What is the gematria of the word "chai" ("living") equal to, and which letters produce it? — answer: "Eighteen: chet — 8 and yud — 10" *Why check:* the number 18 and the custom of giving sums in multiples — check how this is presented to the candidate.
- [ ] `ivrit-n5` (hard, reform) Which year from the creation of the world does the lesson write with the letters תשפ״ז? — answer: "The year 5787; the thousands are usually omitted" *Why check:* the example תשפ״ז = 5787 and the rule of omitting the thousands — check the number and the wording about the calendar.
- [ ] `ivrit-n6` (easy, reform) Hebrew writing has no capital letters. — answer: true *Why check:* the statement that there are no capital letters — a short check against the lesson.
- [ ] `ivrit-n7` (hard, reform) A final kaf with a dagesh inside is the ordinary form at the end of a word. — answer: false *Why check:* the rarity of a final kaf with a dagesh, as against an ordinary sheva — a precise orthographic detail.
- [ ] `ivrit-n8` (medium, reform) Match the letter with the mark of its shape. — pairs: Dalet → A sharp angle with a projection; Reish → A rounded head; Samekh → The line is rounded at the bottom; Final mem → A square with right angles; Lamed → It rises above the line *Why check:* the distinguishing marks of similar letters — check that the descriptions do not diverge from the typeface accepted in the siddur.
- [ ] `ivrit-n9` (easy, reform) By what hint-word does one remember the five letters that have a final form? — answer: "MaNTzePaKh" *Why check:* the mnemonic "MaNTzePaKh" and the order of the letters in it — check the accepted pronunciation.
- [ ] `ivrit-n10` (easy, reform) The beit din asks: why do you need to distinguish the printed type of the siddur from handwritten cursive? *Why check:* the name "ktav ashuri" and what the beit din expects of reading — check the tone of the answer.
- [ ] `ivrit-n11` (hard, reform) Explain at the beit din why one and the same sound in Hebrew is often written with different letters. *Why check:* the statement that an ancient difference among the sounds has been preserved in the spelling — the lesson's historical formulation.
- [ ] `ivrit-n12` (easy, reform) Where is full vowel pointing printed, so that one can learn to read from it? — answer: "In the siddur, the Chumash, and children's books" *Why check:* the list of editions with vowel pointing (the siddur, the Chumash, children's books) — a check against the lesson.
- [ ] `ivrit-n13` (medium, reform) In which syllable does even the Israeli reading give kamatz the sound "o"? — answer: "In a closed unstressed syllable — this is kamatz katan" *Why check:* the rule of kamatz katan in a closed unstressed syllable, and the examples "kol" and "chokhmah."
- [ ] `ivrit-n14` (hard, reform) How is the word רוּחַ read, and what becomes of the vowel under the last letter? — answer: "'Ruach': the hidden patach is sounded before the chet" *Why check:* the reading "ruach" and the rule of patach gnuva before a final chet and ayin.
- [ ] `ivrit-n15` (hard, reform) Which letters, as a rule, do not take a dagesh? — answer: "The gutturals alef, hei, chet, ayin, and reish" *Why check:* the list of letters without a dagesh: the gutturals and reish — check the wording "as a rule."
- [ ] `ivrit-n16` (easy, reform) Kamatz and patach, in Israeli pronunciation, give one and the same sound, "a." — answer: true *Why check:* the Israeli "a" of kamatz and patach, as against the Ashkenazi "o" — a difference of pronunciations.
- [ ] `ivrit-n17` (easy, reform) A sheva at the end of a word is always pronounced as the vowel "e." — answer: false *Why check:* a silent sheva at the end of a word, and the traditional dots under a final chaf.
- [ ] `ivrit-n18` (medium, reform) A chataf segol under the alef in the word "Eloheinu" gives a very short "e." — answer: true *Why check:* the analysis of "Eloheinu": a chataf segol under the alef, and the syllabic reading.
- [ ] `ivrit-n19` (easy, reform) Match each vowel point with how it looks in writing. — pairs: Kamatz → A mark like a small "t"; Patach → A single horizontal line; Tzere → Two dots side by side; Segol → Three dots in a triangle; Kubutz → Three slanted dots under the letter *Why check:* the descriptions of the shapes of the signs — check them against how they are drawn in the community's siddur.
- [ ] `ivrit-n20` (easy, reform) What is writing without vowel points called, where vav and yud suggest the vowels? — answer: "Ktiv male" *Why check:* the term "ktiv male" and the role of vav and yud in a text without vowel points.
- [ ] `ivrit-n21` (hard, reform) At the beit din you are asked to analyze the sheva: when does it sound, and when is it silent? *Why check:* the three cases of a vocal sheva, and silence at the end of a word — the precise reading rule.
- [ ] `ivrit-n22` (hard, reform) The beit din asks: where does the stress usually fall in Hebrew, and what sets segolate words apart? *Why check:* segolate stress and the role of the te'amim — check the examples "melech" and "eretz."
- [ ] `ivrit-n23` (easy, reform) How does the Shema differ from a request addressed to the Almighty? — answer: "It is a proclamation of unity, and not a request" *Why check:* the characterization of the Shema as a proclamation, and not a request — a theological formulation.
- [ ] `ivrit-n24` (easy, reform) What does one do with the right hand while saying the first verse of the Shema? — answer: "One covers one's eyes with it, so as not to be distracted" *Why check:* the custom of covering the eyes with the right hand at the first verse — a minhag; it should be confirmed.
- [ ] `ivrit-n25` (hard, reform) How many blessings surround the full reading of the Shema in the morning and in the evening? — answer: "In the morning two before and one after, in the evening two before and two after" *Why check:* the number of brachot around the Shema in the morning (2+1) and in the evening (2+2) — an exact count; check it against the siddur.
- [ ] `ivrit-n26` (hard, orthodox) What is required for the first verse of the Shema to count as the mitzvah? — answer: "Kavanah: without a thought of the meaning, the mitzvah is not fulfilled" *Why check:* the requirement of kavanah for the first verse of the Shema — a halachic detail of Kitzur Shulchan Aruch 17.
- [ ] `ivrit-n27` (medium, reform) "Baruch Shem" was first said by the High Priest — in response to the Shema of Yaakov's sons. — answer: false *Why check:* two traditions about "Baruch Shem" — Yaakov's response and the Temple response — do not mix them up.
- [ ] `ivrit-n28` (hard, orthodox) It is preferable to recite the evening Shema before midnight, and in an extreme case one may do so until dawn. — answer: true *Why check:* the times for the evening Shema: after the stars appear, preferably before midnight, the latest time is dawn.
- [ ] `ivrit-n29` (easy, reform) Match each word of the first verse of the Shema with the meaning the lesson gives. — pairs: Shema → Not only to hear, but to accept; Yisrael → The whole people and each person; Eloheinu → Our G-d, the G-d of Israel; Echad → One, united, and indivisible *Why check:* the interpretation of the words of the first verse, especially "shema" as "accept" and "echad" as indivisibility.
- [ ] `ivrit-n30` (easy, reform) What is the reading of the Shema that is said before sleep called? — answer: "Shema al ha-mitah" *Why check:* the name "Shema al ha-mitah" and the connection with the blessing "Ha-Mapil."
- [ ] `ivrit-n31` (medium, reform) The beit din asks: in what order do the three passages of the Shema come, and why does the Mishnah arrange them that way? *Why check:* the order of the three passages and Reform practice with the second passage in "Mishkan T'filah."
- [ ] `ivrit-n32` (hard, orthodox) Whom does halacha obligate to recite the Shema in the morning and in the evening, and what is customary among those who are exempt from this obligation? *Why check:* the obligation of men, the exemption of women, and the custom of the first verse — halacha and minhag.
- [ ] `ivrit-n33` (easy, reform) What does the Talmud call the Amidah when it speaks simply of "prayer"? — answer: "Ha-Tefillah — 'Prayer' without further specification" *Why check:* the term "Ha-Tefillah" as the name of the Amidah in the language of the Sages.
- [ ] `ivrit-n34` (easy, reform) Which of the three daily prayers is the shortest in its composition? — answer: "Mincha: Ashrei, the Amidah, and the concluding prayers" *Why check:* the composition of Mincha, and that it is the shortest of the three daily prayers.
- [ ] `ivrit-n35` (medium, reform) Which prayer is added on Yom Kippur beyond Shacharit, Mincha, Maariv, and Musaf? — answer: "Ne'ilah — the prayer of the 'locking of the gates'" *Why check:* Ne'ilah as the fifth prayer of Yom Kippur, and the meaning of the name "locking of the gates."
- [ ] `ivrit-n36` (hard, reform) Who, according to the Talmud, set the text of the Amidah in order at the beginning of the Second Temple era? — answer: "The Men of the Great Assembly" *Why check:* the authorship of the order of the Amidah — the Men of the Great Assembly, Megillah 17b, not the Patriarchs.
- [ ] `ivrit-n37` (hard, orthodox) Until what time is it better to say the morning Amidah, and what time is still permitted? — answer: "Better until the end of the fourth hour, the latest time — noon" *Why check:* the fourth hour and noon as the times for the morning Amidah — Mishnah Berachot 4:1; do not confuse this with the time for the Shema.
- [ ] `ivrit-n38` (medium, reform) On Shabbat the middle section of the Amidah is filled with the same thirteen requests as on weekdays. — answer: false *Why check:* the omission of the weekday requests in the Shabbat Amidah, and one middle blessing about the sanctity of the day.
- [ ] `ivrit-n39` (hard, reform) Before the Amidah one takes three steps back and three forward, and at the end, three steps back. — answer: true *Why check:* the number and the order of the steps before the Amidah and at the end — a custom; it should be confirmed.
- [ ] `ivrit-n40` (medium, reform) Match each blessing of the weekday Amidah with its theme. — pairs: Gevurot → Might, life, and rain; Modim → Gratitude for the miracles of each day; Shalom → A request for peace; Avodah → Service and the return of the Shekhinah to Zion; Who hears prayer → A request that the prayer be heard *Why check:* the themes of Gevurot, Avodah, Modim, Shalom, and "Who hears prayer" — check the names against the community's siddur.
- [ ] `ivrit-n41` (medium, reform) What is the repetition of the Amidah aloud called, which the shaliach tzibbur leads after the silent prayer? — answer: "Chazarat ha-shatz" *Why check:* the term "chazarat ha-shatz," and that the repetition belongs to matters that require a minyan.
- [ ] `ivrit-n42` (hard, reform) From which word of the Torah do the Sages derive that ten people are needed for a "matter of holiness"? — answer: "Edah" *Why check:* the derivation of the minyan from the word "edah" in Bamidbar 14:27 — the source should be checked.
- [ ] `ivrit-n43` (easy, reform) The beit din asks: which way do you face in the Amidah, and whose example set its whisper? *Why check:* the direction toward Jerusalem and the Temple Mount, and the example of Chana — check the wording.
- [ ] `ivrit-n44` (easy, reform) What must be uttered in a bracha, otherwise it does not count as complete? — answer: "The Name of the Almighty and Kingship — 'Melech ha-olam'" *Why check:* the rule that without the Name and Kingship a bracha is not complete — the lesson's halachic formulation.
- [ ] `ivrit-n45` (medium, reform) Which bracha does one say when washing the hands from a vessel before a meal with bread? — answer: "'Al netilat yadayim' — on the washing of the hands" *Why check:* the bracha "al netilat yadayim," specifically before a meal with bread and from a vessel.
- [ ] `ivrit-n46` (hard, reform) Who, according to tradition, composed the first three blessings of Birkat HaMazon? — answer: "Moshe — food, Yehoshua — the Land, David and Shlomo — Jerusalem" *Why check:* the tradition about the authorship of the first three brachot of Birkat HaMazon — Moshe, Yehoshua, David, and Shlomo.
- [ ] `ivrit-n47` (hard, orthodox) How often does a sofer check a household mezuzah, and why? — answer: "Twice in seven years: the letters can become damaged" *Why check:* the interval for checking a mezuzah — twice in seven years, Yoma 11a, and the reason: damage to the letters.
- [ ] `ivrit-n48` (medium, reform) If a meal was begun with bread, separate brachot are usually not said over the other dishes. — answer: true *Why check:* the rule that "ha-motzi" covers the meal — the practical halacha of food.
- [ ] `ivrit-n49` (hard, orthodox) "Shehakol" is chosen in advance for any food, even when the correct bracha is known. — answer: false *Why check:* the distinction between bediavad and lechatchila for "shehakol" — a fine point of halacha; a check is needed.
- [ ] `ivrit-n50` (easy, reform) Match each thanksgiving after eating with the situation from the lesson. — pairs: After a meal with bread → Birkat HaMazon; Three adults or more → Zimmun before the blessing; Food in which there was no bread → Borei nefashot or Al ha-michyah; The fourth bracha after bread → "Who is good and does good," from Yavneh *Why check:* zimmun from three adults and the fourth bracha from Yavneh; the lesson does not separate "Borei nefashot" and "Al ha-michyah" by kinds of food.
- [ ] `ivrit-n51` (easy, reform) How does a woman say the first morning words of gratitude for the soul that has been returned? — answer: "Modah ani" *Why check:* the feminine form "Modah ani," and that it is said before netilat yadayim.
- [ ] `ivrit-n52` (medium, reform) At what moment do you say "Shehecheyanu," and for what does this bracha give thanks? *Why check:* the occasions for "Shehecheyanu," especially the day of giyur, where the lesson says explicitly that customs differ, and the Reform status of the brachot.
- [ ] `ivrit-n53` (hard, orthodox) Within what period, outside Israel, does one affix a mezuzah in rented housing, and on which openings is it not placed? *Why check:* 30 days outside Israel (Menachot 44a), the exceptions of the bathroom and the toilet, and checking twice in seven years (Yoma 11a).
- [ ] `ivrit-n54` (easy, reform) Which lamp in the synagogue is a reminder of the Temple menorah? — answer: "Ner tamid — the lamp that does not go out" *Why check:* ner tamid as a remembrance of the Temple menorah — check the name and the meaning.
- [ ] `ivrit-n55` (medium, reform) How does nusach Sefard differ from nusach Edot HaMizrach? — answer: "Sefard — the nusach of the Hasidim, Edot HaMizrach — Sephardim and the East" *Why check:* the confusion of nusach Sefard (Hasidim) and Edot HaMizrach (Sephardim) — the names of the customs.
- [ ] `ivrit-n56` (hard, orthodox) How many windings between the knots of the tzitzit do Ashkenazim make? — answer: "Seven, eight, eleven, and thirteen" *Why check:* the Ashkenazi windings 7, 8, 11, and 13, and five double knots — the numbers and the custom.
- [ ] `ivrit-n57` (hard, orthodox) How many blessings over tefillin do Ashkenazim recite, and how many do Sephardim? — answer: "Ashkenazim — two, Sephardim — one, on the hand tefillin" *Why check:* two Ashkenazi blessings over tefillin as against one Sephardi blessing — a difference of custom.
- [ ] `ivrit-n58` (easy, reform) The synagogue replaces the Temple, and therefore it is called the great Sanctuary. — answer: false *Why check:* the wording "mikdash me'at": the synagogue does not replace the Temple.
- [ ] `ivrit-n59` (medium, orthodox) All Jews begin to wear the large tallit only after marriage. — answer: false *Why check:* when one begins to wear a tallit gadol: after marriage among some Ashkenazim, from bar mitzvah among Sephardim and German Jews.
- [ ] `ivrit-n60` (hard, orthodox) The strap of the hand tefillin is wound around the forearm seven times. — answer: true *Why check:* seven windings on the forearm, and a different direction among Ashkenazim, Sephardim, and Hasidim.
- [ ] `ivrit-n61` (medium, reform) Match the words of prayer with what they refer to. — pairs: Lehitatef ba-tzitzit → The bracha when one wraps oneself in the tallit; Lehaniach tefillin → The bracha for putting on tefillin; Mah tovu → The verse on entering the synagogue; Mikdash me'at → The synagogue as a small Sanctuary *Why check:* the wording of the brachot for the tallit and for tefillin, and the verse "Mah tovu" — check them against the siddur.
- [ ] `ivrit-n62` (hard, orthodox) What is the women's gallery in an Orthodox synagogue called? — answer: "Ezrat nashim" *Why check:* the term "ezrat nashim" alongside the mechitzah — the arrangement of an Orthodox synagogue.
- [ ] `ivrit-n63` (easy, reform) The beit din asks: is a kippah a mitzvah of the Torah? Why do you cover your head? *Why check:* the status of the kippah as a custom, four cubits, and "the Shekhinah is above me" — Kiddushin 31a.
<!-- /sec -->
<!-- sec:03-shabbat -->
### Shabbat

- [ ] `shabbat-n1` (easy, reform) What does the root sh-b-t mean, from which the word "Shabbat" comes? — answer: "To cease, to stop" *Why check:* check the wording of the meaning of the root sh-b-t.
- [ ] `shabbat-n2` (easy, reform) When does Shabbat begin? — answer: "On Friday, at sunset" *Why check:* the time Shabbat begins — make sure the wording does not contradict tosefet Shabbat.
- [ ] `shabbat-n3` (medium, reform) Why is Friday evening already considered Shabbat? — answer: "Because the Jewish day begins in the evening: 'and there was evening, and there was morning'" *Why check:* the basis for the day beginning in the evening, from Bereshit 1:5.
- [ ] `shabbat-n4` (hard, reform) Which Jewish thinker called Shabbat "a palace in time"? — answer: "Rabbi Abraham Joshua Heschel" *Why check:* the attribution of the image "a palace in time" to Rabbi Heschel.
- [ ] `shabbat-n5` (hard, orthodox) To what does the Kitzur Shulchan Aruch (72:1) liken one who violates Shabbat? — answer: "To one who denies the entire Torah" *Why check:* the accuracy of the rendering of Kitzur Shulchan Aruch 72:1 about a public violator of Shabbat.
- [ ] `shabbat-n6` (easy, reform) On Shabbat it is customary to fast, in order to emphasize the sanctity of the day. — answer: false *Why check:* the rule about fasting on Shabbat, and the exception of Yom Kippur.
- [ ] `shabbat-n7` (hard, reform) The words "More than the Jews have kept Shabbat, Shabbat has kept the Jews" belong to Ahad Ha'am. — answer: true *Why check:* the attribution of the quotation to Ahad Ha'am, and the date of the essay.
- [ ] `shabbat-n8` (hard, reform) Match each place in the Tanakh with what is said there about Shabbat. — pairs: Bereshit 2:3 → G-d blessed and sanctified the seventh day; Shemot 20:8 → "Remember the Shabbat day"; Devarim 5:12 → "Keep the Shabbat day"; Shemot 31:16–17 → Shabbat is a sign and an eternal covenant; Yeshayahu 58:13 → "You shall call Shabbat a delight" *Why check:* the correspondence of the verses and their content (verse numbers).
- [ ] `shabbat-n9` (hard, reform) What are the Ten Commandments — the "ten utterances" — called in Hebrew? — answer: "Aseret Ha-Dibrot" *Why check:* the transliteration "Aseret Ha-Dibrot" and acceptable variants.
- [ ] `shabbat-n10` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "The Torah calls Shabbat a sign between the Almighty and the people of Israel. How does this sign show in your life?" *Why check:* the model answer about the "sign" — check whether it is appropriate for the candidate.
- [ ] `shabbat-n11` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "What is menuchah? How does your Shabbat differ from an ordinary day off?" *Why check:* the model answer about menuchah and about oneg/kavod Shabbat.
- [ ] `shabbat-n12` (easy, reform) What do Ashkenazim call the traditional hot dish for the Shabbat lunch? — answer: "Cholent" *Why check:* the names of the Shabbat hot dish by community (custom).
- [ ] `shabbat-n13` (medium, reform) What do the worshippers do during the last stanza of "Lecha Dodi" — "Bo'i ve-shalom"? — answer: "They stand, turn toward the entrance of the synagogue, and bow" *Why check:* the custom at the stanza "Bo'i ve-shalom" — communities may differ.
- [ ] `shabbat-n14` (hard, reform) Which psalms open Kabbalat Shabbat in the Ashkenazi rite — one for each weekday? — answer: "Tehillim 95–99 and 29" *Why check:* the set of psalms of Kabbalat Shabbat in the Ashkenazi rite (the numbers).
- [ ] `shabbat-n15` (hard, orthodox) From what time, according to halacha, may one accept Shabbat before sunset? — answer: "From plag ha-mincha — about an hour and a quarter of 'seasonal' hours before sunset" *Why check:* the definition of plag ha-mincha and "an hour and a quarter of seasonal hours."
- [ ] `shabbat-n16` (easy, reform) According to the Talmud, the great Sages assigned preparation for Shabbat to servants and did not take part in it themselves. — answer: false *Why check:* the rendering of the account in Shabbat 119a about the Sages' preparation.
- [ ] `shabbat-n17` (medium, reform) On the eve of Shabbat, Rabbi Chanina would put on his best clothes and say: "Come, let us go to greet Queen Shabbat." — answer: true *Why check:* the attribution of the words to Rabbi Chanina (Shabbat 119a).
- [ ] `shabbat-n18` (hard, reform) Many Sephardim, following the Shulchan Aruch, first light the Shabbat candles and only afterward recite the blessing. — answer: false *Why check:* the order of the blessing over the candles among Sephardim — the custom differs.
- [ ] `shabbat-n19` (easy, reform) Match each part of the preparation for Shabbat with what it includes. — pairs: Food → Everything that needs to be cooked is cooked before Shabbat; The home → A white tablecloth, candlesticks, two challot under a napkin; Body and clothing → A shower, cutting the nails, festive clothing; Affairs → Pay the bills, notify colleagues, set the phone aside; Tzedakah → Coins into the box before lighting the candles *Why check:* the elements of preparation, including the custom of tzedakah before the candles.
- [ ] `shabbat-n20` (easy, reform) What is the name of the hymn ("Go, my friend") with which, in Kabbalat Shabbat, one welcomes the Shabbat bride? — answer: "Lecha Dodi" *Why check:* acceptable spellings of "Lecha Dodi."
- [ ] `shabbat-n21` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "The Sages of the Talmud prepared for Shabbat with their own hands. Why is preparation part of the mitzvah, and what do you yourself do for Shabbat?" *Why check:* the model answer and the sources Avodah Zarah 3a / Shabbat 119a.
- [ ] `shabbat-n22` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "In the synagogue on Friday evening, Shabbat is welcomed as a bride and a queen. Where does this image come from, and how do you experience the Kabbalat Shabbat service?" *Why check:* the model answer about Kabbalat Shabbat and the image of the bride.
- [ ] `shabbat-n23` (easy, reform) With what words are daughters blessed on Friday evening? — answer: "'May G-d make you like Sarah, Rivka, Rachel, and Leah'" *Why check:* the formula of the blessing of daughters.
- [ ] `shabbat-n24` (easy, reform) What is sprinkled on the Shabbat challah after the blessing "ha-motzi"? — answer: "Salt" *Why check:* the custom of salting the challah, and its basis.
- [ ] `shabbat-n25` (medium, reform) What, in most communities, follows immediately after kiddush? — answer: "Washing the hands (netilat yadayim)" *Why check:* the order of washing the hands relative to kiddush — differences of custom, and Reform practice.
- [ ] `shabbat-n26` (hard, reform) Who composed the Aramaic Shabbat hymn "Ya Ribon Olam" ("Master of the world")? — answer: "Rabbi Yisrael Najara" *Why check:* the attribution of "Ya Ribon Olam" to R. Yisrael Najara.
- [ ] `shabbat-n27` (hard, orthodox) Over what, according to halacha, does one recite the Friday-evening Shabbat kiddush if there is neither wine nor grape juice? — answer: "Over two challot" *Why check:* kiddush over challot when there is no wine, and chamar medinah — check the halachic accuracy.
- [ ] `shabbat-n28` (medium, reform) If one person recites kiddush and the others listen and answer "amen," they too fulfill the mitzvah. — answer: true *Why check:* fulfilling kiddush by listening and answering "amen."
- [ ] `shabbat-n29` (hard, orthodox) According to halacha, one may recite kiddush in one place and eat the meal itself later in another house. — answer: false *Why check:* the rule of "kiddush be-makom seudah."
- [ ] `shabbat-n30` (easy, reform) Match each element of the Shabbat meal with its description. — pairs: "Shalom Aleichem" → A greeting to the ministering angels; "Eshet Chayil" → "A woman of valor" (Mishlei 31); Lechem mishneh → Two whole challot on the table; Zmirot → Shabbat songs between the courses; "Retzei" → The insertion about Shabbat rest in Birkat HaMazon *Why check:* the description of the elements of the meal, including the insertion "Retzei."
- [ ] `shabbat-n31` (easy, reform) What is the third Shabbat meal called, the one eaten toward Shabbat evening? — answer: "Seudah shlishit" *Why check:* variant spellings of "seudah shlishit."
- [ ] `shabbat-n32` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "What happens at your Shabbat table besides the food — which songs do you sing, and what do you talk about?" *Why check:* the model answer about zmirot and words of Torah.
- [ ] `shabbat-n33` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "Do you have a custom of blessing the children on Friday evening? How is it done, and what is said then?" *Why check:* the formulas for blessing the children, and their place in the order of the dinner.
- [ ] `shabbat-n34` (easy, conservative) What does the verse Shemot 35:3 directly forbid? — answer: "To kindle a fire in all dwellings on the Shabbat day" *Why check:* the accuracy of the quotation of Shemot 35:3.
- [ ] `shabbat-n35` (easy, conservative) Which of the things listed does one do at once on Shabbat, for the sake of pikuach nefesh? — answer: "They take a woman in labor to the hospital" *Why check:* examples of pikuach nefesh — make sure the distractors clearly do not belong to saving a life.
- [ ] `shabbat-n36` (medium, orthodox) Squeezing juice from fruit is considered a derivative (toladah) of which primary melachah? — answer: "Threshing (dash)" *Why check:* the classification of squeezing juice as a derivative of "threshing."
- [ ] `shabbat-n37` (medium, orthodox) What is shevut? — answer: "A fence-prohibition of the Sages (for example, not to engage in trade)" *Why check:* the definition of shevut, and examples.
- [ ] `shabbat-n38` (easy, conservative) The Torah itself directly lists all 39 kinds of work forbidden on Shabbat. — answer: false *Why check:* the wording that the Torah does not list the 39 melachot.
- [ ] `shabbat-n39` (medium, orthodox) Food, dishes, books, and clothing are muktzeh, and therefore one may not move them on Shabbat. — answer: false *Why check:* which objects are not muktzeh.
- [ ] `shabbat-n40` (hard, orthodox) Connected with the eruv techumin is the measure of travel permitted on Shabbat beyond the city — 2000 cubits. — answer: true *Why check:* eruv techumin and the measure of 2000 cubits.
- [ ] `shabbat-n41` (hard, orthodox) Match each halachic term with its meaning. — pairs: Reshut ha-yachid → Private domain: an enclosed house and courtyard; Reshut ha-rabim → Public domain: a large street; Karmelit → An intermediate domain; Tzurat ha-petach → "The form of a doorway" — the fence of an eruv; Eruv tavshilin → It allows one, on a Festival, to prepare food for Shabbat *Why check:* the definitions of the domains, tzurat ha-petach, and eruv tavshilin.
- [ ] `shabbat-n42` (easy, orthodox) What are the objects called which the Sages forbade moving on Shabbat (Heb. "set aside")? — answer: "Muktzeh" *Why check:* the term muktzeh and variant spellings.
- [ ] `shabbat-n43` (hard, conservative) A member of the beit din asks: "On Shabbat your neighbor suddenly has severe chest pain. What will you do? What is your decision based on?" *Why check:* the model answer about pikuach nefesh, and the references to Orach Chaim 328:2.
- [ ] `shabbat-n44` (easy, reform) When does Shabbat end? — answer: "When three medium stars are visible in the sky" *Why check:* the end of Shabbat — three medium stars; communities' calculations differ.
- [ ] `shabbat-n45` (medium, reform) About how long does Shabbat last from its beginning until the stars come out? — answer: "About 25 hours" *Why check:* the length of Shabbat, "about 25 hours" — it depends on custom and place.
- [ ] `shabbat-n46` (hard, reform) Whose opinion underlies the custom of many Hasidim to wait 72 minutes after sunset before Shabbat ends? — answer: "Rabbeinu Tam" *Why check:* the attribution of the 72 minutes to the opinion of Rabbeinu Tam.
- [ ] `shabbat-n47` (hard, orthodox) Until what time may one make up havdalah if it was not done on Saturday evening? — answer: "Until the end of Tuesday, but without the candle and the spices" *Why check:* making up havdalah until Tuesday, without the candle and the spices.
- [ ] `shabbat-n48` (easy, reform) After havdalah one sings the song "Eliyahu HaNavi," about the prophet who, according to tradition, will come to announce the redemption. — answer: true *Why check:* the custom of singing "Eliyahu HaNavi" after havdalah.
- [ ] `shabbat-n49` (hard, orthodox) If Shabbat passes directly into a Festival, havdalah is included in the Festival kiddush, and spices are not used. — answer: true *Why check:* havdalah when Shabbat passes into a Festival — check the wording.
- [ ] `shabbat-n50` (easy, reform) Match each havdalah blessing with what it is said over. — pairs: Borei pri ha-gafen → A cup of wine; Borei minei vesamim → Spices (among Ashkenazim); Borei me'orei ha-esh → A candle with several wicks; Ha-mavdil bein kodesh le-chol → The main blessing over the cup at the end *Why check:* the texts of the havdalah blessings; among Sephardim the blessing over the spices is sometimes different.
- [ ] `shabbat-n51` (easy, reform) What are the spices one smells at havdalah called in Hebrew? — answer: "Besamim" *Why check:* variant spellings of "besamim."
- [ ] `shabbat-n52` (hard, orthodox) What is the name of the prayer of separation inserted into the fourth blessing of the Amidah of the evening service at the close of Shabbat? — answer: "Atah Chonantanu" *Why check:* the place of "Atah Chonantanu" in the Amidah, and variant spellings.
- [ ] `shabbat-n53` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "How does the evening go for you after havdalah? What do 'Shavua tov' and melaveh malkah mean?" *Why check:* the model answer about melaveh malkah and the connection with King David.
- [ ] `shabbat-n54` (easy, reform) What is found in the communities of all three movements — Orthodox, Conservative (Masorti), and Reform? — answer: "Lighting candles, kiddush, challot, and havdalah" *Why check:* the wording of what is common to all the movements — the difference between the movements.
- [ ] `shabbat-n55` (easy, reform) With what, according to the sample path in the lesson, is it best to begin keeping Shabbat? — answer: "With Friday dinner: candles, kiddush, challot" *Why check:* the recommended first step toward keeping Shabbat.
- [ ] `shabbat-n56` (medium, reform) Why do Orthodox families usually live within walking distance of the synagogue? — answer: "Because it is forbidden to travel by vehicle on Shabbat" *Why check:* the reason for living near the synagogue — Orthodox practice.
- [ ] `shabbat-n57` (hard, reform) Who were the authors of the "Responsum on Shabbat" of the CJLS in 1950? — answer: "Rabbis Morris Adler, Jacob Agus, and Theodore Friedman" *Why check:* the authors of the CJLS responsum of 1950 — the difference between the movements.
- [ ] `shabbat-n58` (easy, reform) Almost all rabbis advise beginning to keep Shabbat gradually, adding something new every few weeks. — answer: true *Why check:* the advice to keep Shabbat gradually — different rabbis may take a different approach.
- [ ] `shabbat-n59` (medium, reform) The CJLS responsum of 1950 permitted driving a car on Shabbat not only to the synagogue, but also to pay a visit or to go shopping. — answer: false *Why check:* the limits of the CJLS 1950 permission to travel.
- [ ] `shabbat-n60` (hard, reform) Match each document with its content. — pairs: The Pittsburgh Platform (1885) → Ritual laws that do not accord with the views of modern civilization are not obligatory; The CJLS responsum (1950) → Travel to the synagogue for prayer, and electric light; "Gates of Shabbat" → A CCAR guide to Shabbat observance; Statement of Principles (1999) → A call to study and fulfill the mitzvot, and to sanctify Shabbat *Why check:* the content of the documents of Reform and of the Conservative (Masorti) movement, and the dates.
- [ ] `shabbat-n61` (hard, orthodox) Which tractate of the Talmud says that a non-Jew should not observe Shabbat in full? — answer: "Sanhedrin" *Why check:* the source Sanhedrin 58b about a non-Jew and Shabbat.
- [ ] `shabbat-n62` (medium, orthodox) A member of the beit din asks: "Why is it important to us to see that you spend Shabbat in the community? How does this happen for you?" *Why check:* the model answer about the Orthodox requirements for the candidate, and differences among the movements.
- [ ] `shabbat-n63` (easy, reform) A member of the beit din asks: "Many people say that the hardest thing on Shabbat is giving up the phone. How do you handle this?" *Why check:* the model answer about a digital rest — whether it is appropriate for the beit din.
<!-- /sec -->
<!-- sec:04-kashrut -->
### Kashrut

- [ ] `kashrut-n1` (easy, reform) What, according to the Talmud, atones for a person now that there is no Temple and no altar? — answer: "A person's table" *Why check:* the wording of Berakhot 55a about the table in place of the altar.
- [ ] `kashrut-n2` (easy, reform) To what does halacha apply the word "kasher"? — answer: "To everything fit according to the law: to food, a Torah scroll, a mezuzah, a shofar, and a mikveh" *Why check:* the list of objects called "kasher" in halacha.
- [ ] `kashrut-n3` (easy, reform) Why is it customary at a meal to put salt on the bread? — answer: "In memory of the offerings in the Temple, which were salted" *Why check:* the custom of salt on bread and the connection with Vayikra 2:13.
- [ ] `kashrut-n4` (hard, reform) Who objected to explaining kashrut by hygiene alone, saying that then the Torah would be a medical book? — answer: "Abarbanel" *Why check:* attribution of the objection to Abarbanel against the hygienic explanation.
- [ ] `kashrut-n5` (medium, reform) According to Pirkei Avot, three who ate at one table and spoke words of Torah are as if they ate from the table of the Almighty. — answer: true *Why check:* the status of kashrut before and after giyur; there is a note about the Reform position.
- [ ] `kashrut-n6` (medium, reform) The Ramban explains the prohibition of birds of prey by saying that their meat is harmful to health. — answer: false *Why check:* accuracy in conveying the Ramban's explanation of Vayikra 11:13.
- [ ] `kashrut-n7` (medium, reform) The laws of shechita and of forbidden food are set out in detail in the Talmud tractate Berakhot. — answer: false *Why check:* the recommended order of a gradual transition to kashrut.
- [ ] `kashrut-n8` (easy, reform) Match the group of mitzvot with its description. — pairs: Mishpatim → Clear to reason: the prohibition of theft and murder; Edot → They recall events of history: the festivals; Chukim → Decrees without an explanation: the red heifer *Why check:* the division of the mitzvot into mishpatim, edot, and chukim, and the examples.
