- [ ] `beit-din-n42` (hard, orthodox) According to Orthodox practice, how is the body positioned during immersion, and what does the word “kosher” mean? *Why check:* the position of the eyes and lips at tevilah — check against the custom of the beit din; the lesson gives a general formula
- [ ] `beit-din-n43` (medium, reform) You are offered the chance to undergo tevilah in a river near your home, without asking a rabbi. What will you do? *Why check:* a river as a mikveh — the lesson’s caveat is broad; the practical decision rests only with a rabbi
- [ ] `beit-din-n44` (easy, reform) Who, in the words of the lesson, literally repeats the path of the forefather by undergoing brit milah? — answer: “An adult ger” *Why check:* repeating the path of Avraham — check the wording
- [ ] `beit-din-n45` (easy, reform) Whom does tradition call “the father of gerim”? — answer: “Avraham” *Why check:* Avraham is the father of gerim — check
- [ ] `beit-din-n46` (medium, reform) Why is the tevilah of a male ger usually performed only several weeks after brit milah? — answer: “Because after circumcision time is needed for healing” *Why check:* healing before tevilah is a detail from the lesson; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n47` (hard, reform) How does the milah of a ger differ in its timing from the milah of a Jewish infant? — answer: “A ger’s milah is not performed on Shabbat or on festivals, whereas an infant is circumcised on the eighth day even on Shabbat” *Why check:* an infant’s milah on Shabbat has exceptions (illness, certain births); the lesson does not discuss them
- [ ] `beit-din-n48` (easy, reform) Brit milah for an adult ger is performed by a mohel — a specialist who knows the laws of milah, often a physician, under local anesthesia. — answer: true *Why check:* the role of the mohel is a detail from the lesson; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n49` (medium, reform) Hatafat dam brit is a repeated full circumcision of a man who is already circumcised. — answer: false *Why check:* the essence of hatafat dam brit — check against Shabbat 135a
- [ ] `beit-din-n50` (easy, reform) Match the idea connected with a ger’s name with its description. — pairs: “Ben Avraham Avinu” → The patronymic of a male ger; “Bat Avraham ve-Sarah” → A variant of the patronymic that includes the matriarch’s name; Ketubah → The marriage contract, in which the ger’s name is written; “One hundred and twenty years” → An expression about the end of life: the name is then written on the tombstone *Why check:* “one hundred and twenty years” as an expression about death, not as an age for the inscription
- [ ] `beit-din-n51` (easy, reform) What is the “covenant of circumcision” called in Hebrew? — answer: “Brit milah” *Why check:* the term “brit milah” — check
- [ ] `beit-din-n52` (medium, reform) Why is a ger’s brit milah not an ordinary medical operation? What in the rite itself must be correct? *Why check:* “according to many opinions — three” at the milah, not as an undisputed law for every beit din
- [ ] `beit-din-n53` (easy, reform) Why does a ger bear the patronymic “son (daughter) of Avraham,” and not by the name of his or her non-Jewish parents? *Why check:* the patronymic after Avraham is a custom plus a letter of the Rambam; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n54` (hard, reform) How many times, according to the Talmud, does the Torah warn against wronging a ger? — answer: “36 times, according to another opinion — 46” *Why check:* the counts of 36 and 46 in Bava Metzia 59b are different tallies of verses, not to be memorized as the only one
- [ ] `beit-din-n55` (medium, reform) Loving the ger is, according to the Rambam, how many mitzvot? — answer: “Two” *Why check:* the two mitzvot of love for the ger are a detail of the Rambam; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n56` (easy, reform) What, according to the Mishnah (Bava Metzia 4:10), may one not say to a person who has repented of his past? — answer: ““Remember your former deeds”” *Why check:* the prohibition on reminding someone of the past is a detail of the Mishnah; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n57` (hard, orthodox) What restriction on marriage applies to a giyoret under Orthodox halacha? — answer: “She cannot marry a kohen” *Why check:* the prohibition on a giyoret marrying a kohen is Orthodox halacha; Masorti has other responsa, and the lesson does not cite them
- [ ] `beit-din-n58` (easy, reform) A ger has the same communal rights as a Jew by birth: he is called to the Torah, he counts in a minyan, and he may become a rabbi or the head of a community. — answer: true *Why check:* the lesson explicitly gives the ger a call to the Torah, a place in the minyan, and the possibility of being a rabbi or the head of a community; some authorities place a limit on a ger’s communal authority — the word “head” is worth checking
- [ ] `beit-din-n59` (easy, reform) The Book of Ruth is read on Shavuot — on the festival of the giving of the Torah. — answer: true *Why check:* the reading of Ruth on Shavuot is a detail; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n60` (easy, reform) What is “hurt with words” called, including reminding a ger of his past or of his origin? — answer: “Ona’at devarim” *Why check:* the term “ona’at devarim” — check
- [ ] `beit-din-n61` (medium, reform) What does the comparison of a ger to a newborn infant mean? *Why check:* the image of the newborn is an interpretation of the Talmud; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n62` (medium, reform) What will you do if someone in the community tactlessly asks who you were before giyur? *Why check:* a ger’s personal boundaries are advice from the lesson; check
- [ ] `beit-din-n63` (medium, reform) If a giyoret gives birth to a child after giyur, what will that child’s status be? *Why check:* a Jew from birth — a child born to a giyoret after giyur; not the child of a male ger and a non-Jewish woman
<!-- /sec -->
<!-- new-questions:end -->

<!-- review-doubts:start -->
## Reviewers’ doubts about the new questions (November 2026)

Each new question and each card was checked by a second tool, not the one that wrote them. Below are the places where the reviewer was not sure, or where the lesson itself requires clarification. Items that were in English have been translated word for word.

### Foundations (review: Cursor)

1. **Sinai as giyur (n4).** The lesson says that the Sages taught that the entire people “underwent giyur” at Sinai, and it does not name a source. The procedures are described in Keritot 9a (milah, tevilah, korban). Shevuot 39a, where it is said that the souls of future gerim stood at Sinai, is a different statement. The question follows the lesson’s sentence.
2. **Reminding a ger of his origin (n5).** Shemot 22:20 and Vayikra 19:33–34 forbid oppression. The specific prohibition on mentioning the past is ona’at devarim, Bava Metzia 58b. The lesson presents this as a command of the Torah. The question follows the lesson.
3. **Thirty-six warnings (n6).** In Bava Metzia 59b there are thirty-six, and according to the opinion of some, forty-six. The false statement “only three” is safe. It is worth confirming that students are supposed to remember 36.
4. **Hatafat dam brit (n7).** The lesson attributes it to Yevamot 46a–47b and to the Rambam, Issurei Biah 13–14. The procedure itself is also in Shabbat 135a and in the Rambam 14:5. The source line was left within the lesson’s range.
5. **Baruch Shem (n32).** It is said aloud on Yom Kippur. The question now says “usually.” Confirm that it is this particular qualification that should be shown to students.
6. **Women and the Shema (n31).** The lesson gives the majority opinion: women are exempt, because this is a mitzvah bound to a specific time, but they must recite at least the first verse (Mishnah Berachot 3:3). There is a real opinion that women are obligated because of kabbalat ol. In differs it says that Reform women recite the entire Shema, and that Masorti communities usually encourage them to do so. Confirm the sentence about Masorti; the lesson itself says only that “customs differ.”
7. **The sea and the Exodus (n40).** The splitting of the Yam Suf is the climax of the Exodus (Shemot 14), not an event before it. The lesson says that G-d brought the people out “after” the plagues and the splitting. The question now repeats this sentence.
8. **Even ha-Ezer (n13).** The lesson says “marriage.” This section also covers divorce; niddah is in Yoreh De’ah. The question asks only about marriage.
9. **Rambam, Kings 8:11.** The lesson, osnovy-q51, and card osnovy-6-n6 say that a righteous gentile who observes the seven laws has a share in the World to Come. The Rambam’s full condition: he observes them because G-d commanded them in the Torah. Left in the lesson’s wording.
10. **Driving on Shabbat (n59).** The lesson sets out the Conservative permission of 1950 to drive to the synagogue. It does not say that other trips were rejected, or that Israeli Masorti did not accept this responsum. The question matches the lesson.
11. **Modern Orthodox and Haredim (n60).** The lesson says that they observe the same laws of Shabbat, kashrut, and family purity. Practical pesakim and chumrot differ. The false statement “more lenient laws” follows the lesson.
12. **Recognition of giyur (n63).** Lesson 1 says that Masorti recognition of Reform giyur depends on brit milah and tevilah, and that Orthodoxy does not recognize non-Orthodox giyur. Lesson 7 does not say this. n63 remains within lesson 7. A question about the full matrix would belong to lesson 1.
13. **Crescas, Albo, Abravanel.** The lesson calls them, somewhat loosely, “contemporaries and followers” of the Rambam. They belong to the 14th–15th centuries. n21 no longer offers Albo or Abravanel as if they agreed with the Rambam’s list.
14. **Date of the Second Temple (card 5-n3).** About 516 BCE — the dedication, under Darius. The decree of Cyrus — 538. The card now separates these two dates.
15. **The CCAR and the rites (card 1-n7).** The removed date “1892” is not stated in the lesson. The usual date of the resolution that did not require milah and tevilah is 1893. The card now says only that the rites are determined by the community rabbi.

### Hebrew and prayer (review: Kimi)

1. **ivrit-n7**: the lesson's terminology ("final chaf" = with a sheva, "final kaf" = with a dagesh) is nonstandard; it is worth confirming the wording so that the candidate does not confuse the letter with its state.
2. **ivrit-n15**: "resh, as a rule, does not take a dagesh" — in the text of the Tanakh a dagesh in resh occurs rarely; the wording with the qualifier "as a rule" is correct, but it would be good to confirm it.
3. **ivrit-n25**: the count of berachot around the Shema (morning 2+1, evening 2+2) is the standard Ashkenazi nusach; check it against the siddur of the candidate's community.
4. **ivrit-n39**: three steps back/forward are a minhag, not a din; confirm that presenting this as an established custom is appropriate.
5. **ivrit-n52**: "Shehecheyanu" on the day of giyur — the lesson itself notes the difference in communities' customs; clarify the local practice.
6. **ivrit-n53**: the period of 30 days for rental housing outside Israel (Menachot 44a) — check the application in contemporary communities.
7. **ivrit-n59**: the custom "tallit gadol after the wedding" varies (Poland/Lithuania vs Germany/Sephardim); confirm the presentation.
8. **ivrit-n12**: the list "siddur, Chumash, children's books" — check it against the editions accepted in the community.
9. **ivrit-n60**: seven windings and the different direction of wrapping (Ashkenazim away from oneself, Sephardim/Hasidim toward oneself) — confirm.
10. **ivrit-n62**: "ezrat nashim" beside the mechitzah — clarify that the term refers to the gallery, and not to the partition.

### Shabbat (review: Cursor)

1. **Lesson, KSA 72.** The lesson says that a public transgressor is "in many respects" like one who denies the entire Torah, and cites only 72:1. In the Kitzur, 72:1 — any transgressor = as if he had denied the Torah; 72:2 — a public transgressor (ten Jews, or known to them) = like a non-Jew with regard to wine, bread, and cooked food. The questions now follow 72:1. The lesson's own sentence was not corrected. It is worth adding one corrective line to the section, and only then a question on 72:2.
2. **Eruv techumin.** "An extension … — 2000 cubits" in the lesson is an elementary formula. An eruv moves the point from which the 2000 cubits are counted; it does not add 2000 cubits in each direction. n40 and card 4-n5 no longer say "extends." Confirm the wording that the students are to remember.
3. **Squeezing fruit (n36).** The lesson calls this a toladah of dash. The classic de'oraita case is grapes and olives (mefarek). For other fruits it depends on whether they are ordinarily squeezed. Left in the lesson's wording.
4. **72 minutes (n46).** The lesson attributes the wait of 72 minutes to Rabbeinu Tam. This is an established custom of many Hasidim; its shiur changes with the seasons and with latitude. Left in the lesson's wording.
5. **Ahad Ha'am (n7).** This is his sentence from "Shabbat and Zionism" (Ha-Shiloah, 1898): יותר משישראל שמרו את השבת שמרה השבת אותם. The lesson says only "an essay on Shabbat (1898)." The attribution in the question is correct.
6. **Heschel's name.** In the lesson, "Avraham Yeshua Heschel." The usual Hebrew name is Avraham Yehoshua. The questions follow the lesson ("a palace in time" is correct).
7. **Shabbat leading into Yom Tov (n49, card 5-n4).** Besamim are omitted; the blessing over the candle is still recited, over a flame lit before Yom Tov (YaKNeHaZ). The lesson does not mention the candle, so the items were not expanded.
8. **Kiddush at night without wine.** Some authorities permit chamar medina at night if there is neither wine nor bread. The patch follows the lesson: challah at night; chamar medina in the morning for Ashkenazim.
9. **Salt on the challah (n24).** Honey is a distractor. On a festival that falls on Shabbat, some dip in honey. The question is about an ordinary Shabbat, as in the lesson.
10. **Women and havdalah.** The lesson says that the majority obligate women. The Rema's caution and the Ashkenazi custom regarding wine are real. The card now says "according to custom" about the wine. Confirm that this is the wording you want for the candidates.
11. **Domains.** The lesson lists three (yachid, rabbim, karmelit) and does not mention makom patur. n41 follows the lesson.
12. **Names in the 1950 responsum.** Morris Adler, Yaakov Agus, Theodore Friedman — correct. "Morris Adler" from the lesson is retained.

### Kashrut (review: Cursor)

1. **Pareve cooked in meat or dairy utensils** (lesson 4, card kashrut-4-n8 and model kashrut-q39). The lesson says that the food takes on the status of the utensil. If the utensil is not ben yomo, the taste is pagum, and many poskim permit eating this pareve with the other type. It is worth adding one sentence to the lesson.
2. **Blood in an egg (n17, card kashrut-2-c16).** The lesson says not to use the egg. Ashkenazim discard a store-bought unfertilized egg; some Sephardim remove the spot. The question follows the lesson's flat rule.
3. **Hard cheese, six months (n37).** The lesson says that this is the usual boundary. Poskim differ as to which cheeses count. The question says "usually," as the lesson does.
4. **"The sixth hour has entered" (card kashrut-4-n4).** This is a dispute within the six-hour custom, and not a fourth custom alongside 3 hours and 1 hour.
5. **Abarbanel (n4, card kashrut-1-n4).** The line "The Torah would be a medical book" is a paraphrase of the lesson. The commentary does reject a purely medical reading; there is no page in the lesson.
6. **Yayin nesech (card kashrut-6-n1).** The lesson defines it as wine *intended* for idolatry, and links it with Devarim 32:38. Classically it is wine with which a libation was actually performed; the verse is an asmachta.
7. **The temperature of mevushal** — only one sentence in lesson 6. This is no longer tested. If the rabbi wants a figure or a range, add it to the lesson first.
8. **Sturgeon and black caviar (n13).** The correct answer is supervised red salmon caviar. The lesson marks sturgeon as non-kosher "according to the Orthodox position"; some non-Orthodox opinions permitted it.
9. **Magen Tzedek (n57, card kashrut-6-n3).** The Conservative initiative of the 2000s is real. It did not become a widely used mark. The question follows the lesson.
10. **24 non-kosher birds (n15, card kashrut-2-n1).** Chullin 63b arrives at 24 by deduction; the written lists are shorter. The question quotes the lesson's Talmudic conclusion.
11. **Glatt (n29, n33).** "Strictly speaking, the lungs of cattle" and the Beit Yosef / Rema distinction match the lesson. Store "glatt" on chicken and cheese is marketing, as the lesson says.
12. **Rav Moshe Feinstein and chalav stam (n49).** He permitted milk under serious government supervision; he did not forbid it. Many communities still keep chalav Yisrael. The false statement is correct.
13. **Shechita and tza'ar ba'alei chayim (n23, card kashrut-3-n2).** The lesson presents the cut as an expression of this value. The card does not claim a modern veterinary consensus.

### Festivals (review: Claude)

1. **KSA 97** for the laws of Rosh Chodesh (n7, and in the lesson itself). Please check the siman number; the halacha itself (no repetition at Ma'ariv) corresponds to OC 422:1.
2. **n16 / prazdniki-2-n1**: should the lesson say explicitly that blowing the shofar throughout Elul is an Ashkenazi custom? Sephardim generally do not blow it that way at Selichot.
3. **prazdniki-3-n7**: in Orthodox practice children are called up together with an adult ("Kol HaNe'arim"), and the lesson does not mention this. Add it?
4. **n57**: on Tisha B'Av that falls on Shabbat and is postponed to Sunday (nidcheh), some leniencies apply. This is not in the lesson, and the question does not ask about it.
5. **n53**: the question about a woman reading the Megillah for men is shown at the Reform level with a Conservative differs. Is the Orthodox description as "a matter of dispute" acceptable?

### Life cycle and family (review: Kimi)

1. **semya-n53** (typed "sefer keritut"): it matches in fact the existing card `semya-6-c1` ("What does the Torah call the divorce document?") — the same question in another modality. Left intentionally (an examination question ≠ a review card), but if one wishes, n53 can be removed.
2. **semya-n38 / semya-n40**: the wording "according to a widespread custom of Ashkenazim and Sephardim, the count does not begin before the fifth day" is accepted practice, but for a beit din it is worth confirming that this presentation of a "custom" (and not a strict norm) is appropriate.
3. **semya-n44**: in `differs`, for Masorti and Reform, it says that immersion before the chuppah is an encouraged/voluntary practice, and not an obligatory condition. Check that this accurately reflects the movements' positions.
4. **semya-n61**: accepted spellings also include "seudat ha-havraa" — confirm the preferred Russian vocalization of the term (סעודת הבראה).
5. **semya-n27**: in the modelAnswer, the wife of a ger who married a kohen's daughter "loses the right to eat terumah" (Vayikra 22:12) — the standard presentation according to the lesson; confirm that this wording is sufficient for the examination without qualifications.
6. **semya-n2** is close in topic to the existing `semya-q5` (both about brit milah and Shabbat), but the fact is different: q5 — "the eighth day is not postponed because of Shabbat"; n2 — "one born on Shabbat is circumcised on the next Shabbat" (the count follows the Jewish day). Both are kept.

### Ethics (review: Cursor)

1. **One sixth (the lesson, card etika-5-c9).** The lesson says that an error of a sixth or more makes the sale invalid and returns the difference. Choshen Mishpat 227 distinguishes exactly 1/6 (the difference only) and more than 1/6 (voidable). The bad true/false statement was removed. The lesson's line is still in the section file.
2. **KSA addresses in the lesson.** "A true hin" as "let yes be yes" — 62:17; 62:16 — keeping one's word and a mental agreement. The lesson cites 62:16. Loading — 189 (left as is). 186 and 191 are not chapters about work on Shabbat. 63:4 is geneivat da'at, and not rental.
3. **KSA 62:10.** A heaped measure is a local custom in some places; where the custom is a level measure, one does not add a heap. etika-n43 now follows 62:9 (give a little more) and does not teach the heap as a universal rule.
4. **Mi shepara (etika-n45).** The option matches the lesson. The full KSA 62:15 also names Gomorrah and the Egyptians who drowned in the sea.
5. **Correction after harmful speech (etika-n41).** The lesson says to tell the one who heard that the words were inaccurate. The Chafetz Chaim does not permit denying a truthful report, and one does not inform the subject if he does not know. It is worth adding one sentence to the lesson.
6. **The sick (etika-n17).** Bava Metzia 58b forbids speaking to one who is suffering as the friends of Iyov spoke. The lesson extends this to "do not remind a sick person of the illness." The question follows the lesson.
7. **Gezeirah shavah (etika-n34).** Arachin 15b compares the word "great." Calling this a formal gezeirah shavah (which requires a received tradition) is the lesson's terminology; the rabbi may prefer "a comparison by a word."
8. **Four questions before repeating (etika-n42).** This is a note of the lesson, and not the seven conditions of to'elet in the Chafetz Chaim (rebuke first, without exaggeration, the harm no greater than the offense).
9. **Ma'aser ani (etika-n29).** The lesson and Devarim 26:12 name the third year. The sixth year of the cycle is also a year of the poor tithe.
10. **Ben Azzai (etika-n11).** The Sifra quotes Bereshit 5:1, "this is the book of the generations of Adam"; the image of G-d is the reason this verse is a greater principle. The question uses the lesson's shorter form.
11. **A ger's parents (etika-n63).** Mora and kavod are set out as stated. The lesson does not say whether the Torah's full obligation applies to non-Jewish parents after giyur. The existing etika-bd6 is a practical question; this one should not be read as its resolution.
12. **Delay of wages (etika-n49).** The lesson says that delay is equated with taking a life. The verse says that the worker's life depends on the wages (Devarim 24:15).
13. **Prozbul (etika-n54).** The lesson calls it a contract that preserves the debt. The mechanism is a declaration before a court.

### Beit din (review: Cursor)

1. **An apostate ger (beit-din-n4, lesson 1).** Yevamot 47b: he is an apostate Jew and remains a Jew. The lesson states this for all movements; the section's own card beit-din-1-c14 is marked as Orthodox. Reform generally does not single out the category "apostate Jew." The question follows the lesson and remains at the Reform level.
2. **Ger toshav (beit-din-n8).** The lesson's definition: a non-Jew in Eretz Israel who observes the seven laws of the descendants of Noah. The Rambam, Kings 8:10, also requires acceptance before a court and regards this institution as in effect in the era when the yovel is observed. The question does not add this.
3. **Two sages (beit-din-n11).** The court is of three. Yevamot 47b says that two scholars tell him this at the immersion; the third often stands outside for reasons of modesty. For a man the order is: first milah, then tevilah. For a woman the bodily rite is tevilah; lesson 4 says that the judges again name some mitzvot in the water.
4. **Nativ (beit-din-n19).** The lesson presents it as an example of an Israeli preparatory program. It is a course of the Jewish Agency / the IDF, and not the only civilian path into the state courts.
5. **Recognition by the Rabbinate (beit-din-n15, beit-din-n17, beit-din-n21).** The list of foreign courts that the Chief Rabbinate recognizes changes. The questions follow the lesson and do not list the courts.
6. **Doubt and Mashiach (beit-din-n28).** The lesson says that doubt is a normal part of a believer's life, and that an Orthodox beit din still requires one to accept the principles of faith as truth. The question rejects replacing that acceptance with doubt. This should not be read as though any inner struggle makes the candidate unfit.
7. **Forty se'ah (beit-din-n36).** The lesson says "several hundred liters." Ordinary calculations give about 332 liters and about 575; some give more. The old source was Yoreh De'ah 198 — that was the wrong siman; 201 is the construction siman, and it is not among this lesson's references. The source is now the lesson.
8. **The second and third immersions (beit-din-n39).** The lesson calls them a custom. According to the lesson, this is not what makes the first immersion valid.
9. **Eyes and lips (beit-din-n42).** The lesson's wording is general. A local beit din may express the same concern differently.
10. **A river (beit-din-n43).** The only qualification the lesson names is rivers fed by rain; the rabbi makes the decision. The question is not a shiur on the difference between a ma'ayan and a bor.
11. **An infant on Shabbat (beit-din-n47).** The lesson's rule: the eighth day overrides Shabbat, and a ger's milah does not. Illness, and also some cases of birth, postpone an infant's brit. The lesson does not teach these exceptions. The North American Reform movement does not formally require milah for adults; the differs note says this.
12. **"One hundred and twenty years" (beit-din-n50).** An idiom about death. The name is then inscribed on the tombstone, and not beginning from one's 120th year.
13. **Three judges at a milah (beit-din-n52).** The lesson says "in the opinion of many." Kabbalat mitzvot and tevilah are the parts that the lesson explicitly names as requiring three. One who was already circumcised outside the framework of a brit needs hatafat dam brit, and not a second full operation (beit-din-n49, beit-din-q46).
14. **36 and 46 (beit-din-n54).** Bava Metzia 59b gives both counts of the warnings about the ger. In many verses "ger" is used in the Torah's broader sense, and not only of one who has undergone giyur. The question follows the lesson.
15. **A giyoret and a kohen (beit-din-n57).** Orthodox halacha forbids this marriage (Yevamot 60b; the Rambam, Issurei Biah 18:3). The question is for the Orthodox movement only. The lesson does not consider Masorti responsa that permit such a marriage, or the further question of the daughter of two gerim.
16. **Head of a community (beit-din-n58).** The lesson says that a ger may be called to the Torah, counts in a minyan, and may become a rabbi or the head of a community. Some authorities limit a ger's communal authority (serarah). A giyoret's honors follow that community's practice regarding women. The question no longer says that a non-observant ger retains aliyot and a minyan.
17. **A giyoret's child (beit-din-n63).** "A Jew from birth" is a child she bears after her giyur, including one conceived before it, because status is determined by the mother at the moment of birth. A later child of a male ger and a non-Jewish woman is not a Jew. Children born before the mother's giyur are beit-din-q31, not this question.
18. **When tevilah may be performed.** Lesson 1 marks the wording "by day, not on Shabbat and not on Yom Tov" as Orthodox. Lesson 4 sets out the same rule in the general procedure. The existing beit-din-q41 is Orthodox. beit-din-n43 no longer asks this.

<!-- review-doubts:end -->

<!-- PRAYERS-BEGIN -->
## Daily prayers

All texts of the «Prayers» section (content/en/prayers.json), same ids as in docs/RABBI-REVIEW.md. Generated by tools/gen_rabbi_review_en.py. Nusach: Ashkenaz. Status: requires review by a rabbi.

### What to check first

- [ ] References to the Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim: 62:2, 101:4, 185:1 (prayer in a language one understands), 215:4 (the Name when studying), 4 (washing the hands in the morning), 6 («Asher yatzar»), 46 (morning blessings), 110:1 («Havinenu»), 110:4–7 (Tefilat HaDerech), 158 (washing before bread), 167–168, 202–208 (blessings over food), 187:1 («Brich Rachamana»), 223–225 («Shehecheyanu»), 239 (the bedtime Shema), 263 (candles), 271 (kiddush). Talmud references are given with each prayer below.
- [ ] The translation of «Brich Rachamana» and the short Birkat HaMazon: the app gives «Brich Rachamana» (Berachot 40b) and the full first blessing («HaZan»), advising to read the rest from a siddur. Is this enough, and how should it be worded for a beginner?
- [ ] «Havinenu»: when it may be said instead of the full Amidah (not in the winter rainy season, not at the end of Shabbat and festivals, in haste or on the road), and whether the first three and last three blessings are rendered correctly.
- [ ] Tefilat HaDerech: text according to the Daat siddur; other nuschaot have further phrases. Should the app say where it is begun (after leaving the town) and when it is said without the Name (a short journey)?
- [ ] The order «Mezonot / HaGafen / HaEtz / HaAdamah / Shehakol» and the rules of «Al HaMichyah»: the variants for Israel («al ha'aretz ve'al peroteha», «ve'al hamichyah»), the kezayit and revi'it amounts for the blessing after eating.
- [ ] Shabbat and festival candles: the text of the blessings; that a woman candidate before giyur lights candles without the blessing or otherwise — presented as «ask your rabbi».
- [ ] Morning blessings: a candidate before giyur does not say «shelo asani goy»; after giyur some omit it and others say «she'asani ger». The other morning blessings (including «shelo asani eved / ishah» and the women's «she'asani kirtzono») are given without a separate note for the candidate — is one needed? All marked «ask your rabbi».
- [ ] Transliteration of the Name («Adonai», «Eloheinu») and the advice to say «Hashem» / «Elokeinu» when studying. In the Hebrew the Name is written as יְיָ.
- [ ] The bedtime Shema: the short form (forgiveness, the first paragraph of the Shema, «HaMapil», «B'yado afkid»); the translation of «HaMapil». Does a candidate say it with the Name?
- [ ] Kiddush: a candidate before giyur does not make kiddush for others and does not say «You have chosen us… commanded us»; revi'it is given as «about 86 ml» (there are opinions of a larger amount).
- [ ] «Modeh ani»: the text says the formula entered the siddurim later than the Talmud (16th–17th centuries). Check the dating.
- [ ] Washing the hands in the morning: three times on each hand, alternately; and before bread (two or three times on each hand from a vessel) — customs differ.
- [ ] The note «You may pray in a language you understand»: the wording that Hebrew is preferable and that the first line of the Shema is best said in Hebrew.

Sources of the text: Hebrew — The Metsudah Siddur (1981, CC-BY) and the Daat siddur (public domain), both via Sefaria, nusach Ashkenaz; cantillation marks and meteg removed. The English translation and transliteration are Shorashim's own work (from the Russian edition). The texts of the blessings over food, candles and «Shehecheyanu» are the accepted formulas.

### General notes of the section

- [ ] **You may pray in a language you understand**. The Mishnah (Sotah 7:1) lists what may be said in any language: the Shema, the prayer (the Amidah), and Birkat Hamazon. The Shulchan Aruch rules the same way (Orach Chaim 62:2, 101:4, 185:1). The conditions are: you understand the language, and the translation is accurate. It is better to pray with understanding than without understanding. Hebrew is still preferable: the whole Jewish people prays in it, and a translation inevitably loses part of the meaning. Many halachic authorities advise saying at least the first line of the Shema in Hebrew. Begin with Russian, and add Hebrew gradually, line by line. — *Mishnah Sotah 7:1; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 62:2, 101:4, 185:1*
- [ ] **The Name of G-d in the text**. In the Hebrew, the Name of G-d is written as it is in the siddurim: יְיָ. In prayer, and in a blessing said for its actual purpose, it is pronounced “Adonai.” When you are learning a blessing and not saying it for its actual purpose, the custom is to say “HaShem” instead of “Adonai” and “Elokeinu” instead of “Eloheinu,” so as not to pronounce the Name in vain. — *Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 215:4*
- [ ] **Before giyur: check with a rabbi** *(ask your rabbi)*. Until a candidate has completed giyur, the mitzvot of Israel do not yet rest upon the candidate, and some words of the prayers are said only by a Jew. For example, blessings with the words “asher kid’shanu b’mitzvotav v’tzivanu” (“who has sanctified us with His mitzvot and commanded us”), the blessing “shelo asani goy,” and the kiddush “You have chosen us.” Many rabbis advise, before giyur, omitting the Name of G-d in them, saying them without the blessing, or only listening and answering “amen”; others permit more. Praise, thanksgiving, the Shema, the blessings over food, and personal prayer can usually be said already now. Each prayer has a note, “Before giyur” — be sure to check all of this with a rabbi: the decisions depend on the community and the beit din. — *Sanhedrin 58b; Rambam, Laws of Kings 10:9–10*
- [ ] **Siddurim of the Reform movement** *(ask your rabbi)*. The texts in this section follow nusach Ashkenaz (the Hebrew is from Metsudah and Daat, via Sefaria). Reform communities use their own siddurim. In North America the principal one today is Mishkan T'filah (“Mishkan T’filah,” CCAR Press, 2007), which replaced Gates of Prayer (“Gates of Prayer,” 1975); for the Days of Awe the machzor Mishkan HaNefesh has been published. In them Hebrew and the translation stand side by side; the matriarchs are often included in the Amidah, and some blessings offer alternative wordings. Prayer in a language one understands is a deliberate principle of the movement, not a replacement for Hebrew: the sacred language of the people continues to be studied and heard in the service. In many progressive synagogues instrumental music is heard on Shabbat, and men and women lead the service equally. In Russian-speaking communities of ROOPI (the Religious Association of Progressive Jewish Communities) the siddur may differ in its contents from the North American one. If the wording in your book differs from the text in the app, pray from your community’s siddur and check with your rabbi. — *Mishkan T'filah, CCAR Press, 2007; Mishkan HaNefesh; the practice of ROOPI communities*
- [ ] **Facing Jerusalem** *(ask your rabbi)*. The standing prayer, the Amidah, is customarily said facing Jerusalem, the eternal capital of the Jewish people. The Amidah itself contains requests for the ingathering of the exiles and for the return to the city of David. In this way the daily prayer keeps the bond with the Land of Israel, even if a person still lives in the Diaspora. One turns, in doing so, only to the Almighty, directly, with no intermediary. — *Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 94; the siddur, the Amidah*

### Prayer texts

#### `modeh-ani` Modeh Ani — מוֹדֶה אֲנִי

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Morning. Gratitude upon waking
- **When:** Right after waking, still in bed, before washing the hands.
- **How:** Briefly, sitting or lying down. This sentence has no Name of G-d, so it is said even before washing the hands. A woman says “Modah ani” (מוֹדָה אֲנִי).
- **About the prayer:** The first words of the day are gratitude. The Sages call sleep “one-sixtieth of death” (Berachot 57b), and every morning G-d returns the soul, as a faithful guardian returns a pledge: “great is Your faithfulness” (Eichah 3:23). This formula appeared in the siddurim later than the Talmud (the 16th–17th centuries).
- **References:** Berachot 57b; Eichah 3:23
- **Before giyur:** “Modeh ani” has neither the Name of G-d nor the words “commanded us,” so it is usually said even before giyur. Check with a rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

> מוֹדֶה אֲנִי לְפָנֶיךָ מֶלֶךְ חַי וְקַיָּם שֶׁהֶחֱזַרְתָּ בִּי נִשְׁמָתִי בְּחֶמְלָה, רַבָּה אֱמוּנָתֶךָ:
>
> *Modeh ani lefanecha, melech chai v'kayam, shehechezarta bi nishmati b'chemlah, rabbah emunatecha.*
>
> I thank You, living and eternal King, that in mercy You have returned my soul to me. Great is Your faithfulness.

#### `netilat-yadayim` Netilat yadayim (in the morning) — נְטִילַת יָדַיִם

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Morning. Washing the hands after sleep
- **When:** In the morning, soon after waking, before the morning blessings.
- **How:** Fill a cup with water and pour it in turn over the right hand and the left, three times on each (this is the custom among Ashkenazim and among many Sephardim in the morning). Then dry your hands and say the blessing. Many say it together with “Asher Yatzar.”
- **About the prayer:** The morning washing prepares a person for the new day and for prayer, as the priest washed his hands before the service in the Temple (Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 4). Before a meal with bread the hands are washed again, with its own order (see the section “Food”).
- **References:** Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 4
- **Before giyur:** The words “v’tzivanu” (“and commanded us”) are said by one upon whom the mitzvot already rest. Before giyur, many rabbis advise washing the hands without the blessing, or saying it without the Name of G-d; others permit it in full. Check with a rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְצִוָּנוּ עַל נְטִילַת יָדָיִם:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kid'shanu b'mitzvotav v'tzivanu al netilat yadayim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His mitzvot and commanded us to wash the hands.

#### `birkot-hashachar` Birkot HaShachar (short) — בִּרְכוֹת הַשַּׁחַר

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Morning. The morning blessings
- **When:** In the morning, after washing the hands and “Asher Yatzar.” They may be said at home, before coming to the synagogue.
- **How:** Each blessing is gratitude for what we usually do not notice: sight, clothing, the ability to stand and to walk. Say them without hurrying, understanding the words. Here is a short order: “Elohai Neshamah” and fifteen short blessings; in a full siddur the blessings of the Torah and the reading of passages come next.
- **About the prayer:** The blessings are listed in the Talmud (Berachot 60b): when a person wakes, hears the rooster, opens the eyes, gets dressed, and stands up, each act has its own blessing. Later they came to be said all together at the beginning of the morning prayer.
- **References:** Berachot 60b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 46
- **Before giyur:** Before giyur, a candidate does not say the blessing “shelo asani goy” (“who has not made me a non-Jew”). After giyur, opinions differ: some omit it, others say “she’asani ger” (“who has made me a ger”). The other blessings are praise, and many rabbis permit them even before giyur. Check with a rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

*Elohai Neshamah — “The soul that You have given me”*

> אֱלֹהַי נְשָׁמָה שֶׁנָּתַתָּ בִּי טְהוֹרָה הִיא אַתָּה בְרָאתָהּ אַתָּה יְצַרְתָּהּ אַתָּה נְפַחְתָּהּ בִּי וְאַתָּה מְשַׁמְּרָהּ בְּקִרְבִּי וְאַתָּה עָתִיד לִטְּלָהּ מִמֶּנִּי וּלְהַחֲזִירָהּ בִּי לֶעָתִיד לָבֹא, כָּל זְמַן שֶׁהַנְּשָׁמָה בְקִרְבִּי מוֹדֶה אֲנִי לְפָנֶיךָ יְיָ אֱלֹהַי וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתַי רִבּוֹן כָּל הַמַּעֲשִׂים אֲדוֹן כָּל הַנְּשָׁמוֹת: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַמַּחֲזִיר נְשָׁמוֹת לִפְגָרִים מֵתִים:
>
> *Elohai, neshamah shenatata bi tehorah hi. Atah veratah, Atah yetzartah, Atah nefachtah bi, ve'Atah meshamrah bekirbi, ve'Atah atid litlah mimeni ulehachazirah bi le'atid lavo. Kol zman shehaneshamah bekirbi, modeh ani lefanecha, Adonai Elohai veilohei avotai, Ribbon kol hama'asim, Adon kol haneshamot. Baruch atah Adonai, hamachazir neshamot lifgarim metim.*
>
> My G-d, the soul that You have given me is pure. You created it, You formed it, You breathed it into me, You keep it within me, and one day You will take it from me and return it to me in the time to come. All the time that the soul is within me, I thank You, L-rd, my G-d and G-d of my fathers, Master of all creations, Lord of all souls. Blessed are You, L-rd, who returns souls to lifeless bodies.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר נָתַן לַשֶּׂכְוִי בִינָה לְהַבְחִין בֵּין יוֹם וּבֵין לָיְלָה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher natan lasechvi binah lehavchin bein yom uvein lailah.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who gave the heart (and the rooster) understanding to distinguish day from night.

*Three blessings about one’s own station*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁלֹּא עָשַׂנִי גּוֹי:
בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁלֹּא עָשַׂנִי עָבֶד:
בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁלֹּא עָשַׂנִי אִשָּׁה: (אִשָּׁה אוֹמֶרֶת: שֶׁעָשַׂנִי כִּרְצוֹנוֹ:)
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, shelo asani goy.
Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, shelo asani aved.
Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, shelo asani ishah. (Ishah omeret: she'asani kirtzono.)*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has not made me a non-Jew.
Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has not made me a slave.
Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has not made me a woman. (A woman says: “who has made me according to His will.”)

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, פּוֹקֵחַ עִוְרִים:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, pokeach ivrim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who opens the eyes of the blind.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, מַלְבִּישׁ עֲרֻמִּים:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, malbish arumim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who clothes the naked.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, מַתִּיר אֲסוּרִים:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, matir asurim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who frees the bound.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, זוֹקֵף כְּפוּפִים:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, zokef kefufim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who straightens the bowed.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, רוֹקַע הָאָרֶץ עַל הַמָּיִם:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, roka ha'aretz al hamayim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who spread the earth over the waters.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁעָשָׂה לִי כָּל צָרְכִּי:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, she'asah li kol tzorki.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has given me all that I need.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, הַמֵּכִין מִצְעֲדֵי גָבֶר:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hamechin mitzadei gaver.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who guides a person’s steps.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אוֹזֵר יִשְׂרָאֵל בִּגְבוּרָה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, ozer Yisrael bigvurah.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who girds Israel with strength.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, עוֹטֵר יִשְׂרָאֵל בְּתִפְאָרָה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, oter Yisrael betifarah.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who crowns Israel with glory.

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, הַנּוֹתֵן לַיָּעֵף כֹּחַ:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hanoten laya'ef koach.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who gives strength to the weary.

*HaMa’avir sheinah — “Who removes sleep”*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם הַמַּעֲבִיר שֵׁנָה מֵעֵינָי וּתְנוּמָה מֵעַפְעַפָּי: וִיהִי רָצוֹן מִלְּפָנֶיךָ יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתֵינוּ שֶׁתַּרְגִּילֵנוּ בְּתוֹרָתֶךָ וְדַבְּקֵנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתֶיךָ, וְאַל תְּבִיאֵנוּ לֹא לִידֵי חֵטְא וְלֹא לִידֵי עֲבֵרָה וְעָוֹן וְלֹא לִידֵי נִסָּיוֹן וְלֹא לִידֵי בִזָּיוֹן וְאַל יִשְׁלֹט בָּנוּ יֵצֶר הָרָע וְהַרְחִיקֵנוּ מֵאָדָם רָע וּמֵחָבֵר רָע וְדַבְּקֵנוּ בְּיֵצֶר הַטּוֹב וּבְמַעֲשִׂים טוֹבִים וְכוֹף אֶת־יִצְרֵנוּ לְהִשְׁתַּעְבֶּד־לָךְ וּתְנֵנוּ הַיּוֹם וּבְכָל־יוֹם לְחֵן וּלְחֶסֶד וּלְרַחֲמִים בְּעֵינֶיךָ וּבְעֵינֵי כָל־רוֹאֵינוּ וְתִגְמְלֵנוּ חֲסָדִים טוֹבִים: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ גּוֹמֵל חֲסָדִים טוֹבִים לְעַמּוֹ יִשְׂרָאֵל:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hama'avir sheinah me'einai utnumah me'afapai. Vihi ratzon milfanecha, Adonai Eloheinu veilohei avoteinu, shetargilenu b'toratecha v'dabkenu b'mitzvotecha, v'al tevi'enu lo lidei chet, v'lo lidei averah ve'avon, v'lo lidei nisayon, v'lo lidei bizayon. V'al yishlot banu yetzer hara, v'harhikenu me'adam ra ume'chaver ra, v'dabkenu b'yetzer hatov uv'ma'asim tovim, v'chof et yitzrenu l'hishta'abed lach. Utnenu hayom uv'chol yom l'chen ul'chesed ul'rachamim b'einecha uv'einei kol ro'einu, v'tigmelenu chasadim tovim. Baruch atah Adonai, gomel chasadim tovim l'amo Yisrael.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who removes sleep from my eyes and slumber from my eyelids. May it be Your will, L-rd our G-d and G-d of our fathers, to accustom us to Your Torah and to draw us near to Your mitzvot. Do not bring us to sin, nor to transgression and guilt, nor to trial, nor to disgrace. Do not let the evil inclination rule over us; keep us far from an evil person and an evil companion; draw us near to the good inclination and to good deeds, and bend our inclination to serve You. Grant us today and every day to find favor, love, and compassion in Your eyes and in the eyes of all who see us, and bestow good kindnesses upon us. Blessed are You, L-rd, who bestows good kindnesses upon His people Israel.

#### `shma` Shema Yisrael — שְׁמַע יִשְׂרָאֵל

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Morning, Evening. The central declaration of faith, the first paragraph
- **When:** Twice a day: in the morning (before the end of the first quarter of the daylight hours; the exact time is in the “Calendar”) and in the evening, after the stars come out. Once more — before sleep.
- **How:** The first line is said aloud, the eyes covered with the right hand, with the thought of accepting the sovereignty of Heaven. The second line (“Baruch shem…”) is said in a whisper. One who prays without a minyan adds, by Ashkenaz custom, “El melech ne’eman” (“G-d, the faithful King”) at the beginning. If it is still difficult, begin with the first two lines and the first paragraph.
- **About the prayer:** The first line and the first paragraph are Torah, Devarim 6:4–9: the unity of G-d, love for Him, teaching the Torah to children, tefillin, and the mezuzah. Reading the Shema in the morning and in the evening is a mitzvah of the Torah (Berachot 1:1–3). The full Shema in the siddur includes two further paragraphs: Devarim 11:13–21 and Bamidbar 15:37–41. The word “echad” in the first line speaks of the unity of G-d: He is one, and is not made up of parts and “persons”; these words are taken upon oneself every day.
- **References:** Devarim 6:4–9; Mishnah Berachot 1:1–3
- **Before giyur:** Anyone may read the Shema, and many rabbis advise a candidate to read it every day: it is part of the preparation. It becomes the obligation of the mitzvah of the Shema after giyur. Check with a rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

*Aloud*

> שְׁמַע יִשְׂרָאֵל יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ יְיָ אֶחָד:
>
> *Shema Yisrael, Adonai Eloheinu, Adonai echad.*
>
> Hear, O Israel: the L-rd is our G-d, the L-rd is One.

*In a whisper*

> בָּרוּךְ שֵׁם כְּבוֹד מַלְכוּתוֹ לְעוֹלָם וָעֶד:
>
> *Baruch shem kevod malchuto le'olam va'ed.*
>
> Blessed is the name of the glory of His kingdom for ever and ever.

*Ve’ahavta — the first paragraph*

> וְאָהַבְתָּ אֵת יְיָ אֱלֹהֶיךָ בְּכָל־לְבָבְךָ וּבְכָל־נַפְשְׁךָ וּבְכָל־מְאֹדֶךָ: וְהָיוּ הַדְּבָרִים הָאֵלֶּה אֲשֶׁר אָנֹכִי מְצַוְּךָ הַיּוֹם עַל־לְבָבֶךָ: וְשִׁנַּנְתָּם לְבָנֶיךָ וְדִבַּרְתָּ בָּם בְּשִׁבְתְּךָ בְּבֵיתֶךָ וּבְלֶכְתְּךָ בַדֶּרֶךְ וּבְשָׁכְבְּךָ וּבְקוּמֶךָ: וּקְשַׁרְתָּם לְאוֹת עַל־יָדֶךָ וְהָיוּ לְטֹטָפֹת בֵּין עֵינֶיךָ: וּכְתַבְתָּם עַל־מְזֻזוֹת בֵּיתֶךָ וּבִשְׁעָרֶיךָ:
>
> *Ve'ahavta et Adonai Elohecha bechol levavcha uvchol nafshecha uvchol me'odecha. Vehayu hadevarim ha'eleh asher anochi metzavecha hayom al levavecha. Veshinantam levanecha vedibarta bam beshivtecha beveitecha uvlechtecha vaderech uvshochbecha uvkumecha. Ukshartam le'ot al yadecha, vehayu letotafot bein einecha. Uchtavtam al mezuzot beitecha uvish'arecha.*
>
> Love the L-rd your G-d with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your substance. Let these words, which I command you today, be upon your heart. Repeat them to your children and speak of them when you sit in your house and when you walk on the way, when you lie down and when you rise. Bind them as a sign upon your hand, and let them be a band above your eyes. And write them on the doorposts of your house and on your gates.

#### `asher-yatzar` Asher Yatzar — אֲשֶׁר יָצַר

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Day, Morning. Thanks for a healthy body
- **When:** Every time after using the bathroom and washing your hands, including in the morning.
- **How:** Wash your hands (without the blessing for washing) and say it outside the bathroom.
- **About the prayer:** A blessing from the Talmud (Berachot 60b). It teaches us to see the miracle in the most ordinary things: if even one of the body’s “openings” were to close or open at the wrong time, a person could not survive even for an hour.
- **References:** Berachot 60b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 6
- **Before giyur:** This blessing does not contain the words “who commanded us”; it is an expression of thanks. Many rabbis permit saying it even before giyur. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם אֲשֶׁר יָצַר אֶת־הָאָדָם בְּחָכְמָה וּבָרָא בוֹ נְקָבִים נְקָבִים חֲלוּלִים חֲלוּלִים גָּלוּי וְיָדוּעַ לִפְנֵי כִסֵּא כְבוֹדֶךָ שֶׁאִם יִפָּתֵחַ אֶחָד מֵהֶם אוֹ יִסָּתֵם אֶחָד מֵהֶם אִי אֶפְשַׁר לְהִתְקַיֵּם וְלַעֲמֹד לְפָנֶיךָ אֲפִילוּ שָׁעָה אֶחָת. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ רוֹפֵא כָל־בָּשָׂר וּמַפְלִיא לַעֲשׂוֹת:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher yatzar et ha'adam bechochmah, uvara vo nekavim nekavim, chalulim chalulim. Galui veyadua lifnei chisei chevodecha, she'im yipatei'ach echad mehem, o yisatem echad mehem, i efshar lehitkayem vela'amod lefanecha afilu sha'ah echat. Baruch atah Adonai, rofei chol basar umafli la'asot.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who formed man with wisdom and created within him many openings and many hollows. It is revealed and known before the throne of Your glory that if one of them were to be opened or one of them were to be closed, it would be impossible to survive and stand before You even for one hour. Blessed are You, Lord, who heals all flesh and acts wondrously.

#### `amida-short` The short Amidah with “Havinenu” — תְּפִלַּת הֲבִינֵנוּ

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Day. The central prayer in abbreviated form
- **When:** The Amidah is said three times a day: in the morning (Shacharit), in the afternoon (Minchah) and in the evening (Maariv). The sages permitted the short form when there is no time or strength: while traveling, when ill, or in a hurry. For a beginner it helps in learning the order of the prayer.
- **How:** Stand with your feet together, facing Jerusalem. Before beginning, take three steps back and three steps forward. Read quietly, but moving your lips, so that you can hear yourself. You bow four times: at the beginning and at the end of the first blessing, and at the beginning and at the end of “Modim”: at “Baruch” you bend your knees, at “Atah” you bow forward, and by “Adonai” you stand straight again. At the end, at “Oseh shalom”, you take three steps back and bow to the left, to the right and forward. In the blessing “Shome'a tefillah” and at the end, after “Yihyu leratzon”, you may add your own request in your own words, in any language.
- **About the prayer:** The full weekday Amidah consists of nineteen blessings: three of praise, thirteen of requests and three of thanks. Shmuel in the Talmud (Berachot 29a) composed “Havinenu”, a single blessing that briefly contains the thirteen middle ones. The first three and the last three blessings remain in full. Restrictions: “Havinenu” is not said in winter, during the season of the prayer for rain, nor at the conclusion of Shabbat and festivals (Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 110:1); on Shabbat and festivals the Amidah is different. Check with your rabbi. The direction of prayer is Jerusalem, the eternal capital; the middle blessings of the full Amidah include requests for the ingathering of the exiles and for the city of David.
- **References:** Berachot 29a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 110:1
- **Before giyur:** A ger says “G-d of our fathers” just like every Jew: Avraham is the father of all gerim (Rambam, letter to Ovadiah the Ger). Before giyur many rabbis advise praying the Amidah as a personal prayer and learning its order, but opinions differ as to exactly how. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria; “Havinenu”: Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

*Introduction*

> אֲדֹנָי שְׂפָתַי תִּפְתָּח וּפִי יַגִּיד תְּהִלָּתֶךָ:
>
> *Adonai sefatai tiftach, ufi yagid tehilatecha.*
>
> Lord, open my lips, and my mouth will declare Your praise.

*1. Avot: “Fathers”*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתֵינוּ אֱלֹהֵי אַבְרָהָם אֱלֹהֵי יִצְחָק וֵאלֹהֵי יַעֲקֹב הָאֵל הַגָּדוֹל הַגִּבּוֹר וְהַנּוֹרָא אֵל עֶלְיוֹן גּוֹמֵל חֲסָדִים טוֹבִים וְקוֹנֵה הַכֹּל וְזוֹכֵר חַסְדֵי אָבוֹת וּמֵבִיא גוֹאֵל לִבְנֵי בְנֵיהֶם לְמַעַן שְׁמוֹ בְּאַהֲבָה: מֶלֶךְ עוֹזֵר וּמוֹשִׁיעַ וּמָגֵן: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ מָגֵן אַבְרָהָם:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu vElohei avoteinu, Elohei Avraham, Elohei Yitzchak vElohei Yaakov, ha'El hagadol hagibor vehanora, El elyon, gomel chasadim tovim vekoneh hakol, vezocher chasdei avot, umevi go'el livnei veneihem lema'an shemo be'ahavah. Melech ozer umoshia umagen. Baruch atah Adonai, magen Avraham.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d and G-d of our fathers, G-d of Avraham, G-d of Yitzchak and G-d of Yaakov, the great, mighty and awesome G-d, G-d Most High, who bestows good kindnesses and possesses all, who remembers the devotion of the fathers and brings a redeemer to their children’s children for the sake of His Name, with love. King, Helper, Savior and Shield. Blessed are You, Lord, Shield of Avraham.

*2. Gevurot: “Might”*

> אַתָּה גִבּוֹר לְעוֹלָם אֲדֹנָי מְחַיֵּה מֵתִים אַתָּה רַב לְהוֹשִׁיעַ: (בַּקַּיִץ: מוֹרִיד הַטָּל | בַּחֹרֶף: מַשִּׁיב הָרוּחַ וּמוֹרִיד הַגֶּשֶׁם:) מְכַלְכֵּל חַיִּים בְּחֶסֶד מְחַיֵּה מֵתִים בְּרַחֲמִים רַבִּים סוֹמֵךְ נוֹפְלִים וְרוֹפֵא חוֹלִים וּמַתִּיר אֲסוּרִים וּמְקַיֵּם אֱמוּנָתוֹ לִישֵׁנֵי עָפָר, מִי כָמוֹךָ בַּעַל גְּבוּרוֹת וּמִי דּוֹמֶה לָּךְ מֶלֶךְ מֵמִית וּמְחַיֵּה וּמַצְמִיחַ יְשׁוּעָה: וְנֶאֱמָן אַתָּה לְהַחֲיוֹת מֵתִים: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ מְחַיֵּה הַמֵּתִים:
>
> *Atah gibor le'olam Adonai, mechayeh metim atah, rav lehoshia. (In summer: morid hatal. In winter: mashiv haruach umorid hageshem.) Mechalkel chayim bechesed, mechayeh metim berachamim rabim, somech noflim, verofei cholim, umatir asurim, umekayem emunato lishenei afar. Mi chamocha ba'al gevurot umi domeh lach, melech memit umechayeh umatzmiach yeshuah. Vene'eman atah lehachayot metim. Baruch atah Adonai, mechayeh hametim.*
>
> You are mighty forever, Lord; You revive the dead, You are great in saving. (In summer: who sends down the dew. In winter: who makes the wind blow and brings down the rain.) In kindness You sustain the living, in great mercy You revive the dead, You support the falling, heal the sick, free the captives and keep Your faith with those who sleep in the dust. Who is like You, Master of mighty deeds, and who can compare to You, King who brings death and restores life and causes salvation to flourish! And You are faithful to revive the dead. Blessed are You, Lord, who revives the dead.

*3. Kedushat HaShem: “Holiness of the Name”*

> אַתָּה קָדוֹשׁ וְשִׁמְךָ קָדוֹשׁ וּקְדוֹשִׁים בְּכָל־יוֹם יְהַלְלוּךָ סֶּלָה. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הָאֵל הַקָּדוֹשׁ:
>
> *Atah kadosh veshimcha kadosh, ukedoshim bechol yom yehalelucha selah. Baruch atah Adonai, ha'El hakadosh.*
>
> You are holy and Your Name is holy, and holy ones praise You every day forever. Blessed are You, Lord, the holy G-d.

*Havinenu: in place of the thirteen middle blessings*

> הֲבִינֵנוּ יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ לָדַעַת דְּרָכֶיךָ, וּמוֹל אֶת לְבָבֵנוּ לְיִרְאָתֶךָ, וְתִסְלַח לָנוּ לִהְיוֹת גְּאוּלִים, וְרַחֲקֵנוּ מִמַּכְאוֹב, וְדַשְּׁנֵנוּ בִּנְאוֹת אַרְצֶךָ, וּנְפוּצוֹתֵינוּ מֵאַרְבַּע תְּקַבֵּץ, וְהַתּוֹעִים עַל דַּעְתְּךָ יִשָּׁפֵטוּ, וְעַל הָרְשָׁעִים תָּנִיף יָדֶךָ, וְיִשְׂמְחוּ צַדִּיקִים בְּבִנְיַן עִירֶךָ וּבְתִקּוּן הֵיכָלֶךָ, וּבִצְמִיחַת קֶרֶן לְדָוִד עַבְדֶּךָ, וּבַעֲרִיכַת נֵר לְבֶן יִשַׁי מְשִׁיחֶךָ. טֶרֶם נִקְרָא אַתָּה תַעֲנֶה. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ, שׁוֹמֵעַ תְּפִלָּה:
>
> *Havinenu Adonai Eloheinu lada'at derachecha, umol et levavenu leyir'atecha, vetislach lanu lihyot ge'ulim, verachakenu mimachov, vedashenenu bine'ot artzecha, unefutzoteinu me'arba tekabetz, vehato'im al da'atcha yishafetu, ve'al haresha'im tanif yadecha, veyismechu tzadikim bevinyan irecha uvetikun heichalecha, uvitzmichat keren leDavid avdecha, uva'arichat ner leven Yishai meshichecha. Terem nikra atah ta'aneh. Baruch atah Adonai, shome'a tefillah.*
>
> Grant us understanding, Lord, our G-d, to know Your ways, and open our hearts to the awe of You. Forgive us so that we may be redeemed, keep us far from suffering, satisfy us from the pastures of Your land, and gather our scattered ones from the four corners of the earth. Let those who stray be judged according to Your wisdom, and raise Your hand against the wicked. And let the righteous rejoice in the rebuilding of Your city and the restoration of Your Temple, in the flourishing of the strength of David, Your servant, and in the lamp prepared for the son of Yishai, Your anointed. Before we call, You will answer. Blessed are You, Lord, who hears prayer.

*4. Avodah: “Service”*

> רְצֵה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ בְּעַמְּךָ יִשְׂרָאֵל וּבִתְפִלָּתָם וְהָשֵׁב אֶת הָעֲבוֹדָה לִדְבִיר בֵּיתֶךָ וְאִשֵּׁי יִשְׂרָאֵל וּתְפִלָּתָם בְּאַהֲבָה תְקַבֵּל בְּרָצוֹן וּתְהִי לְרָצוֹן תָּמִיד עֲבוֹדַת יִשְׂרָאֵל עַמֶּךָ: וְתֶחֱזֶינָה עֵינֵינוּ בְּשׁוּבְךָ לְצִיּוֹן בְּרַחֲמִים: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַמַּחֲזִיר שְׁכִינָתוֹ לְצִיּוֹן:
>
> *Retzeh Adonai Eloheinu be'amcha Yisrael uvitefilatam, vehashev et ha'avodah lidvir beitecha, ve'ishei Yisrael utefilatam be'ahavah tekabel beratzon, utehi leratzon tamid avodat Yisrael amecha. Vetechezenah eineinu beshuvcha leTziyon berachamim. Baruch atah Adonai, hamachazir shechinato leTziyon.*
>
> Be favorable, Lord, our G-d, toward Your people Israel and their prayer. Restore the service to the inner sanctuary of Your House; accept the fire-offerings of Israel and their prayer with love and favor, and may the service of Israel, Your people, always be pleasing to You. May our eyes behold Your merciful return to Zion. Blessed are You, Lord, who restores His Presence to Zion.

*5. Modim: “We thank”*

> מוֹדִים אֲנַחְנוּ לָךְ שָׁאַתָּה הוּא יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתֵינוּ לְעוֹלָם וָעֶד צוּר חַיֵּינוּ מָגֵן יִשְׁעֵנוּ אַתָּה הוּא לְדוֹר וָדוֹר נוֹדֶה לְּךָ וּנְסַפֵּר תְּהִלָּתֶךָ עַל־חַיֵּינוּ הַמְּסוּרִים בְּיָדֶךָ וְעַל נִשְׁמוֹתֵינוּ הַפְּקוּדוֹת לָךְ וְעַל נִסֶּיךָ שֶׁבְּכָל יוֹם עִמָּנוּ וְעַל נִפְלְאוֹתֶיךָ וְטוֹבוֹתֶיךָ שֶׁבְּכָל עֵת עֶרֶב וָבֹקֶר וְצָהֳרָיִם הַטּוֹב כִּי לֹא כָלוּ רַחֲמֶיךָ וְהַמְרַחֵם כִּי לֹא תַמּוּ חֲסָדֶיךָ מֵעוֹלָם קִוִּינוּ לָךְ: וְעַל־כֻּלָּם יִתְבָּרַךְ וְיִתְרוֹמַם שִׁמְךָ מַלְכֵּנוּ תָּמִיד לְעוֹלָם וָעֶד: וְכֹל הַחַיִּים יוֹדוּךָ סֶּלָה וִיהַלְלוּ אֶת־שִׁמְךָ בֶּאֱמֶת הָאֵל יְשׁוּעָתֵנוּ וְעֶזְרָתֵנוּ סֶלָה: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַטּוֹב שִׁמְךָ וּלְךָ נָאֶה לְהוֹדוֹת:
>
> *Modim anachnu lach, sha'atah hu Adonai Eloheinu vElohei avoteinu le'olam va'ed. Tzur chayeinu, magen yishenu, atah hu ledor vador. Nodeh lecha unesaper tehilatecha, al chayeinu hamesurim beyadecha, ve'al nishmoteinu hapekudot lach, ve'al nisecha shebechol yom imanu, ve'al nifle'otecha vetovotecha shebechol et, erev vavoker vetzohorayim. Hatov ki lo chalu rachamecha, vehamerachem ki lo tamu chasadecha, me'olam kivinu lach. Ve'al kulam yitbarach veyitromam shimcha malkenu tamid le'olam va'ed. Vechol hachayim yoducha selah, vihalelu et shimcha be'emet, ha'El yeshuatenu ve'ezratenu selah. Baruch atah Adonai, hatov shimcha ulecha na'eh lehodot.*
>
> We thank You, for You are the Lord, our G-d and G-d of our fathers, forever and ever. Rock of our lives, Shield of our salvation, You are He from generation to generation. We thank You and declare Your praise for our lives, which are entrusted to Your hand, for our souls, which are in Your keeping, for Your miracles that are with us every day, and for Your wonders and favors at all times: evening, morning and noon. The Good One, for Your mercy has not ceased; the Merciful One, for Your kindnesses have not ended; we have always placed our hope in You. And for all these may Your Name be blessed and exalted, our King, continually, forever and ever. And all the living will thank You, selah, and praise Your Name sincerely, G-d, our salvation and our help, selah. Blessed are You, Lord, whose Name is the Good One, and to whom it is fitting to give thanks.

*6. Sim Shalom: “Grant peace”*

> שִׂים שָׁלוֹם טוֹבָה וּבְרָכָה חֵן וָחֶסֶד וְרַחֲמִים עָלֵינוּ וְעַל כָּל־יִשְׂרָאֵל עַמֶּךָ. בָּרְכֵנוּ אָבִינוּ כֻּלָּנוּ כְּאֶחָד בְּאוֹר פָּנֶיךָ. כִּי בְאוֹר פָּנֶיךָ נָתַתָּ לָּנוּ יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ תּוֹרַת חַיִּים וְאַהֲבַת חֶסֶד וּצְדָקָה וּבְרָכָה וְרַחֲמִים וְחַיִּים וְשָׁלוֹם. וְטוֹב בְּעֵינֶיךָ לְבָרֵךְ אֶת עַמְּךָ יִשְׂרָאֵל בְּכָל־עֵת וּבְכָל־שָׁעָה בִּשְׁלוֹמֶךָ: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַמְבָרֵךְ אֶת־עַמּוֹ יִשְׂרָאֵל בַּשָּׁלוֹם:
>
> *Sim shalom tovah uverachah, chen vachesed verachamim aleinu ve'al kol Yisrael amecha. Barchenu avinu kulanu ke'echad be'or panecha, ki ve'or panecha natata lanu Adonai Eloheinu torat chayim ve'ahavat chesed, utzedakah uverachah verachamim vechayim veshalom. Vetov be'einecha levarech et amcha Yisrael bechol et uvechol sha'ah bishlomecha. Baruch atah Adonai, hamevarech et amo Yisrael bashalom.*
>
> Grant peace, goodness and blessing, grace, kindness and mercy to us and to all Israel, Your people. Bless us, our Father, all of us as one, with the light of Your countenance, for by the light of Your countenance You gave us, Lord, our G-d, the Torah of life and a love of kindness, righteousness, blessing, mercy, life and peace. And may it be good in Your eyes to bless Your people Israel at every time and at every hour with Your peace. Blessed are You, Lord, who blesses His people Israel with peace.

*Conclusion*

> יִהְיוּ לְרָצוֹן אִמְרֵי פִי וְהֶגְיוֹן לִבִּי לְפָנֶיךָ יְיָ צוּרִי וְגוֹאֲלִי:
עֹשֶׂה שָׁלוֹם בִּמְרוֹמָיו הוּא יַעֲשֶׂה שָׁלוֹם עָלֵינוּ וְעַל כָּל־יִשְׂרָאֵל וְאִמְרוּ אָמֵן:
>
> *Yihyu leratzon imrei fi vehegyon libi lefanecha, Adonai tzuri vego'ali.
Oseh shalom bimromav, hu ya'aseh shalom aleinu ve'al kol Yisrael, ve'imru amen.*
>
> May the words of my mouth and the meditations of my heart be pleasing to You, Lord, my Rock and my Redeemer.
He who makes peace in His heights, may He make peace for us and for all Israel, and say: Amen.

#### `shma-al-hamita` The bedtime Shema — קְרִיאַת שְׁמַע עַל הַמִּטָּה

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Evening. Forgiveness, “HaMapil” and the Shema
- **When:** Before going to sleep, already in bed or beside it.
- **How:** First, you may forgive everyone who hurt you during the day. Then comes the Shema (the first line and the first paragraph, see “Shema Yisrael”). Many say the blessing “HaMapil” last, right before falling asleep, and do not speak after it. If you are afraid of falling asleep before finishing, say “HaMapil” without G-d’s Name. The last lines are from the hymn “Adon Olam”.
- **About the prayer:** The Talmud (Berachot 60b) cites the blessing “HaMapil” and teaches that one should read the Shema before sleep: it protects a person at night. Forgiving those who wronged you before going to sleep is a custom based on the Talmud’s words that whoever forgives has his own sins forgiven (Megillah 28a).
- **References:** Berachot 60b; Megillah 28a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 239
- **Before giyur:** The forgiveness, the Shema and the lines of “Adon Olam” may be said even before giyur. As for saying the blessing “HaMapil” with G-d’s Name before giyur, check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

*Forgiveness*

> רִבּוֹנוֹ שֶׁל עוֹלָם הֲרֵינִי מוֹחֵל לְכָל־מִי שֶׁהִכְעִיס וְהִקְנִיט אוֹתִי אוֹ שֶׁחָטָא כְנֶגְדִּי בֵּין בְּגוּפִי בֵּין בְּמָמוֹנִי בֵּין בִּכְבוֹדִי בֵּין בְּכָל־אֲשֶׁר לִי בֵּין בְּאוֹנֶס בֵּין בְּרָצוֹן בֵּין בְּשׁוֹגֵג בֵּין בְּמֵזִיד בֵּין בְּדִבּוּר בֵּין בְּמַעֲשֶׂה בֵּין בְּמַחֲשָׁבָה בֵּין בְּהַרְהוֹר בֵּין בְּגִלְגּוּל זֶה בֵּין בְּגִלְגּוּל אַחֵר לְכָל־בַּר יִשְׂרָאֵל וְלֹא יֵעָנֵשׁ שׁוּם אָדָם בְּסִבָּתִי: יְהִי רָצוֹן מִלְּפָנֶיךָ יְיָ אֱלֹהַי וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתַי שֶׁלֹא אֶחֱטָא עוֹד וּמַה־שֶּׁחָטָאתִי לְפָנֶיךָ מְחוֹק בְּרַחֲמֶיךָ הָרַבִּים אֲבָל לֹא עַל־יְדֵי יִסּוֹרִים וָחֳלָיִים רָעִים: יִהְיוּ לְרָצוֹן אִמְרֵי־פִי וְהֶגְיוֹן לִבִּי לְפָנֶיךָ יְיָ צוּרִי וְגֹאֲלִי:
>
> *Ribono shel olam, hareini mochel lechol mi shehich'is vehiknit oti o shechata kenegdi, bein begufi, bein bemamoni, bein bichvodi, bein bechol asher li, bein be'ones, bein beratzon, bein beshogeg, bein bemezid, bein bedibur, bein bema'aseh, bein bemachshavah, bein behirhur, bein begilgul zeh, bein begilgul acher, lechol bar Yisrael, velo ye'anesh shum adam besibati. Yehi ratzon milfanecha Adonai Elohai vElohei avotai, shelo echeta od, umah shechatati lefanecha mechok berachamecha harabim, aval lo al yedei yisurim vacholayim ra'im. Yihyu leratzon imrei fi vehegyon libi lefanecha, Adonai tzuri vego'ali.*
>
> Master of the world, I hereby forgive anyone who angered or provoked me or sinned against me, whether against my body, my property, my honor or anything that is mine, whether under compulsion or willingly, by mistake or intentionally, in word, in deed, in thought or in fancy, in this life or in another; any member of Israel, and let no one be punished on my account. May it be Your will, Lord, my G-d and G-d of my fathers, that I sin no more, and whatever sins I have committed before You, wipe them away in Your great mercy, but not through suffering and grievous illnesses. May the words of my mouth and the meditations of my heart be pleasing to You, Lord, my Rock and my Redeemer.

*Shema (followed by the first paragraph, “Ve'ahavta”)*

> שְׁמַע יִשְׂרָאֵל יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ יְיָ אֶחָד:
בָּרוּךְ שֵׁם כְּבוֹד מַלְכוּתוֹ לְעוֹלָם וָעֶד:
>
> *Shema Yisrael, Adonai Eloheinu, Adonai echad.
Baruch shem kevod malchuto le'olam va'ed.*
>
> Hear, O Israel: the Lord is our G-d, the Lord is One.
Blessed is the Name of the glory of His kingdom forever and ever.

*HaMapil*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם הַמַּפִּיל חֶבְלֵי שֵׁנָה עַל עֵינָי וּתְנוּמָה עַל עַפְעַפָּי: וִיהִי רָצוֹן מִלְּפָנֶיךָ יְיָ אֱלֹהַי וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתַי שֶׁתַּשְׁכִּיבֵנִי לְשָׁלוֹם וְתַעֲמִידֵנִי לְשָׁלוֹם וְאַל יְבַהֲלוּנִי רַעְיוֹנַי וַחֲלוֹמוֹת רָעִים וְהַרְהוֹרִים רָעִים וּתְהֵא מִטָּתִי שְׁלֵמָה לְפָנֶיךָ וְהָאֵר עֵינַי פֶּן אִישַׁן הַמָּוֶת כִּי אַתָּה הַמֵּאִיר לְאִישׁוֹן בַּת עָיִן: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַמֵּאִיר לָעוֹלָם כֻּלּוֹ בִּכְבוֹדוֹ:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hamapil chevlei shenah al einai utenumah al af'apai. Vihi ratzon milfanecha Adonai Elohai vElohei avotai, shetashkiveni leshalom veta'amideni leshalom, ve'al yevahaluni ra'ayonai vachalomot ra'im vehirhurim ra'im, utehei mitati shelemah lefanecha, vehaer einai pen ishan hamavet, ki atah hame'ir le'ishon bat ayin. Baruch atah Adonai, hame'ir la'olam kulo bichvodo.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who casts the bonds of sleep upon my eyes and slumber upon my eyelids. May it be Your will, Lord, my G-d and G-d of my fathers, to let me lie down in peace and to raise me up in peace. Let my thoughts, bad dreams and bad imaginings not trouble me, and may my bed be perfect before You (that is, my children). Give light to my eyes, lest I sleep the sleep of death, for it is You who give light to the pupil of the eye. Blessed are You, Lord, who illuminates the whole world with His glory.

*From “Adon Olam”*

> בְּיָדוֹ אַפְקִיד רוּחִי, בְּעֵת אִישַׁן וְאָעִירָה:
וְעִם רוּחִי גְּוִיָּתִי, יְיָ לִי וְלֹא אִירָא:
>
> *Beyado afkid ruchi, be'et ishan ve'a'irah.
Ve'im ruchi geviyati, Adonai li velo ira.*
>
> Into His hand I entrust my spirit, when I go to sleep and when I awake.
And with my spirit, my body too. The Lord is with me, and I shall not fear.

#### `netilat-yadayim-bread` Netilat yadayim before bread — נְטִילַת יָדַיִם לִסְעוּדָה

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Washing the hands before a meal with bread
- **When:** Before a meal that includes bread (challah, lavash, a roll: anything over which “HaMotzi” is said).
- **How:** Take off your rings. Fill a cup and pour water over your right hand, then your left: Ashkenazim customarily pour twice on each hand, while Chabad and many Sephardim pour three times. Raise your hands and say the blessing, then dry them. Do not speak until you have said the blessing over the bread.
- **About the prayer:** The sages instituted washing before bread in memory of the purity with which the kohanim ate holy food (Chullin 106a). The blessing is the same as in the morning.
- **References:** Chullin 106a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 158
- **Before giyur:** As in the morning: before giyur many rabbis advise not saying the words “vetzivanu” (“and commanded us”), or saying the blessing without G-d’s Name. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְצִוָּנוּ עַל נְטִילַת יָדָיִם:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kid'shanu b'mitzvotav v'tzivanu al netilat yadayim.*
>
> Blessed are You, L-rd our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His mitzvot and commanded us to wash the hands.

#### `hamotzi` HaMotzi — הַמּוֹצִיא

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over bread
- **When:** Before bread made from wheat, barley, spelt, oats or rye (after washing the hands).
- **How:** Take the bread in both hands, say the blessing and immediately eat a piece. The blessing over bread covers the whole meal except wine and dessert; according to many opinions, separate blessings are said over those.
- **About the prayer:** Bread is the foundation of a meal, so it has its own “main” blessing. After a meal with bread, Birkat HaMazon is said.
- **References:** Berachot 35a, 38a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 167
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, הַמּוֹצִיא לֶחֶם מִן הָאָרֶץ:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hamotzi lechem min ha'aretz.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who brings forth bread from the earth.

#### `mezonot` Mezonot — מְזוֹנוֹת

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over baked goods and grain dishes
- **When:** Before baked goods (cookies, pie, cake, pizza, according to many opinions), pasta, and porridges made from the five grains (wheat, barley, spelt, oats, rye).
- **How:** Say the blessing while holding the food in your right hand, and eat right away. If porridge or pie is eaten as a full meal, the rules change; check with your rabbi.
- **About the prayer:** The five grains are satisfying, so they have a special blessing, and after eating, “Al HaMichyah” is said.
- **References:** Berachot 36b–37b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 168, 208
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, בּוֹרֵא מִינֵי מְזוֹנוֹת:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei minei mezonot.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates various kinds of nourishment.

#### `hagafen` HaGafen — הַגָּפֶן

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over wine and grape juice
- **When:** Before wine or grape juice.
- **How:** The blessing over wine also covers other drinks consumed at the same time.
- **About the prayer:** Wine “cheers G-d and people” (Shoftim 9:13), so it has its own blessing, like bread. Kiddush and havdalah are made over wine.
- **References:** Berachot 35a–35b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 202:1
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, בּוֹרֵא פְּרִי הַגָּפֶן:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei pri hagafen.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates the fruit of the vine.

#### `haetz` HaEtz — הָעֵץ

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over fruit from trees
- **When:** Before fruits and berries that grow on trees and bushes that live for many years: apples, pears, grapes, nuts, dates.
- **How:** If several fruits are in front of you, the custom is to say the blessing first over one of the seven species of the Land of Israel (olives, dates, grapes, figs, pomegranates).
- **About the prayer:** The Talmud teaches: enjoying the world without a blessing is like taking something holy without permission (Berachot 35a).
- **References:** Berachot 35a, 40a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 202
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, בּוֹרֵא פְּרִי הָעֵץ:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei pri ha'etz.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates the fruit of the tree.

#### `haadama` HaAdamah — הָאֲדָמָה

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over vegetables and produce of the ground
- **When:** Before vegetables, melons (watermelon, cantaloupe), bananas, strawberries, potatoes.
- **How:** If you are not sure whether it is “etz” or “adamah”, say “HaAdamah”: this blessing also works for fruit from trees (Berachot 40a).
- **About the prayer:** Vegetables grow anew from the ground every year, so their blessing speaks of the “fruit of the ground”.
- **References:** Berachot 40a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 203–206
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, בּוֹרֵא פְּרִי הָאֲדָמָה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei pri ha'adamah.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates the fruit of the ground.

#### `shehakol` Shehakol — שֶׁהַכֹּל

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. Over everything else
- **When:** Before meat, fish, dairy, eggs, mushrooms, sweets, water and all drinks except wine and grape juice.
- **How:** “Shehakol” is a general blessing: if you do not know which one to say, it works for almost everything (Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 206:2).
- **About the prayer:** Over things that do not grow directly from the ground, we say “by whose word all things came to be”.
- **References:** Berachot 40b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 204
- **Before giyur:** Blessings over food are praise; they do not contain the words “who commanded us”. The sages permit a non-Jew to say blessings, and candidates are usually advised to start saying them now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁהַכֹּל נִהְיָה בִּדְבָרוֹ:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, shehakol nihyah bidvaro.*
>
> Blessed are You, Lord, our G-d, King of the universe, by whose word all things came to be.

#### `birkat-hamazon` Birkat HaMazon (short form) — בִּרְכַּת הַמָּזוֹן

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. The blessing after a meal with bread
- **When:** After a meal that included bread (at least a kezayit, about 27–30 g), in the same place where you ate.
- **How:** The full Birkat HaMazon consists of four blessings; its text is in every siddur and in separate booklets (“benchers”). Here is only the very shortest beginning. “Brich Rachamana” is the minimal form from the Talmud for someone who cannot yet say more. The first blessing, “HaZan”, is best learned in full right away. Agree with your rabbi on how a beginner may shorten it.
- **About the prayer:** A commandment of the Torah: “And you shall eat and be satisfied, and bless the Lord your G-d” (Devarim 8:10). The Talmud (Berachot 40b) tells of Binyamin the shepherd, who after eating bread would say in Aramaic: “Blessed is the Merciful One, Master of this bread,” and thereby fulfilled the commandment. According to tradition, the first blessing was composed by Moshe when Israel received the manna (Berachot 48b).
- **References:** Devarim 8:10; Berachot 40b, 48b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 185, 187:1
- **Before giyur:** Birkat HaMazon is praise and thanksgiving, and candidates are usually advised to learn it. But the second blessing contains words about the covenant and the Torah “which You have given us” — check with your rabbi how to say them before giyur.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria; “Brich Rachamana” — Berachot 40b

*Brich Rachamana: the shortest form (in Aramaic)*

> בְּרִיךְ רַחֲמָנָא מַלְכָּא דְעָלְמָא מָרֵיהּ דְּהַאי פִּתָּא:
>
> *Brich Rachamana, malka de'alma, marei dehai pita.*
>
> Blessed is the Merciful One, King of the world, Master of this bread.

*1. HaZan — “Who Nourishes”*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם הַזָּן אֶת־הָעוֹלָם כֻּלּוֹ בְּטוּבוֹ בְּחֵן בְּחֶסֶד וּבְרַחֲמִים הוּא נוֹתֵן לֶחֶם לְכָל־בָּשָׂר כִּי לְעוֹלָם חַסְדּוֹ וּבְטוּבוֹ הַגָּדוֹל תָּמִיד לֹא־חָסַר לָנוּ וְאַל־יֶחְסַר לָנוּ מָזוֹן לְעוֹלָם וָעֶד בַּעֲבוּר שְׁמוֹ הַגָּדוֹל כִּי הוּא אֵל זָן וּמְפַרְנֵס לַכֹּל וּמֵטִיב לַכֹּל וּמֵכִין מָזוֹן לְכָל־בְּרִיּוֹתָיו אֲשֶׁר בָּרָא: בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ הַזָּן אֶת־הַכֹּל:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, hazan et ha'olam kulo betuvo, bechen bechesed uverachamim. Hu noten lechem lechol basar, ki le'olam chasdo.
