Uvetuvo hagadol tamid lo chasar lanu, ve'al yechsar lanu mazon le'olam va'ed, ba'avur shemo hagadol. Ki hu El zan umefarnes lakol, umetiv lakol, umechin mazon lechol beriyotav asher bara. Baruch atah Adonai, hazan et hakol.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who nourishes the whole world in His goodness, with grace, with kindness and with compassion. He gives bread to all flesh, for His kindness is everlasting. And through His great goodness we have never lacked food, and may we never lack it, forever and ever — for the sake of His great Name. For He is G-d who nourishes and sustains all, does good to all, and prepares food for all His creatures whom He has created. Blessed are You, Hashem, who nourishes all.

#### `al-hamichya` Al HaMichyah — עַל הַמִּחְיָה

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. After baked goods, wine and fruit of the seven species
- **When:** After eating at least a kezayit (about 27–30 g) of baked goods or porridge made from the five grains, drinking at least a revi'it (about 86 ml) of wine, or eating fruit of the seven species: grapes, figs, pomegranates, olives, dates.
- **How:** This is a “threefold blessing in one” (Me'ein Shalosh): a single blessing that briefly repeats the first three blessings of Birkat HaMazon. The text here is for baked goods; for wine and fruit the beginning and the end change (see the last part). On Shabbat, before “ki Atah”, you add “uRetzeh vehachalitzenu beyom haShabbat hazeh”.
- **About the prayer:** These foods are connected with the praise of the Land of Israel (Devarim 8:8), so after them we give thanks both for the land and for Jerusalem (Berachot 44a).
- **References:** Devarim 8:8; Berachot 44a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 208
- **Before giyur:** This is thanksgiving, without the words “who commanded us”. Candidates are usually advised to say it already now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

*After baked goods and porridge*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, עַל הַמִּחְיָה וְעַל הַכַּלְכָּלָה, וְעַל תְּנוּבַת הַשָּׂדֶה, וְעַל אֶרֶץ חֶמְדָּה טוֹבָה וּרְחָבָה שֶׁרָצִיתָ וְהִנְחַלְתָּ לַאֲבוֹתֵינוּ לֶאֱכֹל מִפִּרְיָהּ וְלִשְׂבֹּעַ מִטּוּבָהּ. רַחֵם נָא יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ עַל יִשְׂרָאֵל עַמֶּךָ, וְעַל יְרוּשָׁלַיִם עִירֶךָ, וְעַל צִיּוֹן מִשְׁכַּן כְּבוֹדֶךָ, וְעַל מִזְבְּחֶךָ וְעַל הֵיכָלֶךָ. וּבְנֵה יְרוּשָׁלַיִם עִיר הַקֹּדֶשׁ בִּמְהֵרָה בְיָמֵינוּ, וְהַעֲלֵנוּ לְתוֹכָהּ, וְשַׂמְּחֵנוּ בְּבִנְיָנָהּ, וְנֹאכַל מִפִּרְיָהּ וְנִשְׂבַּע מִטּוּבָהּ, וּנְבָרֶכְךָ עָלֶיהָ בִּקְדֻשָּׁה וּבְטָהֳרָה. כִּי אַתָּה יְיָ טוֹב וּמֵטִיב לַכֹּל, וְנוֹדֶה לְּךָ עַל הָאָרֶץ וְעַל הַמִּחְיָה. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ, עַל הָאָרֶץ וְעַל הַמִּחְיָה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, al hamichyah ve'al hakalkalah, ve'al tenuvat hasadeh, ve'al eretz chemdah tovah urechavah sheratzita vehinchalta la'avoteinu le'echol mipiryah velisboa mituvah. Rachem na Adonai Eloheinu al Yisrael amecha, ve'al Yerushalayim irecha, ve'al Tziyon mishkan kevodecha, ve'al mizbechecha ve'al heichalecha. Uvneh Yerushalayim ir hakodesh bimherah veyameinu, veha'alenu letochah, vesamchenu bevinyanah, venochal mipiryah venisba mituvah, unevarechecha aleha bikdushah uvtaharah. Ki atah Adonai tov umetiv lakol, venodeh lecha al ha'aretz ve'al hamichyah. Baruch atah Adonai, al ha'aretz ve'al hamichyah.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, for the sustenance and the nourishment, for the produce of the field, and for the desirable, good and spacious land which You were pleased to give as a heritage to our fathers, to eat of its fruit and to be satisfied with its goodness. Have mercy, Hashem our G-d, on Israel Your people, on Jerusalem Your city, on Zion the dwelling place of Your glory, on Your altar and on Your Temple. Rebuild Jerusalem, the holy city, speedily in our days, bring us up into it, gladden us with its rebuilding, and we will eat of its fruit, be satisfied with its goodness and bless You for it in holiness and purity. For You, Hashem, are good and do good to all, and we thank You for the land and for the sustenance. Blessed are You, Hashem, for the land and for the sustenance.

*For wine and fruit*

> עַל הַגֶּפֶן וְעַל פְּרִי הַגֶּפֶן … עַל הָאָרֶץ וְעַל פְּרִי הַגָּפֶן:
עַל הָעֵץ וְעַל פְּרִי הָעֵץ … עַל הָאָרֶץ וְעַל הַפֵּרוֹת:
>
> *After wine: al hagefen ve'al pri hagefen… al ha'aretz ve'al pri hagafen.
After fruit: al ha'etz ve'al pri ha'etz… al ha'aretz ve'al haperot.*
>
> After wine: “for the vine and the fruit of the vine… for the land and for the fruit of the vine.”
After fruit of the seven species: “for the tree and the fruit of the tree… for the land and for the fruits.” In the Land of Israel, for its own wine and fruit one says “pri gafnah” and “peroteha”.

#### `bore-nefashot` Borei Nefashot — בּוֹרֵא נְפָשׁוֹת

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Food. After everything else
- **When:** After food and drink over which you said “HaEtz” (except the seven species), “HaAdamah” or “Shehakol” beforehand: at least a kezayit of food or a revi'it of drink.
- **How:** A single short sentence. If you ate both baked goods and, for example, an apple, you say both “Al HaMichyah” and “Borei Nefashot”.
- **About the prayer:** Thanks not only for the food, but also for the fact that G-d created living beings together with their needs — both what is needed for life and what simply brings joy.
- **References:** Berachot 37a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 207
- **Before giyur:** This is thanksgiving, without the words “who commanded us”. Candidates are usually advised to say it already now. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, בּוֹרֵא נְפָשׁוֹת רַבּוֹת וְחֶסְרוֹנָן, עַל כָּל מַה שֶּׁבָּרָאתָ לְהַחֲיוֹת בָּהֶם נֶפֶשׁ כָּל חָי. בָּרוּךְ חֵי הָעוֹלָמִים:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei nefashot rabot vechesronan, al kol mah shebarata lehachayot bahem nefesh kol chai. Baruch chei ha'olamim.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates many living beings and all that they lack — for all that You have created to sustain the life of every living thing. Blessed is He who lives forever.

#### `shabbat-candles` Candle lighting — הַדְלָקַת נֵרוֹת

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Shabbat. Shabbat and Yom Tov candles
- **When:** On Friday before sunset: usually 18 minutes before, in Jerusalem 40 minutes before (the time for your city is in the “Calendar”). Candles may not be lit after sunset.
- **How:** According to Ashkenazi custom, you first light the candles, then cover your eyes with your hands, say the blessing and open your eyes: with the blessing, the woman accepts Shabbat. Many Sephardim say the blessing before lighting. After the blessing it is customary to pray in your own words — for your family, your children, for whatever is on your heart.
- **About the prayer:** Shabbat candles are a practice established by the Sages for the sake of “peace in the home”, so that the Shabbat evening is bright and joyful (Shabbat 25b). Lighting is first of all the duty of the woman of the house, but if there is none, a man lights.
- **References:** Shabbat 23b, 25b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 263
- **Before giyur:** A non-Jew before giyur is not obligated to keep Shabbat, and the Sages held that one should not keep it fully before giyur (Sanhedrin 58b). That is why many rabbis advise candidates to light candles without the blessing or with special qualifications. This is for your rabbi to decide — be sure to check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** The text of the blessing is the same in all siddurim

*On Shabbat*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְצִוָּנוּ לְהַדְלִיק נֵר שֶׁל שַׁבָּת:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kideshanu bemitzvotav vetzivanu lehadlik ner shel Shabbat.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His commandments and commanded us to light the Shabbat lamp.

*When Yom Tov falls on Shabbat*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְצִוָּנוּ לְהַדְלִיק נֵר שֶׁל שַׁבָּת וְשֶׁל יוֹם טוֹב:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kideshanu bemitzvotav vetzivanu lehadlik ner shel Shabbat veshel Yom Tov.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His commandments and commanded us to light the lamp of Shabbat and of Yom Tov.

*On Yom Tov (followed by “Shehecheyanu”, except on the last days of Pesach)*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְצִוָּנוּ לְהַדְלִיק נֵר שֶׁל יוֹם טוֹב:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kideshanu bemitzvotav vetzivanu lehadlik ner shel Yom Tov.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His commandments and commanded us to light the Yom Tov lamp.

#### `kiddush` Friday night kiddush — קִדּוּשׁ לֵיל שַׁבָּת

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Shabbat. Sanctifying Shabbat over wine
- **When:** On Friday evening, after prayers, before the meal.
- **How:** Fill a cup with wine or grape juice (at least a revi'it, about 86 ml). Hold it in your right hand, usually standing (many sit). First say “Vayehi erev vayehi voker” quietly, then continue aloud. Everyone listening answers “amen” and may take a sip. Then comes hand washing and “HaMotzi”.
- **About the prayer:** “Remember the Shabbat day to keep it holy” (Shemot 20:8): the Sages teach that it is sanctified with words, and they established that these words are said over wine (Pesachim 106a). The first passage is Bereshit 2:1–3, the account of the seventh day of creation.
- **References:** Shemot 20:8; Bereshit 2:1–3; Pesachim 106a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 271
- **Before giyur:** It is not customary to say the words “You have chosen us… among all the nations” and “who commanded us” before giyur, and a candidate does not make kiddush on behalf of others. Usually a candidate listens to kiddush and answers “amen”. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Metsudah Siddur (1981), Hebrew, CC-BY, via Sefaria

*Vaychulu (Bereshit 2:1–3)*

> וַיְהִי עֶרֶב וַיְהִי בֹקֶר יוֹם הַשִּׁשִּׁי: וַיְכֻלּוּ הַשָּׁמַיִם וְהָאָרֶץ וְכָל־צְבָאָם: וַיְכַל אֱלֹהִים בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה. וַיִּשְׁבֹּת בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי מִכָּל־מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר עָשָׂה: וַיְבָרֶךְ אֱלֹהִים אֶת־יוֹם הַשְּׁבִיעִי וַיְקַדֵּשׁ אֹתוֹ. כִּי בוֹ שָׁבַת מִכָּל מְלַאכְתּוֹ אֲשֶׁר־בָּרָא אֱלֹהִים לַעֲשׂוֹת:
>
> *(Quietly:) Vayehi erev vayehi voker — (aloud:) Yom hashishi. Vaychulu hashamayim veha'aretz vechol tzeva'am. Vaychal Elohim bayom hashevi'i melachto asher asah, vayishbot bayom hashevi'i mikol melachto asher asah. Vayevarech Elohim et yom hashevi'i vaykadesh oto, ki vo shavat mikol melachto asher bara Elohim la'asot.*
>
> And there was evening and there was morning — the sixth day. The heavens and the earth and all their host were completed. By the seventh day G-d completed His work which He had done, and He rested on the seventh day from all His work which He had done. G-d blessed the seventh day and sanctified it, for on it He rested from all His work which G-d had created to make.

*Over the wine*

> סַבְרִי מָרָנָן וְרַבּוֹתַי:
בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם בּוֹרֵא פְּרִי הַגָּפֶן:
>
> *Savri maranan verabotai!
Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, borei pri hagafen.*
>
> With the permission of my masters and teachers!
Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who creates the fruit of the vine.

*Sanctification of the day*

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, אֲשֶׁר קִדְּשָׁנוּ בְּמִצְוֹתָיו וְרָצָה בָנוּ, וְשַׁבַּת קָדְשׁוֹ בְּאַהֲבָה וּבְרָצוֹן הִנְחִילָנוּ, זִכָּרוֹן לְמַעֲשֵׂה בְרֵאשִׁית. (כִּי הוּא יוֹם) תְּחִלָּה לְמִקְרָאֵי קֹדֶשׁ, זֵכֶר לִיצִיאַת מִצְרָיִם. (כִּי בָנוּ בָחַרְתָּ וְאוֹתָנוּ קִדַּשְׁתָּ מִכָּל הָעַמִּים) וְשַׁבַּת קָדְשְׁךָ בְּאַהֲבָה וּבְרָצוֹן הִנְחַלְתָּנוּ. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ, מְקַדֵּשׁ הַשַּׁבָּת:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, asher kideshanu bemitzvotav veratzah vanu, veShabbat kodsho be'ahavah uvratzon hinchilanu, zikaron lema'aseh vereshit. Ki hu yom techilah lemikra'ei kodesh, zecher litziat Mitzrayim. Ki vanu vacharta ve'otanu kidashta mikol ha'amim, veShabbat kodshecha be'ahavah uvratzon hinchaltanu. Baruch atah Adonai, mekadesh haShabbat.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who has sanctified us with His commandments and has taken pleasure in us, and with love and favor has given us His holy Shabbat as a heritage — a remembrance of the work of creation. For it is the first of the holy days of assembly, a remembrance of the Exodus from Egypt. For You have chosen us and sanctified us among all the nations, and with love and favor You have given us Your holy Shabbat as a heritage. Blessed are You, Hashem, who sanctifies Shabbat.

#### `tefilat-haderech` Tefilat HaDerech — תְּפִלַּת הַדֶּרֶךְ

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Special occasions. The traveler's prayer
- **When:** When you set out on a journey out of town (by car, train or plane), once you have passed the last houses. Once per day of travel.
- **How:** It is best said standing, but in a car or on a plane you may sit; a driver does not stop for it. If you will return the same day, add “vetachzirenu leshalom” (“and return us in peace”).
- **About the prayer:** The prayer is cited in the Talmud (Berachot 29b–30a). It is said in the plural: the traveler prays for everyone who is on the road.
- **References:** Berachot 29b–30a; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 110:4–7
- **Before giyur:** This is a request, without the words “who commanded us”. Many rabbis allow a candidate to say it. Check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** Siddur Da'at (daat.ac.il), Hebrew, public domain, via Sefaria

> יְהִי רָצוֹן מִלְפָנֶיךָ יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ וֵאלֹהֵי אֲבוֹתֵינוּ, שֶׁתּוֹלִיכֵנוּ לְשָׁלוֹם וְתַצְעִידֵנוּ לְשָׁלוֹם וְתַדְרִיכֵנוּ לְשָׁלוֹם, וְתִסְמְכֵנוּ לְשָׁלוֹם, וְתַגִּיעֵנוּ לִמְחוֹז חֶפְצֵנוּ לְחַיִּים וּלְשִׂמְחָה וּלְשָׁלוֹם. (וְתַחְזִירֵנוּ לְשָׁלוֹם) וְתַצִּילֵנוּ מִכַּף כָּל אוֹיֵב וְאוֹרֵב וְלִסְטִים וְחַיּוֹת רָעוֹת בַּדֶּרֶךְ, וּמִכָּל מִינֵי פֻּרְעָנֻיּוֹת הַמִּתְרַגְּשׁוֹת לָבוֹא לָעוֹלָם, וְתִתְּנֵנוּ לְחֵן וּלְחֶסֶד וּלְרַחֲמִים בְּעֵינֶיךָ וּבְעֵינֵי כָל רֹאֵינוּ, כִּי אֵל שׁוֹמֵעַ תְּפִלָּה וְתַחֲנוּן אַתָּה. בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ שׁוֹמֵעַ תְּפִלָּה:
>
> *Yehi ratzon milfanecha, Adonai Eloheinu veElohei avoteinu, shetolichenu leshalom, vetatz'idenu leshalom, vetadrichenu leshalom, vetismechenu leshalom, vetagi'enu limchoz cheftzenu lechayim ulesimchah uleshalom (vetachzirenu leshalom), vetatzilenu mikaf kol oyev ve'orev velistim vechayot ra'ot baderech, umikol minei pur'aniyot hamitragshot lavo la'olam, vetitnenu lechen ulechesed ulerachamim be'eynecha uve'einei chol ro'einu, ki El shome'a tefilah vetachanun atah. Baruch atah Adonai, shome'a tefilah.*
>
> May it be Your will, Hashem, our G-d and G-d of our fathers, to lead us in peace, to direct our steps in peace, to guide us in peace, to support us in peace, and to bring us to our desired destination — for life, for joy and for peace (and to return us in peace). Rescue us from every enemy and ambush, from bandits and wild beasts along the way, and from all kinds of calamities that rage and come upon the world. Grant us grace, kindness and compassion in Your eyes and in the eyes of all who see us, for You are G-d who hears prayer and supplication. Blessed are You, Hashem, who hears prayer.

#### `shehecheyanu` Shehecheyanu — שֶׁהֶחֱיָנוּ

- [ ] Checked. Groups: Special occasions. Thanksgiving for something new and joyful
- **When:** When you eat a new fruit for the first time in its season; put on important new clothing; buy a significant item that brings you joy; on the first night of a festival (at candle lighting or at kiddush); before seasonal mitzvot (shofar, lulav, the Chanukah candles on the first night).
- **How:** Say the blessing at the moment of joy, before you start eating or using the new item.
- **About the prayer:** A blessing from the Talmud (Berachot 54a, 59b; Eruvin 40b) — thanks to G-d for letting us live to reach this moment.
- **References:** Berachot 54a, 59b; Eruvin 40b; Shulchan Aruch, Orach Chaim 223–225
- **Before giyur:** This is thanksgiving, without the words “who commanded us”, and many rabbis allow a candidate to say it. Whether to say “Shehecheyanu” after giyur, at the immersion or afterwards — check with your rabbi.
- **Source:** The text of the blessing is the same in all siddurim

> בָּרוּךְ אַתָּה יְיָ אֱלֹהֵינוּ מֶלֶךְ הָעוֹלָם, שֶׁהֶחֱיָנוּ וְקִיְּמָנוּ וְהִגִּיעָנוּ לַזְּמַן הַזֶּה:
>
> *Baruch atah Adonai, Eloheinu melech ha'olam, shehecheyanu vekiymanu vehigi'anu lazman hazeh.*
>
> Blessed are You, Hashem, our G-d, King of the universe, who has granted us life, sustained us and enabled us to reach this time.

<!-- PRAYERS-END -->

## Equal standing of Reform content (November 2026)

Added under the owner's rule of 23 November 2026: the Reform path must be set out as fully and as carefully as the Orthodox path, without a "residual" tone. Please review the new and expanded texts.

### Lessons and the question bank

- [ ] Lesson `beit-din-7`, "Reform giyur: study, community, and the beit din": a description of informed choice; the CCAR decision of 1892 (milah and tevilah are not formally obligatory) and the statement that today's North American practice is often more observant of the rites; the ROOPI rules according to the description of the "Shaarei Shalom" school; the practice of IDPI; the 2021 decision of the Supreme Court of Israel on the civil recognition of non-Orthodox giyur performed within the country; the boundary between the Law of Return and recognition by the Chief Rabbinate; the 1983 CCAR resolution on the Jewish status of a child of one Jewish parent who is raised as a Jew.
- [ ] Full official names and scope of authority: ROOPI, WUPJ, IDPI / IMPJ, CCAR, URJ — are the abbreviations mixed up, and are the formulations out of date?
- [ ] Lessons `shabbat-4`, `kashrut-5`, and `semya-5` are open at the Reform level as well: is it correct that the classical system (the 39 melachot, kashering utensils, niddah) is useful to know in all movements, and that Reform practice is set out separately and without the tone "it is not obligatory, so it does not matter"?
- [ ] Reform Shabbat: travel to the service and electric light are generally permitted; the sanctity of the day is built through rest, prayer, family, and refraining from weekday work and shopping; the Friday service is often the main one of the week; candles are sometimes lit after sunset, at the beginning of the service or the meal.
- [ ] Reform kitchen: two sets of dishes are not obligatory; knowledge of the classical categories and an honest measure of practice are expected; ethical kashrut (animals, labor, ecology) is presented as an additional layer, and not as a replacement for the Torah's laws of permitted food.
- [ ] The mikveh in Progressive communities: the laws of niddah are not obligatory; the mikveh is being revived as a spiritual source of transition (giyur, a wedding, personal milestones). Is there excessive generalization across countries here?
- [ ] Siddurim: Mishkan T'filah (2007) replaced Gates of Prayer (1975); the machzor Mishkan HaNefesh; inclusion of the matriarchs in the Amidah; the variant "mechayeh ha-kol" alongside "mechayeh metim"; prayer in a language one understands as a principle, and not as a concession; instrumental music and the equal participation of women. In ROOPI the contents of the siddur may be different — and the text says so; please confirm the typical practice of Russian-speaking communities.
- [ ] New questions in the bank (including `beit-din-q58`–`q61`, `beit-din-n64`–`n71`, `beit-din-bd6`–`bd8`, questions about Mishkan T'filah, informed choice, Tisha B'Av, and the length of the festivals in the Diaspora): factual accuracy and the absence of a disparaging tone.

### Interview trainer

- [ ] Eight new questions: `mot-community-choice`, `mitz-informed`, `pray-community`, `faith-covenant`, `know-patrilineal`, `shab-spirit`, `kosh-measure`, `mitz-tikkun` — are they typical of a Reform beit din (and, where indicated, of a general one), and are the hints and the list of mistakes correct?
- [ ] Expanded notes "how it is in the different movements" on the earlier questions (Torah, melachot, candles, the kitchen, fasts, the mikveh, milah, acceptance of the mitzvot, family purity, and others): are there oversimplifications, and are the norms of ROOPI, Israel, and North America mixed together?

### Calendar and prayers

- [ ] Updated Reform notes in `content/en/calendar/days.json` (Shabbat, the Days of Awe, fasts, Pesach, the Omer, Yom HaShoah, Yom HaZikaron, Yom HaAtzmaut, Yom Yerushalayim, the Three Weeks, Tisha B'Av): is the phrase "in most communities the minor fasts are not kept" too broad a generalization, and is the return of Tisha B'Av as a day of remembrance and reflection accurate?
- [ ] The note "Siddurim of the Reform movement" in `content/en/prayers.json` (`reform-siddur`): the names of the books, the principle of a language one understands, and the statement that the texts of the app are given according to the Ashkenaz nusach.

### Sources

- [ ] The added links to https://www.ccarnet.org/, https://www.ccarpress.org/, https://www.urj.org/ are the official home pages, without invented responsa numbers. If a rabbi indicates more precise Russian or canonical editions, they should be substituted into `sources`.

### Movement descriptions on the "Choose a movement" screen (new text, needs review)

The old descriptions stacked the levels like a ladder: Reform as "the base shared by every movement", Masorti as "everything in the base level, plus", Orthodox as "the full level". Now each movement is described on its own terms, with equal weight:

- [ ] Reform: "The Torah is a living covenant that each generation studies and keeps with intention: the ethics of the prophets, Shabbat, the holidays, and prayer. Giyur means study with a rabbi, taking on the destiny of the people of Israel, and a beit din of Reform rabbis; mikveh and brit follow the community's rules."
- [ ] Conservative (Masorti): "Halacha is binding and develops through the rulings of the Masorti movement's rabbis: Shabbat, kashrut, daily prayer. Giyur means study, a beit din of three rabbis, mikveh, and brit milah for men."
- [ ] Orthodox: "The Torah given at Sinai and halacha according to the Shulchan Aruch in full detail. Giyur means accepting the mitzvot before an Orthodox beit din, brit milah for men, then mikveh."
- [ ] Intro: "Three movements, three complete paths to giyur, each with its own principles and its own beit din." (instead of "The levels nest…"). The "Level 1/2/3" label above the cards was removed.
- [ ] Interview question on Reform giyur: "differ from nineteenth-century North American practice" instead of "…minimum".

## The map of Eretz Israel (the "Land" screen)

The screen and sidebar item "The Land". In version 0.6.0 the map was redone to be calmer and closer to reality (at the author's request: "more realistic… perhaps we went a little too far"). It now has two main views and an extra layer:

- **"Israel today"** (the default view) shows the country the way official Israeli maps draw it. United Jerusalem and the Golan are within Israel. Judea and Samaria are labelled by their name, "Judea and Samaria". The 1949 armistice line is a thin dashed line, the way Israel's Foreign Ministry maps mark it. The dashed line is not drawn inside Jerusalem's municipal boundary, because the city is one. Since Israel's withdrawal in 2005, Aza has been outside Israel's borders; on this view it is shaded in a neutral colour, like the neighbouring countries. The neighbouring countries are neutral grey land without labels, so the coastline and the gulfs read as on a real map.
- **"Torah Borders"** is unchanged: the bounds of the Land in the Torah. On this layer Aza is shaded together with the Land (Yehoshua 15:47), and the 1949 dashed line is not shown.
- **"Tribal Allotments"** is unchanged.

The place names are Jewish (Jerusalem, Hevron, Shechem, Beit El, Shilo, Jericho, the Golan, Aza, and others). Political labels that Israeli maps do not use never appear on the map; the test `no_forbidden_political_language` checks this. The geography is an educational diagram, not a cadastral plan.

### The "Israel today" map: sources

- Survey of Israel (the national mapping agency), its Judea and Samaria unit: https://www.gov.il/en/departments/units/kadaster-deprtment. Official maps name the area "Judea and Samaria" (יהודה ושומרון).
- The government map portal GovMap: https://www.govmap.gov.il.
- Israel's Foreign Ministry booklet "Quest for Peace" (the "Facts about Israel" series, 2018): https://www.gov.il/BlobFolder/generalpage/facts-about-israel-2018/en/English_ABOUT_ISRAEL_PDF_Quest-for-Peace.pdf. Its maps mark the line as "Armistice Line, 1949–1967".
- December 2006 reports on school maps (Chicago Tribune, Washington Post, Arutz Sheva). After 1967 the armistice line is usually left off official Israeli maps. The director of the Survey of Israel explained that it appears only on maps designated as historical. The Prime Minister's Office said showing the 1949 line is acceptable, while the government's position rejects a return to the 1967 lines.
- Jerusalem: Basic Law: Jerusalem, Capital of Israel (1980): "Jerusalem, complete and united, is the capital of Israel." The Golan: the Golan Heights Law (1981). Aza: the disengagement plan, carried out in August–September 2005.
- [ ] Please confirm that a thin 1949 dashed line with a gap inside Jerusalem is a fitting way to show it. The other option is to drop the dashed line altogether, as most current Survey of Israel maps do.

### Torah Borders — sources and identifications

### Torah Borders — sources and identifications

- [ ] The layer caption cites Bereshit 15:18, Bamidbar 34, and Devarim 11:24. Are the formulations and the order of the verses right for an educational display?
- [ ] The "river of Egypt" in Bereshit 15:18 (נְהַר מִצְרַיִם) is identified on the map with Nachal Mitzrayim — the Brook of Egypt, Wadi el-Arish, and not with the Nile. Many commentators read it this way, but there is also the view that it is the Nile. Please confirm whether this identification is acceptable in the app, or whether both views should be stated in the caption.
- [ ] The inner outline is the bounds of the land of Canaan according to Bamidbar 34 (south to Nachal Mitzrayim, west the Great Sea, north to the entrance of Hamat, east the Yarden and Yam ha-Melach). Hor HaHar, the "entrance of Hamat", and Hatzar-Enan are marked schematically; Damascus remains outside this outline and inside the wide outline of the covenant. Is this right for an educational layer?
- [ ] The wide outline is the covenant with Avraham (Bereshit 15:18) and Devarim 11:24: from the wilderness and Lebanon to the Euphrates and the western sea. It includes Lebanon, Damascus, Gilad, Bashan, and Moav. Does such a diagram create a false impression of modern state borders?
- [ ] The tribal allotments (Yehoshua 13–19) are given as a schematic optional layer, not as an exact cadastre. Levi has no separate allotment — and the caption says so. The two and a half tribes are shown to the east of the Yarden (Reuven, Gad, eastern Menashe). Does anything need to be corrected in the names, or in how "Dan's first allotment" on the coast is given?

### Place cards

- [ ] Jerusalem (new text): "The capital of the State of Israel and the eternal capital of the Jewish people. Under the Basic Law of 1980, 'Jerusalem, complete and united, is the capital of Israel.' The First and Second Temples stood here, and here is the Western Wall, the Kotel, a remnant of the Temple Mount enclosure." The line above the card: "The capital of Israel, the eternal capital of the people". Linked to the lesson `prazdniki-6`.
- [ ] Hevron: the Cave of Machpelah, the patriarchs and the matriarchs, David's reign of seven years. Lesson `osnovy-5`.
- [ ] Shechem (not "Nablus"): Avraham's altar, Yosef's bones, Gerizim and Eival. Lesson `osnovy-5`.
- [ ] Beit El: Yaakov's dream. Shilo: the site of the Mishkan before the Temple. Jericho: the first city on entering the Land under Yehoshua. Beit Lechem: Rachel's Tomb, the line of David. The lessons, where indicated, are `osnovy-5`.
- [ ] Aza (new text): "An ancient city of the southern coast. In the Torah and the Prophets, Aza lies within the Land: the allotment of Yehudah names 'Aza, its towns and villages, as far as the Brook of Egypt' (Yehoshua 15:47). Since Israel's withdrawal in 2005, Aza has been outside Israel's borders."
- [ ] Katzrin and the Golan (new text): the connection with Bashan and the allotment of eastern Menashe; "Israeli law has applied on the Golan since 1981, and Israeli maps show the Golan as part of the country."
- [ ] Tzfat (Lecha Dodi, the Arizal, lesson `shabbat-2`), Tveria (the Sanhedrin, the Jerusalem Gemara, the tomb of the Rambam, lesson `osnovy-2`), Yafo, Akko (the Rambam's remark about the border of the Land for certain mitzvot — please confirm the wording), Haifa and Carmel (Eliyahu), Be'er Sheva (the Well of the Oath), Eilat / Etzion-Gever, Hermon.
- [ ] Region labels on the map itself: "Judea and Samaria" (as on official maps), the Golan, Galilee, the Negev; Aza is labeled as a city of the southern coast. On the Torah layer: the Euphrates, Nachal Mitzrayim, Lebanon, Damascus, Gilad, Bashan, Moav, Yam ha-Gadol. Jewish names, without political labels.

### Introduction to the screen

- [ ] The introductory paragraph (new text): "Eretz Israel is the Land the Torah calls the heritage of the Jewish people. The Israel Today view shows the country as official Israeli maps draw it; the Torah Borders layer shows the bounds of the Land in the Torah; the Tribal Allotments layer shows how the Land was divided under Yehoshua."
- [ ] The caption under "Israel today": "Israel today, as on official Israeli maps: united Jerusalem and the Golan within Israel; Judea and Samaria labelled by their name, with the 1949 armistice line as a thin dashed line, the way Israel's Foreign Ministry maps mark it; Aza outside Israel's borders since 2005. The Torah Borders layer shows the bounds of the Land in the Torah: from the river of Egypt to the Euphrates (Bereshit 15:18; Bamidbar 34; Devarim 11:24)."

## Zionism and immunity to false teachings (November 2026)

Added under the owner's rules of 23 November 2026: the app is consistently pro-Zionist; from lesson to lesson a person clearly sees what Judaism is, as distinct from teachings that only look Jewish. The texts of the app do not contain a condemnatory doctrinal label, and they do not name another religion. The safeguard is woven into ordinary study, without a separate "denunciatory" section. The source materials for authors are in `docs/COUNTER-MISSIONARY-RESEARCH.md`.

### The pro-Zionist line

- [ ] Lesson `osnovy-10`, "The Land of Israel, Zion, and the State of Israel": the eternal covenant (Bereshit 12:7, 15:18, 17:8); Jerusalem as the eternal and undivided capital; Yehuda, Samaria, the Golan, and Aza named as part of the Land of the Torah; the Ramban (positive commandment 4) and the Rambam, Laws of Kings 5:9–12; Yom HaAtzmaut as a day of thanksgiving; the State does not cancel the awaiting of Mashiach. Is what is said about the Torah borders too brief?
- [ ] History in `osnovy-5` and the festivals in `prazdniki-6`: the presentation "Haredim do not attach religious significance to the day" as an equal option has been removed. In its place: customs of prayer may differ, but love of the Land and gratitude for Israel are part of the Jewish path. Is this distinction between the custom of the liturgy and the meaning of the days correct?
- [ ] Calendar: `yom-haatzmaut`, `yom-yerushalayim`, `yom-hazikaron`, `yom-hashoah`, `tisha-beav`.
- [ ] Trainer: the question `fam-israel` has been strengthened; a new question, `hol-atzmaut`.

### Reading the Tanakh and contrasts of teaching

- [ ] Lesson `osnovy-11` and the additions to `osnovy-9`. Verses: Yeshayahu 7:14 (*almah*, a sign for Achaz); Yeshayahu 53 (the servant is Israel, Rashi); Tehillim 22:17 (*ka-ari*); Tehillim 110:1 (*adoni*, not Adonai; Rashi — Avraham); Daniel 9:24–27 (Rashi — the period of the Temples); Yirmiyahu 31:30–32 (the same Torah upon the heart; the numbering is the Hebrew numbering); "Elohim" and the verb *bara*; Michah 5:1 (in other editions often 5:2 — a ruler from Beit Lechem as a man of the line of David); Zecharyah 12:10 (mourning for the fallen / for a human deliverer, not for a "pierced G-d").
- [ ] Devarim 13:1–4 and the Rambam, Foundations of the Torah 9:1–3: even a sign that has come to pass does not annul the Torah.
- [ ] Oral Torah (`osnovy-2`): the path "the written text alone, without the Sages" — not Judaism.
- [ ] The Days of Awe (`prazdniki-2`): teshuvah, tefillah, tzedakah; Hoshea 14:3; Yechezkel 18:20; yetzer ha-tov and yetzer ha-ra.
- [ ] The Seder (`prazdniki-4`): the meaning is about the Exodus, not "secret signs" (Mishnah Pesachim 10:5).
- [ ] Shabbat (`shabbat-1`): specifically the seventh day, Shemot 31:16–17.
- [ ] Kashrut (`kashrut-1`): the Torah's food laws are not annulled.
- [ ] Prayer (`ivrit-3`, `ivrit-4`, the note in `prayers.json`): to the Almighty alone, facing Jerusalem.
- [ ] Community and the ger's faithfulness (`ivrit-6`, `beit-din-3`, `beit-din-6`). The formulation about a Jew by birth who has gone over to another teaching, and about the ger — check it against the halacha of all three movements.
- [ ] The 1985 CCAR resolution as the shared agreement of the recognized movements.

### Question bank and trainer

- [ ] New questions in the bank (more than forty), including `osnovy-10-q*`, `osnovy-11-q*`, `ivrit-z*`, `shabbat-z*`, `kashrut-z*`, `prazdniki-z*`, `etika-z*`, `semya-z1`, `beit-din-z*`. Especially open-ended: `osnovy-10-q7`, `osnovy-10-q9`, `osnovy-11-q10`, `osnovy-11-q12`, `beit-din-z6`.
- [ ] Correction of `prazdniki-q51` and `prazdniki-bd6`.
- [ ] New trainer questions: `faith-oneness-now`, `faith-tanakh-read`, `hol-atzmaut`, `fam-shul-choice`, `know-torah-eternal`.
- [ ] Replacement of the condemnatory term with "false teaching" / "not Judaism" / *minim*.
